Minimal depth K K -types for wild double covers and Shimura correspondences
Edmund Karasiewicz, Shuichiro Takeda

Abstract
. We construct some Iwahori types, in the sense of Bushnell-Kutzko, for the double cover of an almost simple simply-laced simply-connected Chevalley group G ~ \widetilde{G} over any 2 2 -adic field. These types capture the covering group analog of the Bernstein block of unramified principal series. We also prove that the associated Hecke algebra essentially admits an Iwahori-Matsumoto (IM) presentation. The complete presentation is obtained for types A r A_{r} , D 2 ​ r + 1 D_{2r+1} , E 6 E_{6} , E 7 E_{7} ; for the other types, some technical obstacles remain. Those Hecke algebras with the complete IM presentation are isomorphic to Iwahori-Hecke algebras of explicit linear Chevalley groups, giving rise to Shimura correspondences. Along the way, we show that the Iwahori type extends to a hyperspecial maximal compact subgroup K ~ ⊆ G ~ \widetilde{K}\subseteq\widetilde{G} . This extension has minimal depth among the genuine K ~ \widetilde{K} -representations and allows us to construct a finite Shimura correspondence, generalizing a result of Savin.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
11F70, 22E50

1.
Introduction

Our objective in this work is to address some fundamental problems in the representation theory of wild double covers of
2
-adic groups. Our main results include:

(1)

basic structure theory of the hyperspecial maximal compact subgroups of the wild cover;

(2)

a construction of genuine irreducible Iwahori types that capture the covering group analog of the Bernstein block of unramified principal series;

(3)

an Iwahori-Matsumoto presentation for the Hecke algebra associated to the Iwahori types, which implies a Shimura correspondence with an explicit linear group;

(4)

an extension of the Iwahori type to a hyperspecial maximal compact subgroup, which induces a “finite Shimura correspondence”.

Now we describe the results more precisely. In what follows, for a group
H
, we write
\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{1}_{H}
for the trivial representation of
H
.

Let
G
be a reductive group over a non-archimedean local field
F
of characteristic 0. Let
T\subseteq G
be a maximal split torus, and
I\subseteq G
an Iwahori subgroup intersecting
T
nontrivially. The Iwahori-Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}(G,I)=C_{0}^{\infty}(I\backslash G/\penalty I)
plays an important role in the representation theory of
G
. In particular, the Borel-Casselman theorem
[
Bor76
, Theorem 4.10]
implies that the category of
\mathcal{H}(G,I)
-modules is equivalent to the
(T,\mathbf{1})
Bernstein block of unramified principal series representations of
G
, and no other Bernstein block admits Iwahori fixed vectors. In other words, the pair
(I,\mathbf{1})
is a type of
G
in the sense of Bushnell-Kutzko
[
BK98
, (4.1) Definition]
and this type characterizes the
(T,\mathbf{1})
-block. Furthermore, the Iwahori Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}(G,I)
has a very explicit set of generators and relations
[
IM65
]
, which is known as the Iwahori-Matsumoto presentation.

Assume now that
G
is a simply-connected Chevalley group over
F
. By the work of Steinberg, Moore, and Matsumoto (see
[
GGW18
]
for the history), when
F
contains
\mu_{n}
, the full group of
n
-th roots of unity,
G
has an (essentially) unique nontrivial topological central extension
\widetilde{G}
, fitting into an exact sequence

(1.1)

1\longrightarrow\mu_{n}\longrightarrow\widetilde{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{pr}}}{{\longrightarrow}}G\longrightarrow 1.

For any
H\subseteq G
we write
\widetilde{H}=\operatorname{pr}^{-1}(H)
. A representation of
\widetilde{H}
is called
\epsilon
-genuine if
\mu_{n}
acts through a faithful character
\epsilon:\mu_{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. We fix this character throughout and use it to identify
\mu_{n}
with the
n
-th roots of unity in
\mathbb{C}
.

The maximal torus
\widetilde{T}\subseteq\widetilde{G}
admits a distinguished genuine representation
\rho
. (See
[
Sav04
]
and
[
GG18
, Section 6, Theorems 6.6, 6.8]
.) We note that such a
\rho
is not unique, but they are finite in number. The
(\widetilde{T},\rho)
-block of
\widetilde{G}
is a natural analog of the
(T,\mathbf{1})
-block of
G
. Thus we may ask: is there an analog of the Borel-Casselman theorem for the
(\widetilde{T},\rho)
-block of
\widetilde{G}
? The pair
(\widetilde{I},\mathbf{1})
does not serve the purpose because
\mathbf{1}_{\widetilde{I}}
is not genuine, and hence can only detect representations of
\widetilde{G}
that factor through
G
.

When
\mathrm{GCD}(p,n)=1
, the cover
\widetilde{G}
is called a tame cover. In this case, Savin
[
Sav04
]
established this analog of the Borel-Casselman Theorem when
G
is simply-laced. The main consequence of the tame assumption for this work is that a hyperspecial maximal compact subgroup
K\subseteq G
splits the sequence (
1.1
). To highlight the significance of this splitting we make a brief digression.

Here we are not making any assumption on
\mathrm{GCD}(n,p)
. Suppose the subgroup
H\subset G
splits into
\widetilde{G}
, and let
\mathbf{s}:H\rightarrow\widetilde{G}
be a splitting. Then the map
\mu_{n}\times H\rightarrow\widetilde{H}
defined by
(\zeta,h)\mapsto\zeta\mathbf{s}(h)
defines a group isomorphism. Thus, there is a transparent correspondence (depending on the splitting) between the representations of
H
and the
\epsilon
-genuine representations of
\widetilde{H}
. Specifically, if
\pi
is a representation of
H
, then
\epsilon\otimes\pi
is an
\epsilon
-genuine representation of
\widetilde{H}\cong\mu_{n}\times H
.

Now we return to the tame case. Let
\mathbf{s}:K\rightarrow\widetilde{G}
be the unique splitting of
K
. Hence
I\subseteq K
splits to
\widetilde{G}
by restricting the splitting
\mathbf{s}
. A natural candidate for the Iwahori type describing the
(\widetilde{T},\rho)
-block is the pair
(\mu_{n}\times\mathbf{s}(I),\epsilon\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{s}(I)})
; the associated Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}_{\epsilon}(\widetilde{G},\mathbf{s}(I))
consists of compactly supported smooth functions on
\widetilde{G}
that are
\mathbf{s}(I)
-biinvariant and
\epsilon
-genuine. Using this pair
(\mu_{n}\times\mathbf{s}(I),\epsilon\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{s}(I)})
, Savin proves that the block of
(\widetilde{T},\rho)
is equivalent to
\mathcal{H}_{\epsilon}(\widetilde{G},\mathbf{s}(I))
-mod giving an analog of the Borel-Casselman theorem. Savin also shows that
\mathcal{H}_{\epsilon}(\widetilde{G},\mathbf{s}(I))
admits a Bernstein presentation. This allows Savin to identify
\mathcal{H}_{\epsilon}(\widetilde{G},\mathbf{s}(I))
with the Iwahori Hecke algebra of an explicit linear group
G^{\prime}
giving a correspondence between certain representations of
\widetilde{G}
and
G^{\prime}
, called a Shimura correspondence.

When
\mathrm{GCD}(n,p)\neq 1
, we say
\widetilde{G}
is wild. In this case, the Iwahori subgroup
I
does not split. So, neither does
K
. Thus there is no obvious candidate for a type to describe the
(\widetilde{T},\rho)
-block. A few special cases have been worked out. Loke-Savin
[
LS10b
]
resolve the case of the
2
-fold cover of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}
over
\mathbb{Q}_{2}
. This was generalized by Wood
[
Woo14
]
to the
2
-fold cover of
\operatorname{Sp}_{2r}
also over
\mathbb{Q}_{2}
. Takeda-Wood
[
TW18
]
further extend the work of Wood to include any
2
-adic field. Karasiewicz
[
Kar21
]
resolves the case of
2
-fold covers of almost simple simply-laced simply-connected Chevalley groups over
\mathbb{Q}_{2}
. We note that
[
Woo14
,
LS10b
,
Kar21
]
all construct a type on a proper subgroup of the Iwahori. In all of these cases the construction of the type is somewhat ad hoc. In particular, we note that a naive generalization of these types to the situation where the
2
-adic field
F/\mathbb{Q}_{2}
has ramification results in the incorrect representation. The work of Takeda-Wood
[
TW18
]
constructs a type on the Iwahori. Their construction is conceptually satisfying as it uses the
K
-types of the Weil representation. Unfortunately, the special nature of the Weil representation makes the possibility of generalization unclear.

In this paper, our main theorem is an analog of the Borel-Casselman theorem for the nontrivial
2
-fold cover of an almost simple simply-laced simply-connected Chevalley group over an
arbitrary

2
-adic field
F
. This generalizes the work of Karasiewicz
[
Kar21
]
to an arbitrary
2
-adic field. The main novelty of our work is how we address the technical issues arising from the ramification of
F/\mathbb{Q}_{2}
. We will say more about this after stating our results.

To state our main theorem, we must first describe some of our other results on the structure and representation theory of the compact groups
\widetilde{T}_{0}:=\widetilde{T}\cap\widetilde{K}
and
\widetilde{K}
. These results are interesting in their own right. From now on, unless otherwise stated, we assume that
F
is a
2
-adic field and
G
is the
F
-points of an almost simple simply-connected simply-laced Chevalley group of rank at least
2
, and
\widetilde{G}
is the unique nontrivial topological
2
-fold cover of
G
. In particular,
\widetilde{G}
is a wild cover.

We begin with
\widetilde{T}_{0}
. Here our main result is an explicit construction of a family of Weyl invariant genuine irreducible
\widetilde{T}_{0}
-representations (Theorem
4.14
). These
\widetilde{T}_{0}
-representations are called pseudo-spherical representations. They are not unique, but are finite in number, and our construction produces all of them. We also prove that these representations are types for the
(\widetilde{T},\rho)
-block in the category of smooth
\widetilde{T}
-representations. Our explicit construction is arithmetic in nature and requires special properties of the
2
-adic quadratic Hilbert symbol.

Now we turn to the structure theory of
\widetilde{K}
. For any smooth irreducible genuine
\widetilde{K}
-representation
\pi
, the subgroup
\operatorname{pr}(\ker\pi)
must split into
\widetilde{K}
and the image of this splitting (which is just
\ker\pi
) must be normal in
\widetilde{K}
. Our main theorem on the structure of
\widetilde{K}
identifies the maximal principal congruence subgroup of
K
that splits into
\widetilde{K}
with normal image.

Theorem 1.2

(Propositions
5.2
,
5.3
)
.

Let
e
be the ramification index of
F/\mathbb{Q}_{2}
. Let
\Gamma(k)\subset K
be the
k
-th principal congruence subgroup of
K
. Then

(1)

There exists a splitting
\mathbf{s}_{\Gamma(2e)}:\Gamma(2e)\rightarrow\widetilde{G}
such that its image
\mathbf{s}_{\Gamma(2e)}(\Gamma(2e))
is normal in
\widetilde{K}
;

(2)

if
\Gamma(2e-1)
splits into
\widetilde{K}
, then the image of this splitting is not normal in
\widetilde{K}
, nor
\widetilde{I}
.

This result is also arithmetic in nature and requires special properties of the
2
-adic quadratic Hilbert symbol. We make a few more comments on this theorem.

The notion of depth introduced by Moy-Prasad
[
MP94
,
MP96
]
has been generalized to tame covers by Howard-Weissman
[
HW09
]
. The main point is that in a tame topological central extension any pro-
p
subgroup must split uniquely
[
HW09
, Proposition 2.3]
. The situation is more complicated for wild covers. As the previous theorem demonstrates, a genuine irreducible representation of
\widetilde{K}
cannot have a large kernel; among the principal congruence subgroups,
\Gamma(2e)
is maximal such that its image under a splitting can be contained in the kernel of a genuine
\widetilde{K}
-representation. We will not define a notion of depth for
\widetilde{K}
or
\widetilde{G}
, but we will call the genuine irreducible
\widetilde{K}
-representations that are trivial on the image of a splitting of
\Gamma(2e)
the minimal depth representations of
\widetilde{K}
.

With some handle on the structure of
\widetilde{K}
we can turn to representation theory. Ideally we would like to establish some correspondence between representations of
\widetilde{K}
and
K
, as we had in the tame case. Since both
I
and
K
do not split into
\widetilde{G}
there is no obvious correspondence between, say,
K
-representations and genuine
\widetilde{K}
-representations. However, we are able to construct a correspondence between the depth
0
representations of
K
and certain representations of
\widetilde{K}
(Theorem
5.35
). This generalizes a result of Savin
[
Sav12
]
for the unramified
F/\mathbb{Q}_{2}
, where the correspondence is called a finite Shimura correspondence. This correspondence is constructed using a very special representation of
\widetilde{K}
. Now that we describe the construction of this representation, we will return to the correspondence.

Let
\tau
be a pseudo-spherical representation of
\widetilde{T}_{0}
. In fact,
\tau
remains irreducible when restricted to
\widetilde{T}_{1}=\widetilde{T}\cap\widetilde{\Gamma}(1)
. Let
\sigma=\sigma_{\tau}
be the
\widetilde{\Gamma}(1)
-representation constructed using the parahoric induction functor of Dat
[
Dat09
, Section 2.6]
. From general properties of parahoric induction the representation
\sigma
is an irreducible genuine
\widetilde{\Gamma}(1)
-representation that is trivial on
\mathbf{s}_{\Gamma(2e)}(\Gamma(2e))
. A more in-depth study of
\sigma
reveals that this representation actually extends to
\widetilde{K}
.

Theorem 1.3

(Theorem
5.25
)
.

The irreducible genuine
\widetilde{\Gamma}(1)
-representation
\sigma
constructed by parahoric induction from a pseudo-spherical representation of
\widetilde{T}_{1}
extends uniquely to a representation of
\widetilde{K}
. In particular, the extension of
\sigma
is a minimal depth representation of
\widetilde{K}
.

We also write
\sigma
for the extension of
\sigma
to
\widetilde{K}
. The minimal depth
\widetilde{K}
-representation
\sigma
actually gives us substantial control over an interesting family of genuine representations of
\widetilde{K}
via a finite Shimura correspondence with the depth
0
representations of
K
. To state this precisely we introduce some additional notation.

Let
\kappa
be the residue field of
F
, and
G_{\kappa}
the
\kappa
-points of
G
. For a totally disconnected topological group
H
let
\mathcal{M}(H)
be the category of smooth representations of
H
. Let
\mathcal{M}_{(\widetilde{\Gamma}(1),\sigma)}(\widetilde{K})
be the full subcategory of
\mathcal{M}(\widetilde{K})
consisting of smooth representations of
\widetilde{K}
such that their restriction to
\widetilde{\Gamma}(1)
is
\sigma
-isotypic. Note that any representation of
G_{\kappa}
inflates to a non-genuine representation of
\widetilde{K}
via the isomorphism
G_{\kappa}\cong K/\Gamma(1)
induced by the reduction mod
p
map. Now we can state the finite Shimura correspondence.

Theorem 1.4

(Theorem
5.35
)
.

The functor
\mathcal{M}(G_{\kappa})\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{(\widetilde{\Gamma}(1),\sigma)}(\widetilde{K})
defined by
\pi\mapsto\pi\otimes\sigma
defines an equivalence of categories.

As mentioned earlier, this theorem generalizes Savin
[
Sav12
, Theorem 4.1]
, which assumes
F
is unramified over
\mathbb{Q}_{2}
.

The finite Shimura correspondence provides a construction of a large family of minimal depth representations of
\widetilde{K}
and may be valuable for the construction of minimal depth supercuspidal representations of
\widetilde{G}
. It also behaves well with respect to induction and thus can be used to reduce the study of certain Hecke algebras on
\widetilde{K}
to related Hecke algebras on
K
(Theorem
6.7
).

Now we can state our main theorem on the analog of the Borel-Casselman Theorem for
\widetilde{G}
.

Theorem 1.5

(Theorem
7.8
)
.

Let
\rho
be a distinguished genuine
\widetilde{T}
-representation. Let
\tau
be a pseudo-spherical
\widetilde{T}_{0}
-representation that is a type for
\rho
. Let
\sigma=\sigma_{\tau}
be the
\widetilde{K}
-representation from Theorem
1.3
. We also write
\sigma
for its restriction to
\widetilde{I}\subset\widetilde{K}
. (Recall that
\sigma
is irreducible when restricted to
\widetilde{\Gamma}(1)\subset\widetilde{I}
.)

The pair
(\widetilde{I},\sigma)
is a type in the sense of Bushnell-Kutzuko
[
BK98
, (4.1) Definition]
. Furthermore, the category of
\mathcal{H}(G,\widetilde{I};\sigma)
-modules is equivalent to the
(\widetilde{T},\rho)
Bernstein block of
\widetilde{G}
, and no other Bernstein block contains representations with
(\widetilde{I},\sigma)
-isotypic vectors.

As in the linear case, we expect the Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}(\widetilde{G},\widetilde{I};\sigma)
to admit an Iwahori-Matsumoto (IM) presentation
[
IM65
]
, which essentially describes the algebra as a deformation of the group algebra of an extended affine Weyl group. We mostly prove this, but fall short of the complete IM presentation for certain Cartan types where technical obstructions appear. We briefly indicate the extent of our results here, and refer the reader to the body of the paper for the precise statements of our results.

First we note that in all Cartan types we can determine a weak IM presentation for
\mathcal{H}(\widetilde{G},\widetilde{I};\sigma)
. This includes identifying an unnormalized basis for
\mathcal{H}(\widetilde{G},\widetilde{I};\sigma)
consisting of invertible elements, identifying a set of generators for the algebra, braid relations and quadratic relations up to nonzero scalar for these generators, and a complete IM presentation for elements supported in
\widetilde{K}
(so in particular the basis elements supported in
\widetilde{K}
can be normalized properly).

Now we will describe our results for the specific Cartan types. We can obtain the complete IM presentation when
G
has Cartan type
A_{r},D_{2r+1},E_{6},E_{7}
(Theorem
6.34
). In type
D_{2r}
we obtain an IM presentation only up to a
2
-cocycle on the length
0
elements. In type
E_{8}
we will not be able to obtain the proper normalization of the one generator corresponding to the simple affine reflection.

We note that when
F/\mathbb{Q}_{2}
is unramified the techniques of Karasiewicz
[
Kar21
]
can be adapted to the present setting to derive the complete IM presentation for all Cartan types. We have opted not to pursue this because we believe that the technical obstructions in types
D_{2r}
and
E_{8}
for any
2
-adic field
F
will naturally be resolved as we refine and expand the techniques used in this paper. For example, the difficulty with
E_{8}
stems from the fact that its affine Dynkin diagram only has a single special vertex, while all of the other types we consider have at least two. We expect this to be resolved as follows. It seems plausible that one can extend the finite Shimura correspondence to other points in the Bruhat-Tits building of
G
. We expect such a finite Shimura correspondence to encode an isomorphism of Hecke algebras, as we see in this paper for the finite Shimura correspondence at a special vertex (Theorem
6.7
). This isomorphism of Hecke algebras when applied to a non-special vertex in type
E_{8}
should determine the proper normalization for the last generator completing the IM presentation in type
E_{8}
.

One of the consequences of the IM presentation is that there is an explicit linear Chevalley group
G^{\prime}
and Iwahori subgroup
I^{\prime}\subseteq G^{\prime}
such that
\mathcal{H}(G^{\prime},I^{\prime})\cong\mathcal{H}(\widetilde{G},\widetilde{I};\sigma)
. This Hecke algebra isomorphism gives a Shimura correspondence of representations.

Theorem 1.6

(Corollary
7.9
)
.

For
G
of Cartan type
A_{r},D_{2r+1},E_{6},E_{7}
, there is an equivalence of categories between the
(I^{\prime},\mathbf{1})
-block of
G^{\prime}
and the
(\widetilde{I},\sigma)
-block of
\widetilde{G}
.

We emphasize that this result will also hold for the other Cartan types, once the the remaining gaps in the IM presentation are filled.

Another consequence of the IM presentation is that the subalgebra
\mathcal{H}(\widetilde{G},\widetilde{K},\sigma)\subset\mathcal{H}(\widetilde{G},\widetilde{I},\sigma)
satisfies a Satake isomorphism. Specifically,
\mathcal{H}(\widetilde{G},\widetilde{K},\sigma)
is isomorphic to the center of
\mathcal{H}(\widetilde{G},\widetilde{I},\sigma)
, which is well known as a consequence of the IM presentation.

Next we highlight several of the ideas and tools that we develop to overcome the technical issues raised by the ramification of
F/\mathbb{Q}_{2}
. The first technical issue is that the structure of the
2
-fold cover
\widetilde{G}
over
F
is closely connected with the quadratic Hilbert symbol of
F
. When
F
has odd residual characteristic the quadratic Hilbert symbol is well understood and even obeys a relatively simple formula. (e.g. see
[
Neu99
, (3.4) Proposition]
.) When
F
has even residual characteristic the quadratic Hilbert symbol is more complicated and its properties seem to be less well-known; the complexity is directly connected to the ramification index. So, to get a handle on the structure of
\widetilde{G}
and its subgroups we must first establish some general properties of the quadratic Hilbert symbol for an arbitrary
2
-adic field. The most important result we prove is an explicit description of the radical of the Hilbert symbol restricted to
\mathcal{O}^{\times}
. We expect that this and the other results we prove about the Hilbert symbol are already known, but we have not been able to locate them in the literature, so we record the proofs in Appendix
A
.

These results on the Hilbert symbol are used repeatedly throughout our paper and are part of the foundation of all of our results. To highlight just two, the construction of the pseudo-spherical representations of
\widetilde{T}_{0}
and the structure theory of
\widetilde{K}
depend crucially on the the results we prove about the Hilbert symbol.

With a pseudo-spherical
\widetilde{T}_{0}
-representation
\tau
that is a type of the
\widetilde{T}
-representation
\rho
, we can begin to try to construct a type for for the Bernstein block of
\widetilde{G}
attached to
(\widetilde{T},\rho)
. Here we want to highlight two points.

The first point is about Bushnell-Kutzko’s notion of
G
-covers, which essentially suggests a candidate type for
G
given a type
\tau
for
\widetilde{T}
. We encountered some difficulty when we applied this theory directly to
\tau
. The theory suggests a candidate type
(\widetilde{I}_{2e},\sigma_{2e})
, where
\widetilde{I}_{2e}\subset\widetilde{I}
is an open compact subgroup and
\sigma_{2e}
an irreducible representation of
\widetilde{I}_{2e}
. The trouble is that
\widetilde{I}_{2e}
is quite small compared to
\widetilde{I}
, even when
e=1
. This makes questions about the support of the associated Hecke algebra difficult to investigate. This seems somewhat similar to the situation in Roche
[
Roc98
]
. In this work Roche investigates types for ramified principal series, so in particular is working in arbitrary depth, using Bushnell-Kutkzo’s theory of
G
-covers. Roche’s candidate types are on small open compact subgroups. He exerts considerable effort to determine the support of the Hecke algebras and must impose constraints on the residual characteristic, which includes
p=2
in all Cartan types (through he mentions a way to remove these restrictions in type
A_{r}
). (See
[
Roc98
, pg. 370-379]
.)

In our situation we are seeing high-depth phenomenon from the ramification of
F/\mathbb{Q}_{2}
, but since we are exclusively working with
2
-adic fields we must deal with residual characteristic
2
. It is not clear if Roche’s techniques adapt to our setting, so we took a different path. To minimize the support difficulties we set out to construct an Iwahori type. This is the approach in Takeda-Wood
[
TW18
]
, but there the Iwahori type is constructed using the Weil representation. In our situation we construct a candidate Iwahori type
(\widetilde{I},\sigma)
using a pseudo-spherical
\widetilde{T}_{0}
-representation
\tau
and the parahoric induction of Dat
[
Dat09
]
. Now we might try to prove that
\sigma
is a type using the theory of
G
-covers. Unfortunately the
\widetilde{I}
-representation
\sigma
does not fit into this theory. However, we are able to adapt the theory to our setting. This seems to suggest that the theory of
G
-covers can be extended by incorporating parahoric induction. (See Lemmas
7.3
,
7.6
.)

The second point is that the statement of our results in terms of Iwahori types of
\widetilde{G}
is misleading, from the standpoint of our arguments. Our arguments crucially use two different models for the Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}(\widetilde{G},\widetilde{I};\sigma)
; the other involving a smaller open compact subgroup
\widetilde{I}_{2}\subset\widetilde{I}
and an irreducible representation
\sigma_{2}
, also constructed from
\tau
via parahoric induction. In fact,
\sigma\cong\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde{I}_{2}}^{\widetilde{I}}\sigma_{2}
(Proposition
5.32
) and so
\mathcal{H}(\widetilde{G},\widetilde{I};\sigma)\cong\mathcal{H}(\widetilde{G},\widetilde{I}_{2};\sigma_{2})
, from general theory. Roughly, the advantage of the
\widetilde{I}
-model appears when bounding the support of the Hecke algebra, this allows us to avoid the tedious support calculations in Karasiewicz
[
Kar21
]
; the advantage of the
\widetilde{I}_{2}
-model is that it is more compatible with the underlying affine Weyl group structure of
\widetilde{G}
than
\widetilde{I}
is. We will briefly elaborate on this affine Weyl group comment.

Let
T\subset G
be a maximal split torus with cocharacter lattice
Y
. Let
\Phi
be the root system of
(G,T)
. Let
\mathfrak{A}\subset\mathbb{R}\otimes Y
be an alcove containing
0
in its closure. The stabilizer of
\mathfrak{A}
in
G
is an Iwahori subgroup, say
I
. Now we turn to
\widetilde{G}
. The root datum for the Langlands dual group of
\widetilde{G}
suggests that the relevant root system for
\widetilde{G}
is
\frac{1}{2}\Phi
. Thus in place of the alcove
\mathfrak{A}
we should consider
2\mathfrak{A}
, and the stabilizer of
2\mathfrak{A}
in
G
is the group we have been calling
I_{2}
.

To close this introduction we will summarize the content of each section. In §
2
we set up notation and recall some well known results. We note that §
2.1
includes the fundamental properties of the Hilbert symbol that we use repeatedly. We expect these results are known, but we have not been able to locate them, and so we include the proofs in Appendix
A
.

In §
3
we recall the presentation of
\widetilde{G}
and prove some basic results on the modified cocharacter lattice
\widetilde{Y}\subset Y
and the action of the Weyl group on it.

§
4
-
7
are the core of our work. In §
4
we construct the pseudo-spherical representations of
\widetilde{T}_{0}
, the maximal compact subgroup of
\widetilde{T}
. While
\widetilde{T}_{0}
is a
2
-step nilpotent group with a Stone-von Neumann Theorem, for use in the rest of the paper we need a fairly explicit construction of these representations. To achieve this we describe a factorization of
\widetilde{T}_{0}
, heavily using the properties of the Hilbert symbol from §
2.1
. We note that we construct all of the pseudo-spherical representations of
\widetilde{T}_{0}
, show that they are Weyl group invariant (Theorem
4.14
), and describe how they branch when restricted to certain subtori of rank one less (Proposition
4.17
).

In §
5
we investigate the structure and representation theory of a hyperspecial maximal compact subgroup
\widetilde{K}\subset\widetilde{G}
and other open compact subgroups. In §
5.1
we examine the structure of
\widetilde{K}
. Our main theorem identifies the maximal principal congruence subgroup of
K
that splits into
\widetilde{G}
with normal image in
\widetilde{K}
(Propositions
5.2
,
5.3
). In §
5.2
we review the parahoric induction and restriction functors of Dat
[
Dat09
]
and their extension by Crisp-Meir-Onn
[
CMO19
]
, and records basic properties of these functors, some from
[
Dat09
,
CMO19
]
and some new (e.g. Proposition
5.15
). In §
5.3
we construct a candidate Iwahori type
\sigma
and several related representations on finite index subgroups of
\widetilde{I}
. The main result in this subsection is Theorem
5.24
, where we show how these representations relate to one another and establish Weyl-invariance, where applicable.

In §
5.4
we prove that the
\widetilde{I}
-representation
\sigma
extends to
\widetilde{K}
(Theorem
5.25
). This is a crucial result. We use it later to construct the finite Shimura correspondence (Theorem
5.35
), which itself essentially determines the structure of the subalgebra of
\mathcal{H}(\widetilde{G},\widetilde{I},\sigma)
supported on
\widetilde{K}
.

In §
5.5
we introduce
I_{2}
and
\sigma_{2}
, mentioned above, and collect various properties of
\sigma
and
\sigma_{2}
. These properties play a role in our investigation of the Hecke algebras in §
6
.

In §
5.7
we prove the finite Shimura correspondence in Theorem
5.35
. This result is interesting in its own right, but it also figures into our study of
\mathcal{H}(\widetilde{G},\widetilde{I},\sigma)
. We return to describe other important properties of this correspondence in §
6.2
, after introducing the Hecke algebra.

In §
5.8
we collect a couple simple corollaries on Whittaker invariants of the representations of
\widetilde{K}
that arise in the finite Shimura correspondence. These results are not used elsewhere in the paper, but they should be important for the study of the Gelfand-Graev representation of
\widetilde{G}
.

In §
6
we begin our study of the Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}(\widetilde{G},\widetilde{I},\sigma)
. After reviewing basic properties of Hecke algebras in §
6.1
, we return in §
6.2
to the finite Shimura correspondence. We prove that this correspondence preserves induction in a certain sense and use this to connect the finite Shimura correspondence to Hecke algebras. Consequently, we show that a family of Hecke algebras on
\widetilde{K}
are isomorphic to Hecke algebras studied by Howlett-Lehrer
[
HL80
]
.

In §
6.3
we prove that the Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}
constructed from
\sigma
and the Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}_{2}=\mathcal{H}(\widetilde{G},\widetilde{I}_{2},\sigma_{2})
constructed from
\sigma_{2}
are in fact isomorphic (Theorem
6.10
). This proves to be quite important since each model offers complementary advantages.

Over the next six subsections we study the structure of
\mathcal{H}\cong\mathcal{H}_{2}
. In §
6.4
we prove in Theorem
6.11
that every Iwahori double coset supports a space of functions of dimension at most one in
\mathcal{H}
. For this theorem the
\mathcal{H}
-model is quite convenient.

We prove in §
6.6
and
6.7
, using the
\mathcal{H}_{2}
-model, that the Hecke algebra supports functions on double cosets represented by certain elements in the extended affine Weyl group. Specifically the simple reflections (Proposition
6.17
) and the length
0
elements (Proposition
6.21
).

In §
6.8
we prove the quadratic relations in the Hecke algebra. For the simple reflections represented by elements of
\widetilde{K}
we can prove the exact quadratic relation (Proposition
6.22
) using the finite Shimura correspondence, and so in particular, this uses the
\mathcal{H}
-model. For the simple affine reflection we can use the
\mathcal{H}_{2}
-model to prove a weak quadratic relation (Proposition
6.25
). Outside of type
E_{8}
we use an automorphism to upgrade this weak quadratic relation to the expected quadratic relation. We also use the
\mathcal{H}_{2}
-model to prove that the length
0
elements satisfy a quadratic relation.

Next we turn to the Braid relations in §
6.9
. Using an auxiliary linear Hecke algebra (introduced in §
6.5
) we can prove that
\mathcal{H}_{2}
has a Braid relation up to a
2
-cocycle (Proposition
6.27
). The rest of this section is devoted to showing when this
2
-cocycle is trivial. We prove that the
2
-cocycle is trivial in types
A_{r},D_{2r+1},E_{6},E_{7}
, and it is expected to be trivial in general.

Finally in §
6.10
we combine our results from the previous subsections to show that
\mathcal{H}\cong\mathcal{H}_{2}
has an IM presentation (Theorem
6.34
). This is complete in types
A_{r},D_{2r+1},E_{6},E_{7}
, but it is expected for all Caratn types. In type
D_{2r}
the last step is to prove that the
2
-cocycle on the length
0
elements is trivial; in type
E_{8}
the last step is to prove that the simple affine reflection satisfies the expected quadratic relation. We end §
6.10
with a few comments on
D_{2r}
, and
E_{8}
.

In §
7
we prove our main Theorem
7.8
that the pair
(\overline{I},\sigma)
is a type in the sense of Bushnell-Kutzko
[
BK98
, (4.1) Definition]
, and this type captures the Bernstein block associated to
(\widetilde{T},\rho)
. This result has no restrictions on the (irreducible) Cartan type. Given our previous results on the structure of the Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}
this argument essentially follows Bushnell-Kutzko
[
BK98
]
. However, as mentioned above, the pair
(\widetilde{I},\sigma)
does not fit into Bushnell-Kutzko’s theory of
G
-covers. So, in this section we also show how the notion of
G
-cover extends to our setting.

This section ends with Corollary
7.9
. This states that for the Cartan types for which we know that
\mathcal{H}
admits a complete IM presentation there is a linear group
G^{\prime}
with an Iwahori subgroup
I^{\prime}\subset G^{\prime}
such that
\mathcal{H}\cong\mathcal{H}(G^{\prime},I^{\prime})
. This isomorphism of Hecke algebras implies that the
(\widetilde{T},\rho)
-Bernstein block of
\widetilde{G}
is equivalent to the block of unramified principal series of
G^{\prime}
, giving a Shimura correspondence of representations.

We end with Appendix
A
. It contains proofs of the important properties of the Hilbert symbol that we have been unable to locate in the literature.

Notation and assumptions

Throughout the paper, we let
F
be a finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{2}
, with ring of integers
\mathcal{O}
and a chosen uniformizer
\varpi
, and let
\kappa=\mathcal{O}/\penalty\varpi\mathcal{O}
be the residue field. Let
e,f\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
be the ramification index and the inertia degree of
F/\penalty\mathbb{Q}_{2}
, respectively, so that

[F:\mathbb{Q}_{2}]=ef,\quad 2\mathcal{O}=\varpi^{e}\mathcal{O}{\quad\text{and}\quad}\kappa=\mathcal{O}/\penalty\varpi\mathcal{O}\cong\mathbb{F}_{q},

where
q=2^{f}
.

Let
G
be any group and
H\subseteq G
a subgroup. Let
x\in G
. We set

{}^{x}H=xHx^{-1}.

For a representation
\pi
of
H
, we define

{}^{x}\pi

to be the representation of
{{}^{x}H}
defined by

{}^{x}\pi(h)=\pi(x^{-1}hx),\quad(h\in{{}^{x}H}).

Note that
x^{-1}hx\in H
because
h\in{{}^{x}H}=xHx^{-1}
, and hence this is well-defined. More generally, for another group
H^{\prime}
and a group isomorphism
\phi:H\to H^{\prime}
, we define

{}^{\phi}\pi

to be the representation of
H^{\prime}
defined by

(1.7)

^{\phi}\pi(h^{\prime})=\pi(\phi^{-1}(h^{\prime})),\quad(h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}).

We let

\operatorname{Int}(x):H\longrightarrow xHx^{-1},\quad h\mapsto xhx^{-1}.

Then
{}^{x}\pi={{}^{\operatorname{Int}(x)}}\pi
. We denote by
\pi^{H}
the space of
H
-invariant vectors in
\pi
.

[
Dat09
]
and Crisp-Meir-Onn
[
CMO19
]
. We also thank Wee Teck Gan for numerous discussions throughout the preparation of this work.
