2.
Preliminaries

In this section, we first establish basic properties of the quadratic Hilbert symbol over our
2
-adic field
F
. After that, we will review the basic theory of Chevalley groups.

2.1.
2
-adic Hilbert symbols

Let

(-,-):F^{\times}\times F^{\times}\longrightarrow\{\pm 1\}

be the quadratic Hilbert symbol of the
2
-adic field
F
. (For the general properties of the Hilbert symbol we refer the reader to
[
Neu99
, Chapter V §3]
; for the more specialized results used in this paper see Appendix
A
.) Throughout, we often use

(x,-x)=(x,1-x)=1{\quad\text{and}\quad}(x,x)=(x,-1)

for all
x\in F^{\times}
such that the symbols are defined.

To investigate the structure of a double cover of a Chevalley group over a
2
-adic field we need to analyze the Hilbert symbol restricted to
\mathcal{O}^{\times}\times\mathcal{O}^{\times}
. Define

(2.1)

R:=\{a\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}\,:\,\text{$(a,-)$ is trivial on $\mathcal{O}^{\times}$}\},

which we call the
integral radical
of the Hilbert symbol.

In the next proposition we collect some properties of the quadratic
2
-adic Hilbert symbol.

Proposition 2.2
.

(1)

(1+\varpi^{2e}\mathcal{O})\mathcal{O}^{\times 2}=R
and
1+\varpi^{2e+1}\mathcal{O}\subseteq\mathcal{O}^{\times 2}
.

(2)

(1+\varpi^{2e}\mathcal{O})/\penalty((1+\varpi^{2e}\mathcal{O})\cap\mathcal{O}^{\times 2})\cong R/\penalty\mathcal{O}^{\times 2}\cong\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}
.

(3)

[\mathcal{O}^{\times}:R]=2^{ef}
.

Proof.

See Appendix
A
.
∎

The following corollary will be crucially used in this paper.

Corollary 2.3
.

Let
u\in(1+\varpi^{2e}\mathcal{O})\smallsetminus\mathcal{O}^{\times 2}
, which exists by the above proposition. Then for all odd
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and
a\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
, we have

(u,a\varpi^{k})=-1.

Proof.

Since
u\in R
, we know
(a,u)=1
. So
(u,a\varpi^{k})=(u,\varpi)
because
k
is odd. Since every element in
F^{\times}
is written as
a\varpi^{n}
for some
a\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, if
(u,\varpi)=1
, then we would have
(u,x)=1
for all
x\in F^{\times}
, which would imply
u\in\mathcal{O}^{\times 2}
.
∎

The Hilbert symbol restricted to units descends to a non-degenerate bilinear form

\mathcal{O}^{\times}/\penalty R\times\mathcal{O}^{\times}/\penalty R\longrightarrow\{\pm 1\}.

Since
\mathcal{O}^{\times 2}\subseteq R
, we can consider
\mathcal{O}^{\times}/\penalty R
as an
\mathbb{F}_{2}
vector space with a nondegenerate symmetric bilinear form. (We note that this form may not arise from a quadratic form on
\mathcal{O}^{\times}/\penalty R
.) For later purposes, we need to decompose this symmetric bilinear form as follows.

Proposition 2.4
.

Let
S\subseteq\mathcal{O}^{\times}/\penalty R
be the set of all isotropic vectors, namely

S=\{u\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}/\penalty R\,:\,(u,u)=1\}.

Then there exists a subspace
D\subseteq\mathcal{O}^{\times}/\penalty R
with
\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}D\leq 2
such that

\mathcal{O}^{\times}/\penalty R=D\oplus D^{\perp}{\quad\text{and}\quad}D^{\perp}\subseteq S.

To be more explicit, we have the following.

(i)

If
-1\in R
, then
D=0
and
S=\mathcal{O}^{\times}/\penalty R
.

(ii)

If
(-1,-1)=-1
, then
D=\langle-1\rangle
and
\langle-1\rangle^{\perp}=S
.

(iii)

If
(-1,-1)=1
and
-1\notin R
, then
D
is diagonalizable and 2-dimensional with
-1\in D
and
D^{\perp}\subsetneq S
. (Here, we have
D^{\perp}\subsetneq S
because
-1\in S
but
-1\notin D^{\perp}
.)

Proof.

Assume
-1\in R
. Then for all
u\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
, we have
(u,u)=(u,-1)=1
. Hence every vector in
\mathcal{O}^{\times}/\penalty R
is isotropic.

Next assume
(-1,-1)=-1
. Then the 1-dimensional subspace
\langle-1\rangle
is non-degenerate. So we have the orthogonal sum decomposition
\mathcal{O}^{\times}/\penalty R=\langle-1\rangle\oplus\langle-1\rangle^{\perp}
. (See
[
Kit93
, Proposition 1.1.1]
for the existence of orthogonal sum decomposition.) Since
(u,u)=(u,-1)
for all
u\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
, we know that
u\in S
if and only if
u\in\langle-1\rangle^{\perp}
.

Finally, assume
(-1,-1)=1
and
-1\notin R
. Since
-1\notin R
, there exists
u_{0}\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
such that
(-1,u_{0})=-1
, so
(u_{0},u_{0})=-1
. One can then check that
(-u_{0},-u_{0})=-1
. Since
u_{0}\neq-u_{0}
, we have the 2-dimensional subspace
D:=\langle u_{0},-u_{0}\rangle
generated by
u_{0}
and
-u_{0}
. (Written multiplicatively,
D=\{\pm 1,\pm u_{0}\}
.) Certainly this space is non-degenerate and contains
-1
. We then have the orthogonal sum decomposition
\mathcal{O}^{\times}/\penalty R=D\oplus D^{\perp}
. Now, let
u\in D^{\perp}
. Since
-1\in D
, we have
(u,-1)=1
, so
(u,u)=1
, namely
u\in S
.
∎

Remark
.

All the three cases in Proposition
2.4
can occur. Case (
i
) occurs whenever
\sqrt{-1}\in F
; Case (
ii
) occurs whenever
[F:\mathbb{Q}_{2}]
is odd because then
(-1,-1)=(-1,N_{F/\penalty\mathbb{Q}_{2}}(-1))_{\mathbb{Q}_{2}}=(-1,-1)_{\mathbb{Q}_{2}}=-1
, where
(-,-)_{\mathbb{Q}_{2}}
is the Hilbert symbol for
\mathbb{Q}_{2}
; Case (
iii
) occurs whenever
\sqrt{-1}\notin F
and
[F:\mathbb{Q}_{2}]
is even, because then
(-1,-1)=(-1,N_{F/\penalty\mathbb{Q}_{2}}(-1))_{\mathbb{Q}_{2}}=1
and
-1=1-2\in 1+\varpi^{e}\mathcal{O}^{\times}
so that
-1\notin R
.

Let
D
be as in the above proposition, and let
\{u_{1},\dots,u_{k}\}
be an orthogonal basis for
D
, where actually
k=0,1,2
. Note that we have

(u_{i},u_{j})=\begin{cases}1&\text{if $i\neq j$};\\
-1&\text{if $i=j$}.\end{cases}

Also since
D^{\perp}\subseteq S
, it admits a symplectic basis (
[
Kit93
, Theorem 1.2.1]
); namely there exists a basis
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{\ell},f_{1},\dots,f_{\ell}\}
of
D^{\perp}
such that

(e_{i},f_{i})=\begin{cases}1&\text{if $i\neq j$};\\
-1&\text{if $i=j$},\end{cases}{\quad\text{and}\quad}(e_{i},e_{j})=(f_{i},f_{j})=1\;\text{for all $i,j$}.

We fix these basis vectors once and for all, so that we have

\mathcal{O}^{\times}/\penalty R=D\oplus D^{\perp}=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\{u_{1},\dots,u_{k}\}\oplus\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\{e_{1},\dots,e_{\ell},f_{1},\dots,f_{\ell}\}.

2.2.
Chevalley groups

In this subsection we review the basic structure theory of Chevalley groups and establish our notation. In particular we recall a presentation of such groups.

Let
G
be the group of
F
-rational points of an almost simple simply-connected Chevalley group. Let
T\subseteq G
be a maximal split torus and let
\Phi=\Phi(G,T)
be the irreducible root system associated with the pair
(G,T)
. Let
W=N_{G}(T)/\penalty T
be the Weyl group. Let
X=X^{*}(T)
and
Y=X_{*}(T)
be the group of rational characters and cocharacters, respectively, so that
(X,\Phi,Y,\Phi^{\vee})
is the root datum for
(G,T)
, where
\Phi^{\vee}
is the set of coroots as usual. Note that we have the bijection

\Phi\xrightarrow{\;\sim\;}\Phi^{\vee},\quad\alpha\mapsto{\check{\alpha}}.

Let

\langle-,-\rangle:X\times Y\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}

be the canonical pairing. It is
W
-invariant and
\langle\alpha,{\check{\alpha}}\rangle=2
for all
\alpha\in\Phi
. It should be mentioned that if
\Phi
is simply-laced we have

\langle\alpha,{\check{\beta}}\rangle=\langle\beta,{\check{\alpha}}\rangle

for all
\alpha,\beta\in\Phi
. Since
G
is simply-connected, we have
Y=\mathbb{Z}\Phi^{\vee}
. So, the identification of
\Phi
with
\Phi^{\vee}
gives rise to a
W
-invariant inner product on
X
, which we denote by the same symbol
\langle-,-\rangle
,

\langle-,-\rangle:X\times X\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}.

Although the symbol
\langle-,-\rangle
has two meanings, the correct one will be clear from context.

Let
B=B^{+}\subseteq G
be a Borel subgroup containing
T
with unipotent radical
U=U^{+}
. Let
B^{-}\supset U^{-}
be the opposite Borel and its unipotent radical. The Borel subgroup
B
determines a set of simple roots

\Delta=\{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{r}\}\subseteq\Phi,

which determines positive roots
\Phi^{+}\subseteq\Phi
. We order the simple roots by Bourbaki’s convention. Let

\alpha_{0}=\text{lowest root with respect to $\Delta$}.

Now we recall a presentation for
G
. The group
G
is generated by the symbols of the form
x_{\alpha}(t)
, namely

G=\langle x_{\alpha}(t)\,:\,\alpha\in\Phi,t\in F\rangle.

For
t\in F^{\times}
, define

\displaystyle w_{\alpha}(t)

\displaystyle:=x_{\alpha}(t)x_{-\alpha}(-t^{-1})x_{\alpha}(t);

\displaystyle h_{\alpha}(t)

\displaystyle:=w_{\alpha}(t)w_{\alpha}(-1).

Then the following is a complete set of relations for the generators
x_{\alpha}(t)
, when
rk(G)\geq 2
:

(R1)

\displaystyle x_{\alpha}(t)x_{\alpha}(u)

\displaystyle=x_{\alpha}(t+u);

(R2)

\displaystyle[x_{\alpha}(t),x_{\beta}(u)]

\displaystyle=\prod_{i\alpha+j\beta\in\Phi}x_{i\alpha+j\beta}(c_{ij}t^{i}u^{j}),\quad(\alpha+\beta\neq 0);

(R3)

\displaystyle h_{\alpha}(t)h_{\alpha}(u)

\displaystyle=h_{\alpha}(tu).

where the
c_{ij}\in\{\pm 1\}
are independent of
t
and
u
but dependent on
\alpha
and
\beta
and the ordering of the roots. Note that, if
i\alpha+j\beta\notin\Phi
, then we interpret
x_{i\alpha+j\beta}(c_{ij}t^{i}u^{j})
as 1. If
\Phi
is simply-laced, (
R2
) simplifies to

(R2
′
)

[x_{\alpha}(t),x_{\beta}(u)]=\begin{cases}1&\text{if $\alpha+\beta\notin\Phi$};\\
x_{\alpha+\beta}(ctu)&\text{if $\alpha+\beta\in\Phi$},\end{cases}\quad(\alpha+\beta\neq 0),

where
c\in\{\pm 1\}
. Note also that
w_{\alpha}(t)^{-1}=w_{\alpha}(-t)
.

The following relations follow from (
R1
), (
R2
), (
R3
):

(R4)

\displaystyle h_{\alpha}(t)h_{\beta}(u)

\displaystyle=h_{\beta}(u)h_{\alpha}(t);

(R5)

\displaystyle w_{\alpha}(t)h_{\beta}(u)w_{\alpha}(-t)

\displaystyle=h_{w_{\alpha}(\beta)}(u);.

(R6)

\displaystyle w_{\alpha}(t)w_{\beta}(u)w_{\alpha}(-t)

\displaystyle=w_{w_{\alpha}(\beta)}(ct^{-\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle}u),

where
c=c(\alpha,\beta)=\pm 1
.

2.3.
Some notation for subgroups

Let us establish some notation for subgroups of
G
.

The maximal torus
T
is generated by the
h_{\alpha}(t)
’s, namely

T=\langle h_{\alpha}(t)\,:\,t\in F^{\times},\alpha\in\Phi\rangle.

For
\alpha\in\Phi
and a subgroup
A\subseteq F^{\times}
, let

T_{\alpha,A}:=\{h_{\alpha}(a)\,:\,a\in A\},

and

T_{A}:=\langle h_{\alpha}(a)\,:\,a\in A,\alpha\in\Phi\rangle,

both of which are subgroups of T. In particular, we often use

(2.5)

T_{R}:=\langle h_{\alpha}(a)\,:\,a\in R,\alpha\in\Phi\rangle,

where
R
is the integral radical defined in (
2.1
). We also set

(2.6)

T_{0}=T_{\mathcal{O}^{\times}}=\langle h_{\alpha}(a)\,:\,a\in\mathcal{O}^{\times},\alpha\in\Phi\rangle.

We write

T_{\alpha,k}:=T_{\alpha,1+\varpi^{k}\mathcal{O}}=\{h_{\alpha}(a)\,:\,a\in 1+\varpi^{k}\mathcal{O}\}

and

(2.7)

T_{k}:=T_{1+\varpi^{k}\mathcal{O}}=\langle h_{\alpha}(a)\,:\,a\in 1+\varpi^{k}\mathcal{O},\alpha\in\Phi\rangle.

Note that every element
t\in T_{A}
is uniquely written as
t=\prod_{1\leq i\leq r}h_{\alpha_{i}}(a_{i})
for some
a_{i}\in A
by using only the simple roots.

Next, for any subgroup
A\subseteq F
and any
\alpha\in\Phi
, we set

U_{\alpha}(A)=\langle x_{\alpha}(a)\,:\,a\in A\rangle

or more generally for any subset
S\subseteq\Phi
, we set

U_{S}(A)=\langle x_{\alpha}(a)\,:\,a\in A,\alpha\in S\rangle.

In particular, we set

U^{\pm}(A)=U_{\Phi^{\pm}}(A).

Also we set

(2.8)

U_{\alpha,j}:=U_{\alpha}(\varpi^{j}\mathcal{O}),\quad U_{j}^{\pm}:=U_{\Phi^{\pm}}(\varpi^{j}\mathcal{O}){\quad\text{and}\quad}U^{\pm}=U^{\pm}(F),

where
j\in\mathbb{Z}
. We often omit the superscript
+
, so in particular

(2.9)

U_{j}=U_{j}^{+}=U_{\Phi^{+}}(\varpi^{j}\mathcal{O}).

Set

K:=\langle x_{\alpha}(a)\,:\,a\in\mathcal{O},\alpha\in\Phi\rangle,

namely the group generated by the subgroups
U_{\alpha,0}
for all
\alpha\in\Phi
. This group
K
is a hyperspecial maximal compact subgroup of
G
. Note that
T_{0}\subseteq K
. Also set

I:=\langle U_{0},T_{0},U_{1}^{-}\rangle,

namely the group generated by
U_{0},T_{0},U_{1}^{-}
, which is an Iwahori subgroup of
G
.

2.4.
Affine Weyl group of
G

In this subsection, we review the basic theory of the affine Weyl group of the split reductive group
G
over
F
. (In this subsection,
G
is not necessarily our Chevalley group but any connected almost simple split reductive group over
F
.)

Let

W_{\mathrm{ea}}:=N_{G}(T)/\penalty T_{0}

be the extended affine Weyl group. The Iwahori-Bruhat decomposition tells that there is a natural bijection

(2.10)

W_{\mathrm{ea}}\xrightarrow{\;\sim\;}I\backslash G/\penalty I,\quad w\mapsto IwI.

Note that
W_{\mathrm{ea}}
acts faithfully on
Y\otimes\mathbb{R}
as affine transformations via the Tits homomorphism
[
Tit79
, page 32]
.

For each
\alpha\in\Phi
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, let

H_{\alpha,k}:=\{v\in Y\otimes\mathbb{R}\,:\,\langle\alpha,v\rangle=k\}

be the affine hyperplane, where
\langle-,-\rangle
is the (
\mathbb{R}
-valued pairing) on
(X\otimes\mathbb{R})\times(Y\otimes\mathbb{R})
naturally induced from the canonical pairing on
X\times Y
. We then define

w_{\alpha,k}:Y\otimes\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow Y\otimes\mathbb{R}

by

w_{\alpha,k}(v)=v-(\langle\alpha,v\rangle-k){\check{\alpha}},

which is the affine reflection fixing
H_{\alpha,k}
. We let the affine Weyl group

W_{\mathrm{af}}:=\langle w_{\alpha,k}\,:\,\alpha\in\Phi,k\in\mathbb{Z}\rangle

be the group of generated by the affine reflections
w_{\alpha,k}
. We will write
w_{\alpha}=w_{\alpha,0}
. Let

W=\langle w_{\alpha}\rangle=\{w\in W_{\mathrm{af}}\,:\,w(0)=0\}.

We call this subgroup the finite Weyl group. It is isomorphic to the Weyl group of
(G,T)
.

With the choice of a set of simple roots
\Delta\subset\Phi
, the affine Weyl group
W_{\mathrm{af}}
can be realized as a Coxeter group with generators
S_{\mathrm{af},\Delta}=\{w_{\alpha}|\alpha\in\Delta\}\cup\{w_{\alpha_{0},-1}\}
, where we recall that
\alpha_{0}
is the lowest root relative to
\Delta
. Similarly,
W
is a Coxeter group with generating set
S_{\Delta}=\{w_{\alpha}|\alpha\in\Delta\}
. Note that the generators in
S_{\mathrm{af},\Delta}
are the reflections through the walls of the alcove

\mathfrak{A}:=\{v\in Y\otimes\mathbb{R}\,:\,\alpha(v)>0\text{ for all }\alpha\in\Delta\text{ and }\alpha_{0}(v)>-1\}.

One can define the length function

\ell=\ell_{\Delta}:W_{\mathrm{af}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}

by setting
\ell(w)
to be the minimum number of generators needed to express
w
. This naturally extends the length function of the finite Weyl group
W
.

The map
W\ltimes\mathbb{Z}[\Phi^{\vee}]\to W_{\mathrm{af}}
, defined by
(w,y)\mapsto(v\mapsto w(v)+y)
, is a group isomorphism

W_{\mathrm{af}}\cong W\ltimes\mathbb{Z}[\Phi^{\vee}].

Similarly, the group
W_{\mathrm{ea}}
is generated by
W_{\mathrm{af}}
and translations by elements of
Y
, and one has

W_{\mathrm{ea}}\cong W\ltimes Y.

Let
\Omega\subset W_{\mathrm{ea}}
be the subgroup that preserves
\mathfrak{A}
. The action of
\Omega
on
\mathfrak{A}
permutes the supporting hyperplanes of
\mathfrak{A}
and thus induces an automorphism on the affine Dynkin diagram. Thus
\Omega
permutes the generators of
W_{\mathrm{af}}
from which it follows that
\Omega
normalizes
W_{\mathrm{af}}
. It is also known that
W_{\mathrm{ea}}
is generated by
\Omega
and
W_{\mathrm{af}}
, and
\Omega\cap W_{\mathrm{af}}=\{1\}
, and thus

W_{\mathrm{ea}}\cong\Omega\ltimes W_{\mathrm{af}}.

With this decomposition we can extend the length function
\ell
on
W_{\mathrm{af}}
to a length function on
W_{\mathrm{ea}}
by defining
\ell(\omega w)=\ell(w)
, where
w\in W_{\mathrm{af}}
and
\omega\in\Omega
. Finally, the group
\Omega
can be shown to be isomorphic to the finite abelian group
Y/\mathbb{Z}[\Phi^{\vee}]
.

3.
Double covers

It is known that the Chevalley group
G
has a unique nontrivial topological
\mu_{2}
-extension
\widetilde{G}
, which fits into an exact sequence

(3.1)

1\longrightarrow\mu_{2}\longrightarrow\widetilde{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{pr}}}{{\longrightarrow}}G\longrightarrow 1.

In this section, we will establish various important properties of this
\mu_{2}
-extension
\widetilde{G}
. Although we assume that everything in this section is essentially known to experts, we will give a self-contained exposition and fix our notation. Also from this section on, we assume that
G
is the almost simple simply-connected Chevalley group associated with a simply-laced root system
\Phi
, unless otherwise stated. Also we exclude
G=\operatorname{SL}_{2}
. Hence
G
is a Chevalley group of type
A_{r}(r\geq 2)
,
D_{r}(r\geq 3)
,
E_{6},E_{7}
or
E_{8}
.

3.1.
Steinberg relations

In terms of generators and relations,
\widetilde{G}
is given by

\widetilde{G}=\langle\epsilon\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(t)\,:\,t\in F,\alpha\in\Phi,\epsilon\in\mu_{2}\rangle

subject to the relations

(MR1)

\displaystyle\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(t)\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(u)

\displaystyle=\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(t+u);

(MR2)

\displaystyle[\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(t),\tilde{x}_{\beta}(u)]

\displaystyle=\prod_{i\alpha+j\beta\in\Phi}\tilde{x}_{i\alpha+j\beta}(c_{ij}t^{i}u^{j}),\quad(\alpha+\beta\neq 0);

(MR3)

\displaystyle\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(t)\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(u)

\displaystyle=(t,u)\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(tu),

where
c_{ij}
is the same as linear case. Again, if
\Phi
is simply-laced, we have

(MR2
′
)

[\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(t),\tilde{x}_{\beta}(u)]=\begin{cases}1&\text{if $\alpha+\beta\notin\Phi$};\\
\tilde{x}_{\alpha+\beta}(ctu)&\text{if $\alpha+\beta\in\Phi$},\end{cases}\quad(\alpha+\beta\neq 0),

where
c\in\{\pm 1\}
. As before, we set

\displaystyle\tilde{w}_{\alpha}(t)

\displaystyle:=\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(t)\tilde{x}_{-\alpha}(-t^{-1})\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(t);

\displaystyle\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(t)

\displaystyle:=\tilde{w}_{\alpha}(t)\tilde{w}_{\alpha}(-1).

Let us note that, under the projection
\widetilde{G}\xrightarrow{\,\operatorname{pr}\,}G
, the images of the elements
\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(t),\tilde{w}_{\alpha}(t)
and
\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(t)
are, respectively,
x_{\alpha}(t),w_{\alpha}(t)
and
h_{\alpha}(t)
.

Many of the important relations holding for these symbols are summarized in
[
Mat69
, p.24]
. In particular, we need to mention the following:

(MR4)

\displaystyle\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(t)\tilde{h}_{\beta}(u)

\displaystyle=(u,t^{\langle\beta,\alpha\rangle})\tilde{h}_{\beta}(u)\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(t);

(MR5)

\displaystyle\tilde{w}_{\alpha}(t)\tilde{h}_{\beta}(u)\tilde{w}_{\alpha}(-t)

\displaystyle=\tilde{h}_{w_{\alpha}(\beta)}(ct^{-\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle}u)\tilde{h}_{w_{\alpha}(\beta)}(ct^{-\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle})^{-1}

\displaystyle=(u,ct^{-\langle\beta,\alpha\rangle})\tilde{h}_{w_{\alpha}(\beta)}(u);

(MR6)

\displaystyle\tilde{w}_{\alpha}(t)\tilde{w}_{\beta}(u)\tilde{w}_{\alpha}(-t)

\displaystyle=\tilde{w}_{w_{\alpha}(\beta)}(ct^{-\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle}u)

where
c=c(\alpha,\beta)\in\{\pm 1\}
is as in (
R6
), which is denoted by
\eta
in
[
Mat69
, (c), p.24]
. As a special case of (
MR5
), we have

(MR5’)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \tilde{w}_{\alpha}(1)\tilde{h}_{\beta}(u)\tilde{w}_{\alpha}(-1)&=\tilde{h}_{\beta}(u)\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(u),\quad(\alpha+\beta\in\Phi);\\
\tilde{w}_{\alpha}(1)\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(u)\tilde{w}_{\alpha}(-1)&=\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(u^{-1}).\end{aligned}

We often use the following.

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
\alpha\in\Phi
. Let
t,u\in F^{\times}
be such that
1+tu\neq 0
. Then

\tilde{x}_{\alpha}\,(t)\tilde{x}_{-\alpha}(u)=(1+tu,-t)\,\tilde{x}_{-\alpha}(\frac{u}{1+tu})\,\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(1+tu)\,\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(\frac{t}{1+tu}),

where we note

(1+tu,-t)=(1+tu,u).

Proof.

This is an elementary exercise using the Steinberg relations. Also note that
(1+tu,-t)=(1+tu,1-(1+tu))(1+tu,-t)=(1+tu,u)
.
∎

The following corollary is immediate.

Corollary 3.3
.

Let
\alpha\in\Phi
. Let
t\in\mathcal{O}
and
u\in\varpi^{2e}\mathcal{O}
. Then

\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(t)\,\tilde{x}_{-\alpha}(u)=\tilde{x}_{-\alpha}(\frac{u}{1+tu})\,\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(1+tu)\,\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(\frac{t}{1+tu}).

Proof.

If
t\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
, then
(1+tu,-t)=1
because
1+tu\in U_{2e}\subseteq R
, where we recall
R
is the integral radical. If
t\in\varpi\mathcal{O}
, then
(1+tu,-t)=1
because
1+tu\in U_{2e+1}\subseteq\mathcal{O}^{\times 2}
.
∎

For any subgroup
H\subseteq G
let

\widetilde{H}:=\operatorname{pr}^{-1}(H).

We say that
H
splits sequence (
3.1
) or splits to
\widetilde{G}
if there exists a group homomorphism
\mathbf{s}:H\rightarrow\widetilde{G}
such that
\operatorname{pr}\circ\mathbf{s}=id_{H}
. In this case,
H\times\mu_{2}\cong\widetilde{H}
via the map
(h,\pm 1)\mapsto\pm\mathbf{s}(h)
.

From the Steinberg relations (
MR1
) and (
MR2
) there is a group homomorphism

(3.4)

\mathbf{s}^{\pm}:U^{\pm}(F)\longrightarrow\widetilde{G}\text{\quad such that\quad}\mathbf{s}^{\pm}(x_{\alpha}(t))=\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(t)

for all
\alpha\in\Phi^{\pm}
and
t\in F
. This defines a splitting for
U^{\pm}
.

3.2.
The lattice
\widetilde{Y}

In this subsection, we will introduce a certain sublattice
\widetilde{Y}
of
Y
and explicitly describe the quotient
\widetilde{Y}/\penalty 2Y
. We will then show how the lattice
\widetilde{Y}
is used to describe the center
Z(\widetilde{T})
of the covering torus
\widetilde{T}
. The description of the quotient
\widetilde{Y}/\penalty 2Y
(Proposition
3.7
) is essentially
[
Sav04
, Proposition 4.2]
. For our construction of pseudo-spherical representations, however, it will be convenient for us to reprove this result.

Recall that
Y=X_{*}(T)
is the group of cocharacters (
i.e.
cocharacter lattice). Since our group
G
is simply-connected, we have
Y=\mathbb{Z}[\Phi^{\vee}]
. Hence each
y\in Y
is uniquely written as

y=\sum_{i}c_{i}{\check{\alpha}}_{i}.

For each
t\in F^{\times}
, we set

y(t)=h_{\alpha_{1}}(t^{c_{1}})\cdots h_{\alpha_{n}}(t^{c_{n}})\in T.

Then the maximal torus
T
is generated by
y(t)
’s. Indeed, we have the isomorphism

Y\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}F^{\times}\longrightarrow T,\quad y\otimes t\mapsto y(t).

Let us consider the analog for the cover
\widetilde{T}
. For each
y=\sum_{i}c_{i}{\check{\alpha}}_{i}
and
t\in F^{\times}
, set

\tilde{y}(t)=\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{1}}(t^{c_{1}})\cdots\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{n}}(t^{c_{n}})\in\widetilde{T},

where the order of multiplication is the order of
\Delta^{\vee}
. However, since
\widetilde{T}
is not commutative due to (MR3) and (MR4), this product is not independent of the order of multiplication. We can, however, choose a sublattice of
Y
so that the product is independent of the ordering as follows.

Let

(3.5)

\widetilde{Y}=\{y\in Y\,:\,\langle\alpha,y\rangle\in 2\mathbb{Z}\;\text{for all $\alpha\in\Phi$}\}.

Note that

2Y\subseteq\widetilde{Y}\subseteq Y.

We will prove that the product of
\tilde{y}(t)
is independent of the ordering of
y\in\widetilde{Y}
. For this purpose, we explicitly derive the conditions the coefficients
c_{i}
’s of
y=\sum_{i}c_{i}{\check{\alpha}}_{i}\in\widetilde{Y}
have to satisfy modulo
2\mathbb{Z}
, namely the “parity conditions” to be satisfied by the
c_{i}
’s, as follows.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
y=\sum_{i}c_{i}{\check{\alpha}}_{i}\in\widetilde{Y}
and fix a simple root
\alpha_{k}
. Then we have

\sum_{\text{$\alpha_{i}$ is adjacent to $\alpha_{k}$}}c_{i}\in 2\mathbb{Z}.

Note that the number of
c_{i}
’s in the sum is at most
3
.

Further, if
\alpha_{k}
is adjacent to a root which has no other adjacent root than
\alpha_{k}
itself (i.e.
\alpha_{k}
is adjacent to an “endpoint” in the Dynkin diagram), then
c_{k}\in 2\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

Since

\langle\alpha_{k},y\rangle=2c_{k}-\sum_{\text{$\alpha_{i}$ is adjacent to $\alpha_{k}$}}c_{i}\in 2\mathbb{Z},

the first part of the lemma follows.

Assume
\alpha_{k}
is adjacent to an endpoint
\alpha_{\ell}
. Then

\langle\alpha_{\ell},y\rangle=2c_{\ell}-c_{k}\in 2\mathbb{Z},

which gives
c_{k}\in 2\mathbb{Z}
. The lemma follows.
∎

Now, given
y=\sum_{i}c_{i}{\check{\alpha}}_{i}\in\widetilde{Y}
, we describe the parity of each
c_{i}
by using the Dynkin diagram as follows. If
c_{i}
is even, we denote the corresponding node for
\alpha_{i}
in the Dynkin diagram by a black dot, and if it is odd, we denote it by a white dot. For example, assume our
\Phi
is the
E_{7}
root system. If
y=\sum_{i=1}^{6}c_{i}{\check{\alpha}}_{i}
is such that
c_{1},c_{3},c_{4}
and
c_{6}
are even and the others are odd, then one can readily see that
y\in\widetilde{Y}
. We denote this
y
modulo
2Y
by

\alpha_{1}
\alpha_{2}
\alpha_{3}
\alpha_{4}
\alpha_{5}
\alpha_{6}
\alpha_{7}
.

Furthermore, one can see that this is the only non-zero element in
\widetilde{Y}/\penalty 2Y
for
E_{7}
by using Lemma
3.6
above, and hence
\widetilde{Y}/\penalty 2Y=\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}
.

With this convention, we have

Proposition 3.7
.

Depending on the type of the Dynkin diagram, we have

\widetilde{Y}/\penalty 2Y=\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}&\text{Type $D_{2r}$};\\
\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}&\text{Type $A_{2r},D_{2r+1}$ and $E_{7}$};\\
1&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

The results are summarized in Table
1
, where the nontrivial elements in
\widetilde{Y}/\penalty 2Y
are described by Dynkin diagrams with white dots indicating odd and black dots even.

Proof.

This can be verified case by case using Lemma
3.6
. Since the verification is elementary, we omit the details.
∎

Table 1.
Lattice
\widetilde{Y}

Type

\widetilde{Y}/\penalty 2Y

\left|Y/\penalty\widetilde{Y}\right|

non-trivial elements in
\widetilde{Y}/\penalty 2Y

A_{2r+1}

\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}

2^{2r}

A_{2r}

1

2^{2r}

D_{2r+1}

\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}

2^{2r}

D_{2r}

\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}

2^{2r-2}

E_{6}

1

2^{6}

E_{7}

\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}

2^{6}

E_{8}

1

2^{8}

As a small corollary, let us mention

Corollary 3.8
.

Let
y=\sum_{i}c_{i}{\check{\alpha}}_{i}\in\widetilde{Y}
. If
\alpha_{i}
and
\alpha_{j}
are adjacent to each other, then
c_{i}c_{j}\in 2\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

This can be read off from the Dynkin diagrams in Table
1
.

∎

This corollary implies

Proposition 3.9
.

Let
y=\sum_{i}c_{i}{\check{\alpha}}_{i}\in\widetilde{Y}
and
t_{i}\in F^{\times}
. The product

\prod_{i}\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(t_{i}^{c_{i}})

is independent of the ordering.

Proof.

We have only to show that if
\alpha_{i}
and
\alpha_{j}
are adjacent then

\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(t_{i}^{c_{i}})\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{j}}(t_{j}^{c_{j}})=\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{j}}(t_{j}^{c_{j}})\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(t_{i}^{c_{i}}).

But

\displaystyle\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(t_{i}^{c_{i}})\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{j}}(t_{j}^{c_{j}})

\displaystyle=(t_{i}^{c_{i}},t_{j}^{-c_{j}})\,\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{j}}(t_{j}^{c_{j}})\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(t_{i}^{c_{i}})\quad\text{by (MR4)}

\displaystyle=(t_{i},t_{j})^{c_{i}c_{j}}\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{j}}(t_{j}^{c_{j}})\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(t_{i}^{c_{i}}),

and
(t_{i},t_{j})^{c_{i}c_{j}}=1
by the above corollary.
∎

In particular, for each
y=\sum_{i}c_{i}{\check{\alpha}}_{i}\in\widetilde{Y}
and
t\in F^{\times}
, we can define

\tilde{y}(t):=\prod_{i}\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(t^{c_{i}}),

which is independent of the ordering by Proposition
3.9
with
t=t_{i}
for all
i
.

3.3.
The Weyl group action on
\widetilde{Y}

The Weyl group
W
acts on
\widetilde{Y}
because the inner product
\langle-,-\rangle
is
W
-invariant. Also
2Y
is
W
-invariant. Hence the action of
W
on
\widetilde{Y}
descends to
\widetilde{Y}/\penalty 2Y
. But this action of
W
on
\widetilde{Y}/\penalty 2Y
is trivial; namely we have

Lemma 3.10
.

If
y\in\widetilde{Y}
, then
wy=y
in
\widetilde{Y}/\penalty 2Y
for all
w\in W
.

Proof.

Let
\alpha\in\Delta
be fixed. We have to show
w_{\alpha}y=y\pmod{2Y}
. Note that

w_{\alpha}y=y-\langle\alpha,y\rangle{\check{\alpha}}.

Now the result follows because
y\in\widetilde{Y}
.

∎

3.4.
The group
\mathcal{W}

For each
\alpha\in\Phi
, let

\tilde{w}_{\alpha}:=\tilde{w}_{\alpha}(1).

We then define

(3.11)

\mathcal{W}:=\langle\tilde{w}_{\alpha}\,:\,\alpha\in\Phi\rangle,

namely the group generated by the
\tilde{w}_{\alpha}
’s.

Note that

\tilde{w}_{\alpha}(1)^{-1}=\tilde{w}_{\alpha}(-1).

Since
\tilde{w}_{\alpha}(-1)^{2}=\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(-1)
and
\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(-1)^{-1}=(-1,-1)\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(-1)
we have

(3.12)

\tilde{w}_{\alpha}(1)^{2}=(-1,-1)\cdot\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(-1).

Hence each
\tilde{w}_{\alpha}
has order 4 or 8, depending on
(-1,-1)=1
or
-1
because
\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(-1)^{2}=(-1,-1)
. The following should be mentioned : if
\alpha+\beta
is a root, then

(3.13)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \tilde{w}_{\alpha}(1)\tilde{w}_{\beta}(1)\tilde{w}_{\alpha}(1)&=\tilde{w}_{\beta}(1)\tilde{w}_{\alpha}(1)\tilde{w}_{\beta}(1)\quad\text{(braid relation)};\\
\tilde{w}_{\alpha}(1)\tilde{h}_{\beta}(t)\tilde{w}_{\alpha}(-1)&=\tilde{h}_{\beta}(t)\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(t),\end{aligned}

where the second one is actually (
MR5’
) listed earlier. (See
[
Mat69
, Théorème 6]
and
[
Sav04
, Lemma 3.2]
.) Set

\widetilde{T}(\mathbb{Z}):=\langle\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(-1)\,:\,\alpha\in\Delta\rangle.

Then we have the exact sequence

(3.14)

1\longrightarrow\widetilde{T}(\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow\mathcal{W}\longrightarrow W\longrightarrow 1,

where
W
is the Weyl group of
G
and the surjection sends each
\tilde{w}_{\alpha}(1)
to the corresponding Weyl group element.

3.5.
Affine Weyl group of
\widetilde{G}

In this section, we modify the theory of affine Weyl groups of the linear group
G
described in §
2.4
to that of the covering group
\widetilde{G}
.

First, note that the root datum
(X,\Phi,Y,\Phi^{\vee})
is isomorphic to the root datum

(\frac{1}{2}X,\frac{1}{2}\Phi,2Y,2\Phi^{\vee}).

We also have a modified root datum of
\widetilde{G}

[
McN12
,
Wei14
]

(\widetilde{X},\frac{1}{2}\Phi,\widetilde{Y},2\Phi^{\vee}),

where
\widetilde{Y}
is as before and
\widetilde{X}=\operatorname{Hom}(\widetilde{Y},\mathbb{Z})
. This modified datum is that of the linear group

G^{\prime}:=G/\penalty Z_{2},

where
Z_{2}
is the 2-torsion of the center of
G
. Note that

Z_{2}\cong\widetilde{Y}/\penalty\mathbb{Z}[2\Phi^{\vee}].

Let
\widetilde{W}_{\mathrm{af}}
be the affine Weyl group associated with the root system
\frac{1}{2}\Phi
, which acts on
Y\otimes\mathbb{R}
. Fix a subset of simple roots
\Delta\subseteq\Phi
. Then
\frac{1}{2}\Delta
is a subset of simple roots for
\frac{1}{2}\Phi
. Let

(3.15)

w_{\mathrm{af}}:=w_{\alpha_{0},-2}=w_{\frac{1}{2}\alpha_{0},-1}.

One can then see that

\widetilde{W}_{\mathrm{af}}=\langle w_{\mathrm{af}},w_{\alpha_{1}},\dots,w_{\alpha_{n}}\rangle.

Also let
\widetilde{W}_{\mathrm{ea}}
be the extended affine Weyl group associated with
G^{\prime}
. We then have

\widetilde{W}_{\mathrm{ea}}\cong W\ltimes\widetilde{Y}{\quad\text{and}\quad}\widetilde{W}_{\mathrm{af}}\cong W\ltimes 2Y,

so that

1\longrightarrow\widetilde{W}_{\mathrm{af}}\longrightarrow\widetilde{W}_{\mathrm{ea}}\longrightarrow\widetilde{Y}/\penalty 2Y\longrightarrow 1.

(Note that in
[
Kar21
]
, our
\widetilde{W}_{\mathrm{ea}}
is denoted by
\widetilde{W}_{\mathrm{aff}}
, and our
\widetilde{W}_{\mathrm{af}}
by
\widehat{W}_{\mathrm{aff}}
.)

Let

\tilde{\ell}=\tilde{\ell}_{\frac{1}{2}\Delta}:\widetilde{W}_{\mathrm{ea}}\cong W\ltimes\widetilde{Y}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},

be the length function, which can be also computed by the formula of
[
IM65
, Proposition 1.23]
; namely

\tilde{\ell}(w,y)=\sum_{\alpha\in\frac{1}{2}\Phi^{+}\cap w\frac{1}{2}\Phi^{+}}|\langle\alpha,y\rangle|+\sum_{\alpha\in\frac{1}{2}\Phi^{-}\cap w\frac{1}{2}\Phi^{-}}|\langle\alpha,y\rangle+1|,

where
(w,y)\in W\ltimes\widetilde{Y}
. Let
\widetilde{\Omega}
be the set of length 0 elements. Then

\widetilde{\Omega}\cong\widetilde{Y}/\penalty 2Y;

indeed, we have

\tilde{\ell}:\widetilde{W}_{\mathrm{af}}\rtimes\widetilde{\Omega}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},\quad\tilde{\ell}(w,\varepsilon)=\tilde{\ell}(w),

for
(w,\varepsilon)\in\widetilde{W}_{\mathrm{af}}\rtimes\widetilde{\Omega}
. Note that
\widetilde{\Omega}
is the subgroup that preserves the alcove
2\mathfrak{A}
, where
\mathfrak{A}
is defined relative to
\Delta\subseteq\Phi
, as in §
2.4
.

Since
G
is simply-connected and

\widetilde{I}\backslash\widetilde{G}/\penalty\widetilde{I}=I\backslash G/\penalty I,

the Iwahori-Bruhat decomposition (
2.10
) implies

W_{\mathrm{ea}}=W_{\mathrm{af}}\cong\widetilde{I}\backslash\widetilde{G}/\penalty\widetilde{I}.

As we will see, however, the Iwahori Hecke algebra we will be considering is not supported on all of
W_{\mathrm{af}}
, but only on
\widetilde{W}_{\mathrm{ea}}
. This is precisely the rationale for introducing the groups
\widetilde{W}_{\mathrm{af}}
and
\widetilde{W}_{\mathrm{ea}}
.

4.
Pseudo-spherical representations of
\widetilde{T}_{0}

Our goal in this section is to construct the pseudo-spherical representations of
\widetilde{T}_{0}
(Definition
4.5
) and show that they are
W
-invariant and types of
\widetilde{T}
in the sense of Bushnell-Kutzko
[
BK98
, (4.1) Definition]
.

4.1.
Structure of
\widetilde{T}_{0}

Let us recall from (
2.6
) and (
2.5
) that we have defined

T_{0}=T_{\mathcal{O}}=\{h_{\alpha}(a)\,:\,a\in\mathcal{O},\alpha\in\Phi\}{\quad\text{and}\quad}T_{R}=\{h_{\alpha}(a)\,:\,a\in R,\alpha\in\Phi\},

where
R
is the integral radical as in (
2.1
). We let
\widetilde{T}_{0}
be the preimage of
T_{0}
in
\widetilde{T}
, which is the maximal compact subgroup of
\widetilde{T}
.

Let

\mathbf{s}_{T_{R}}:T_{R}\longrightarrow\widetilde{T},\quad\prod_{i}h_{\alpha_{i}}(u_{i})\mapsto\prod_{i}\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(u_{i}),

where the product on the right-hand side is independent of the ordering because
u_{i}\in R
for all
i
. By comparing the relations (
R3
) and (
R4
) with (
MR3
) and (
MR4
), one can readily see that this map is a homomorphism (
i.e.
it splits the sequence (
3.1
)), and moreover its image
\mathbf{s}_{T_{R}}(T_{R})
is normal in
\widetilde{T}_{0}
, so that we can view
T_{R}
as a subgroup of
\widetilde{T}_{0}
.

In the rest of this subsection, we will explicitly describe the structure of the finite quotient
\widetilde{T}_{0}/\penalty T_{R}
. As it turns out, the quotient
\widetilde{T}_{0}/\penalty T_{R}
admits a factorization in which each factor has a simple structure, and moreover each factor is preserved by the action of the Weyl group. To show the existence of the factorization, we will use the structure of the quotient
\mathcal{O}^{\times}/\penalty R
viewed as a symmetric bilinear form over
\mathbb{F}_{2}
given by the Hilbert symbol, which is mainly developed in Proposition
2.4
. We will construct a pseudo-spherical representation of
\widetilde{T}_{0}
by constructing a Weyl-invariant representation for each of the factors in this factorization.

Now, recall from Proposition
2.4
that we have the orthogonal sum decomposition

\mathcal{O}^{\times}/\penalty R=D\oplus D^{\perp},

where
\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}D\leq 2
and
D^{\perp}
consists of isotropic vectors. Also recall we have chosen an orthogonal set
\{u_{1},\dots,u_{k}\}
of
D
, and a symplectic basis
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{\ell},f_{1},\dots,f_{\ell}\}
of
D^{\perp}
, so that we have

\mathcal{O}^{\times}/\penalty R=D\oplus D^{\perp}=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\{u_{1},\dots,u_{k}\}\oplus\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\{e_{1},\dots,e_{\ell},f_{1},\dots,f_{\ell}\}.

For each
u_{i}\in\{u_{1},\dots,u_{k}\}
let us define

\widetilde{M}_{i}:=\langle\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(u_{i})\,:\,\alpha\in\Phi\rangle\subseteq\widetilde{T}_{0}/\penalty T_{R},

namely the group generated by the
\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(u_{i})
’s viewed in
\widetilde{T}_{0}/\penalty T_{R}
. Note that by (
MR3
) we have

\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(u_{i})^{2}=(u_{i},u_{i})\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(u_{i}^{2})=(u_{i},u_{i})=-1,

because
\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(u_{i}^{2})=1
in
\widetilde{T}_{0}/\penalty T_{R}
. Hence
\mu_{2}\subseteq\widetilde{M}_{i}
.

For each
\{e_{i},f_{i}\}\subseteq\{e_{1},\dots,e_{\ell},f_{1},\dots,f_{\ell}\}
, let us define

N_{i}:=\langle\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(e_{i}),\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(f_{i})\,:\,\alpha\in\Phi\rangle\subseteq\widetilde{T}_{0}/\penalty T_{R},

namely the group generated by the
\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(e_{i})
’s and
\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(f_{i})
’s viewed in
\widetilde{T}_{0}/\penalty T_{R}
. Note that

\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(e_{i})\tilde{h}_{\beta}(f_{i})=(f_{i},e_{i}^{\langle\beta,\alpha\rangle})\,\tilde{h}_{\beta}(f_{i})\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(e_{i})

by (
MR4
). Hence if we choose
\alpha
and
\beta
so that
\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle=-1
(such
\alpha
and
\beta
exist because
G
is not of type
A_{1}
or
D_{2}
, and
\Phi
is simply-laced), we have
\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(e_{i})\tilde{h}_{\beta}(f_{i})=-\tilde{h}_{\beta}(f_{i})\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(e_{i})
. Hence
\mu_{2}\subseteq\widetilde{N}_{i}
.

Now, let us define

\widetilde{M}:=\widetilde{M}_{1}\cdots\widetilde{M}_{k}{\quad\text{and}\quad}\widetilde{N}:=\widetilde{N}_{1}\cdots\widetilde{N}_{\ell}.

By the orthogonality of the basis vectors
u_{i}
’s,
e_{i}
’s and
f_{i}
’s and relations (
MR3
) and (
MR4
), we know that the
\widetilde{M}_{i}
’s and
\widetilde{N}_{i}
’s mutually commute. Hence
\widetilde{M}
is the group generated by all the elements of the form
\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(u_{i})
viewed in
\widetilde{T}_{0}/\penalty T_{R}
and
\widetilde{N}
is the one generated by all the elements of the form
\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(e_{i})
and
\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(f_{i})
viewed in
\widetilde{T}_{0}/\penalty T_{R}
. We then have

(4.1)

1\longrightarrow(\underbrace{\mu_{2}\times\cdots\times\mu_{2}}_{\text{$k+\ell$-times}})^{\Delta}\longrightarrow\widetilde{M}_{1}\times\cdots\times\widetilde{M}_{k}\times\widetilde{N}_{1}\times\cdots\times\widetilde{N}_{\ell}\longrightarrow\widetilde{M}\cdot\widetilde{N}\longrightarrow 1,

where

(\mu_{2}\times\cdots\times\mu_{2})^{\Delta}=\{(\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{k+\ell})\,:\,\epsilon_{1}\cdots\epsilon_{k+\ell}=1\}\subseteq\mu_{2}\times\cdots\times\mu_{2}.

Further, the group
\widetilde{M}\cdot\widetilde{N}
is the group generated by all the elements
\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(u_{i})
,
\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(e_{i})
and
\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(f_{i})
, which is the image of
\widetilde{T}_{0}
in
\widetilde{T}_{0}/\penalty T_{R}
. Hence we have

(4.2)

1\longrightarrow T_{R}\longrightarrow\widetilde{T}_{0}\longrightarrow\widetilde{M}\cdot\widetilde{N}\longrightarrow 1.

The groups
T_{R}
,
\widetilde{M}_{i}
and
\widetilde{N}_{i}
are all invariant under the action of
\mathcal{W}
as follows.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
\tilde{w}\in\mathcal{W}
. Then we have

(a)

\tilde{w}T_{R}\tilde{w}^{-1}=T_{R}
, so that the conjugation action of
\mathcal{W}
on
\widetilde{T}_{0}
descends to
\widetilde{M}\cdot\widetilde{N}=\widetilde{T}_{0}/\penalty T_{R}
;

(b)

\tilde{w}\widetilde{M}_{i}\tilde{w}^{-1}=\widetilde{M}_{i}
and
\tilde{w}\widetilde{N}_{i}\tilde{w}^{-1}=\widetilde{N}_{i}
.

Proof.

We may assume that
\tilde{w}
is of the form
\tilde{w}_{\alpha}:=\tilde{w}_{\alpha}(1)
for some
\alpha\in\Phi
. For each generator
\tilde{h}_{\beta}(t)
with
t\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
, we have

\displaystyle\tilde{w}_{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\beta}(t)\tilde{w}_{\alpha}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\tilde{w}_{\alpha}(1)\tilde{h}_{\beta}(t)\tilde{w}_{\alpha}(-1)

\displaystyle=(t,c)\cdot\tilde{h}_{\gamma}(t),

where
c=c(\alpha,\beta)=\pm 1
and
\gamma=w_{\alpha}(\beta)
. Hence, if
t\in R
, then
(t,c)=1
, so
\tilde{w}T_{R}\tilde{w}^{-1}=T_{R}
. This proves (a). If
\tilde{h}_{\beta}(t)
is a generator of
\widetilde{M}_{i}
or
\widetilde{N}_{i}
, then
\tilde{w}_{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\beta}(t)\tilde{w}_{\alpha}^{-1}=(t,c)\cdot\tilde{h}_{\gamma}(t)
is also in
\widetilde{M}_{i}
or
\widetilde{N}_{i}
because
\tilde{h}_{\gamma}(t)
is also a generator of
\widetilde{M}_{i}
or
\widetilde{N}_{i}
. This proves (b).
∎

The lemma implies that for each
\tilde{w}\in\mathcal{W}
we have the isomorphisms
\operatorname{Int}(\tilde{w}):\widetilde{M}_{i}\to\widetilde{M}_{i}
and
\operatorname{Int}(\tilde{w}):\widetilde{N}_{i}\to\widetilde{N}_{i}
given by conjugation, which gives rise to the following commutative diagram

(4.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{M}_{1}\times\cdots\times\widetilde{M}_{k}\times\widetilde{N}_{1}\cdots\times\widetilde{N}_{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{M}\cdot\widetilde{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{M}_{1}\times\cdots\times\widetilde{M}_{k}\times\widetilde{N}_{1}\cdots\times\widetilde{N}_{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{M}\cdot\widetilde{N}\hbox to0.0pt{\, ,\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Int}(\tilde{w})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Int}(\tilde{w})}

where the horizontal arrows are the surjections of (
4.1
) and the vertical arrows are the conjugation by
\tilde{w}
.

4.2.
Definition

Let us now define what a pseudo-spherical representation is:

Definition 4.5
.

A
pseudo-spherical representation
of
\widetilde{T}_{0}
is an irreducible genuine representation of
\widetilde{T}_{0}
which is trivial on
T_{R}
.

Remark
.

This terminology is inspired by
[
ABP+07
,
LS10b
,
LS10a
]
.

Note that the pseudo-spherical representations of
\widetilde{T}_{0}
remain irreducible when restricted to the first congruence subgroup.

Lemma 4.6
.

The natural inclusion
\widetilde{T}_{1}\subseteq\widetilde{T}_{0}
descends to a group isomorphism

\widetilde{T}_{1}/T_{R}\cap\widetilde{T}_{1}\xrightarrow{\;\sim\;}\widetilde{T}_{0}/T_{R}.

Consequently, if
\tau
is a pseudo-spherical representation of
\widetilde{T}_{0}
, then
\tau
remains irreducible when restricted to
\widetilde{T}_{1}
.

Proof.

Recall that
\mathcal{O}^{\times}=\mathcal{O}^{\times 2}(1+\varpi\mathcal{O})
by Lemma
A.3
. Thus the natural inclusion
1+\varpi\mathcal{O}\subseteq\mathcal{O}^{\times}
descends to an isomorphism
(1+\varpi\mathcal{O})/R\cap(1+\varpi\mathcal{O})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{O}^{\times}/R
. The result follows.
∎

4.3.
A general construction

With the short exact sequence (
4.2
) in mind, to construct a pseudo-spherical representation of
\widetilde{T}_{0}
we have to construct a genuine irreducible representation for each
\widetilde{M}_{i}
and
\widetilde{N}_{i}
. For this purpose, let us start with the following general lemma.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
H\subseteq G
be a compact abelian subgroup, and
\widetilde{H}
its preimage in
\widetilde{G}
, so that we have

1\longrightarrow\mu_{2}\longrightarrow\widetilde{H}\longrightarrow H\longrightarrow 1.

Let
\operatorname{Irr}_{gen}(\widetilde{H})
be the set of (equivalence classes of) finite dimensional irreducible genuine representations of
\widetilde{H}
and
\operatorname{Irr}_{gen}(Z(\widetilde{H}))
the set of genuine characters on the center
Z(\widetilde{H})
. Then there is a bijection

\operatorname{Irr}_{gen}(Z(\widetilde{H}))\xrightarrow{\;\sim\;}\operatorname{Irr}_{gen}(\widetilde{H}),\quad\chi\mapsto\pi(\chi),

where
\pi(\chi)
is the unique constituent of the isotypic representation
\operatorname{Ind}_{Z(\widetilde{H})}^{\widetilde{H}}\chi
.

Moreover, we have

\operatorname{Ind}_{Z(\widetilde{H})}^{\widetilde{H}}\chi=n\pi(\chi),

where

n=\dim\pi(\chi)=\left|\widetilde{H}/\penalty Z(\widetilde{H})\right|^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Proof.

This is essentially
[
ABP+07
, Proposition 2.2, p.706]
except that the group
H
there is an abelian Lie group with
H/\penalty H^{\circ}
finite. Their proof works for our setting. But, to show
\operatorname{Ind}_{Z(\widetilde{H})}^{\widetilde{H}}\chi
is completely reducible, one has to use that
\widetilde{H}
is compact. The details are left to the reader.
∎

Also if
\mathcal{W}
acts on
\widetilde{H}
through automorphisms, then
\mathcal{W}
also acts on
Z(\widetilde{H})
. Then for each
\tilde{w}\in\mathcal{W}
we have

{}^{\tilde{w}}\left(\operatorname{Ind}_{Z(\widetilde{H})}^{\widetilde{H}}\chi\right)=\operatorname{Ind}_{Z(\widetilde{H})}^{\widetilde{H}}({{}^{\tilde{w}}\chi}).

Hence the bijection of the lemma restricts to the bijection

(4.8)

(\operatorname{Irr}_{gen}Z(\widetilde{H}))^{\mathcal{W}}\xrightarrow{\;\sim\;}(\operatorname{Irr}_{gen}\widetilde{H})^{\mathcal{W}},

where
(\operatorname{Irr}_{gen}Z(\widetilde{H}))^{\mathcal{W}}
is the set of all
\mathcal{W}
-invariant genuine characters on
Z(\widetilde{H})
and
(\operatorname{Irr}_{gen}\widetilde{H})^{\mathcal{W}}
is the set of (equivalence classes of)
\mathcal{W}
-invariant genuine irreducible representations of
\widetilde{H}
.

4.4.
Representations of
\widetilde{M}_{i}

Let us construct (finite dimensional) irreducible genuine representations of
\widetilde{M}_{i}
and show that they are all
\mathcal{W}
-invariant. For this purpose, we describe the center
Z(\widetilde{M}_{i})
concretely as follows.

Lemma 4.9
.

We have a bijection

\mu_{2}\times\widetilde{Y}/\penalty 2Y\longrightarrow Z(\widetilde{M}_{i})

given by

(\epsilon,\,\sum_{\alpha\in\Delta}c_{\alpha}{\check{\alpha}})\mapsto\epsilon\prod_{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(u_{i}^{c_{\alpha}}),

where the product is independent of the ordering. (Note that this does not have to be a group isomorphism.)

Proof.

In this proof, we omit the subscript
i
for
\widetilde{M}_{i}
and
u_{i}
, so we write
\widetilde{M}=\widetilde{M}_{i}
and
u=u_{i}
. Also we write
\sum_{\alpha\in\Delta}c_{\alpha}{\check{\alpha}}=\sum_{i}c_{i}{\check{\alpha}}_{i}
, so that
\prod_{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(u_{i}^{c_{\alpha}})
is
\prod_{i}\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(u^{c_{i}})
.

Note that since
\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(u^{2})=1
, the map is independent of the representative
\sum_{i}c_{i}{\check{\alpha}}_{i}
.

Let us show that
\epsilon\prod_{i}\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(u^{c_{i}})
is indeed in
Z(\widetilde{M})
. Let
\tilde{h}_{\beta}(u)\in\widetilde{M}
, where
\beta\in\Phi
. Then for each
{\check{\alpha}}_{i}
we have

\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(u^{c_{i}})\tilde{h}_{\beta}(u)=(u,u^{\langle\beta,{\check{\alpha}}_{i}\rangle})\tilde{h}_{\beta}(u)\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(u^{c_{i}})=\tilde{h}_{\beta}(u)\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(u^{c_{i}})

by (
MR4
), where we used
\langle\beta,{\check{\alpha}}_{i}\rangle\in 2\mathbb{Z}
. Hence

\left(\prod_{i}\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(u^{c_{i}})\right)\tilde{h}_{\beta}(u)=\tilde{h}_{\beta}(u)\left(\prod_{i}\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(u^{c_{i}})\right),

so
\epsilon\prod_{i}\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(u^{c_{i}})
is indeed in
Z(\widetilde{M})
.

To show that the map is surjective, note that each element in
\widetilde{M}
is (not necessarily uniquely) written as

\epsilon\prod_{i}\tilde{h}(u^{d_{i}})

for some
\sum_{i}d_{i}{\check{\alpha}}_{i}\in Y
. If this element is in the center
Z(\widetilde{M})
, it has to commute with all the
\tilde{h}_{\beta}(u)
. But by the same computation as above, we have

\prod_{i}\tilde{h}(u^{d_{i}})\cdot\tilde{h}_{\beta}(u)=(u,u^{\langle\beta,\;\sum_{i}d_{i}{\check{\alpha}}_{i}\rangle})\,\tilde{h}_{\beta}(u)\cdot\prod_{i}\tilde{h}(u^{d_{i}}).

Hence we must have
\sum_{i}d_{i}{\check{\alpha}}_{i}\in\widetilde{Y}
because
(u,u)=-1
. Hence the given map is surjective.

To show it is injective, compose the map with
Z(\widetilde{M})/\penalty\mu_{2}
. This is a homomorphism whose kernel is
\mu_{2}
. This shows that the given map is injective.
∎

Once we can describe the center
Z(\widetilde{M}_{i})
, we can show

Lemma 4.10
.

The group
\mathcal{W}
acts trivially on
Z(\widetilde{M}_{i})
.

Proof.

In this proof, we omit the subscript
i
for
\widetilde{M}_{i}
and
u_{i}
, so we write
\widetilde{M}=\widetilde{M}_{i}
and
u=u_{i}
. Also we write
\sum_{\alpha\in\Delta}c_{\alpha}{\check{\alpha}}=\sum_{i}c_{i}{\check{\alpha}}_{i}
.

To show the lemma, it suffices to show that for each simple root
\alpha_{j}
we have

\tilde{w}_{\alpha_{j}}(1)\prod_{i}\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(u^{c_{i}})\tilde{w}_{\alpha_{j}}(-1)=\prod_{i}\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(u^{c_{i}}).

To work this out, let us recall from (
MR5
) and (
MR5’
) that we have

\tilde{w}_{\alpha}(1)\tilde{h}_{\beta}(t)\tilde{w}_{\alpha}(-1)=\begin{cases}\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(t^{-1})&\text{if $\beta=\alpha$};\\
\tilde{h}_{\beta}(t)\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(t)&\text{if $\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle=-1$};\\
\tilde{h}_{\beta}(t)&\text{if $\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle=0$},\end{cases}

for all
t\in F^{\times}
. Also noting
\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(u^{2})=1\pmod{T_{R}}
, we always have

\tilde{w}_{\alpha}(1)\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(u^{c})\tilde{w}_{\alpha}(-1)=\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(u^{c})

for any
c\in\mathbb{Z}
and any
\alpha\in\Phi
. Also note that if
c=d\pmod{2}
then
\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(u^{c})=\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(u^{d})\mod{T_{R}}
.

Assume that
\alpha_{j}
is adjacent to exactly one simple root
\alpha_{k}
, namely
\alpha_{j}
is an endpoint of the Dynkin diagram. We then know from Lemma
3.6
that
c_{k}\in 2\mathbb{Z}
. We can then write

\displaystyle\tilde{w}_{\alpha_{j}}(1)\prod_{i}\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(u^{c_{i}})\tilde{w}_{\alpha_{j}}(-1)

\displaystyle=\left(\tilde{w}_{\alpha_{j}}(1)\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{k}}(u^{c_{k}})\tilde{w}_{\alpha_{j}}(-1)\right)\left(\tilde{w}_{\alpha_{j}}(1)\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{j}}(u^{c_{j}})\tilde{w}_{\alpha_{j}}(-1)\right)\prod_{i\neq j,k}\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(u^{c_{i}})

\displaystyle=\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{k}}(u^{c_{k}})\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{j}}(u^{c_{k}})\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{j}}(u^{c_{j}})\prod_{i\neq j,k}\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(u^{c_{i}})

\displaystyle=\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{k}}(u^{c_{k}})\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{j}}(u^{c_{j}})\prod_{i\neq j,k}\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(u^{c_{i}})

\displaystyle=\prod_{i}\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(u^{c_{i}}),

where we used
\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{j}}(u^{c_{k}})=1
.

Next, assume that
\alpha_{j}
is adjacent to exactly two roots
\alpha_{k}
and
\alpha_{\ell}
. By the analogous computation, we have

\displaystyle\tilde{w}_{\alpha_{j}}(1)\prod_{i}\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(u^{c_{\alpha}})\tilde{w}_{\alpha_{j}}(-1)

\displaystyle=\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{k}}(u^{c_{k}})\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{j}}(u^{c_{k}})\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{\ell}}(u^{c_{\ell}})\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{j}}(u^{c_{\ell}})\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{j}}(u^{c_{j}})\prod_{i\neq j,k,\ell}\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(u^{c_{i}}).

Here, by moving round
\tilde{h}
’s, we can further write

\displaystyle\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{k}}(u^{c_{k}})\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{j}}(u^{c_{k}})\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{\ell}}(u^{c_{\ell}})\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{j}}(u^{c_{\ell}})\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{j}}(u^{c_{j}})

\displaystyle=(u^{c_{k}},u^{c_{\ell}})\,\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{k}}(u^{c_{k}})\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{\ell}}(u^{c_{\ell}})\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{j}}(u^{c_{k}})\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{j}}(u^{c_{\ell}})\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{j}}(u^{c_{j}})

\displaystyle=(u^{c_{k}},u^{c_{\ell}})\,(u^{c_{k}},u^{c_{\ell}})\,\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{k}}(u^{c_{k}})\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{\ell}}(u^{c_{\ell}})\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{j}}(u^{c_{k}+c_{\ell}})\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{j}}(u^{c_{j}})

\displaystyle=(u^{c_{k}+c_{\ell}},u^{c_{j}})\,\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{k}}(u^{c_{k}})\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{\ell}}(u^{c_{\ell}})\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{j}}(u^{c_{k}+c_{\ell}+c_{j}})

\displaystyle=(u,u)^{c_{k}c_{j}+c_{\ell}c_{j}}\,\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{k}}(u^{c_{k}})\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{\ell}}(u^{c_{\ell}})\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{j}}(u^{c_{k}+c_{\ell}+c_{j}})

\displaystyle=\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{k}}(u^{c_{k}})\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{\ell}}(u^{c_{\ell}})\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{j}}(u^{c_{j}}),

where for the last step we used that
c_{k}c_{j},c_{\ell}c_{j}\in 2\mathbb{Z}
by Corollary
3.8
, and
c_{k}+c_{\ell}\in 2\mathbb{Z}
by Lemma
3.6
.

Finally, if
\alpha_{k}
has three adjacent roots, then the calculation is essentially the same and left to the reader.
∎

We then have

Proposition 4.11
.

Every irreducible genuine representation of
\widetilde{M}_{i}
is
\mathcal{W}
-invariant and

\left|\operatorname{Irr}_{gen}Z(\widetilde{M}_{i})\right|=\left|\widetilde{Y}/\penalty 2Y\right|.

Further, for each
\pi\in\operatorname{Irr}_{gen}(\widetilde{M}_{i})
, we have

\dim\pi=\left|\widetilde{M}_{i}/\penalty Z(\widetilde{M}_{i})\right|^{\frac{1}{2}}=\left|Y/\penalty\widetilde{Y}\right|^{\frac{1}{2}},

where the cardinality
\left|Y/\penalty\widetilde{Y}\right|
is listed in Table
1
.

Proof.

This follows from Lemma
4.7
and (
4.8
).
∎

Remark
.

When
F=\mathbb{R}
, the above proposition is proven in
[
ABP+07
]
, where the corresponding group is denoted by
\widetilde{M}
. One can actually show that our
\widetilde{M}_{i}
is isomorphic to their
\widetilde{M}
. In
[
Kar21
]
, the corresponding group is denoted by
\widetilde{T}^{\diamond}
, and a
\mathcal{W}
-invariant representation is constructed by constructing an isomorphism between
\widetilde{M}
and
\widetilde{T}^{\diamond}
. One can readily verify that our
\widetilde{M}_{i}
is isomorphic to
\widetilde{T}^{\diamond}
of
[
Kar21
]
via the map
\widetilde{M}_{i}\to\widetilde{T}^{\diamond}
,
\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(u_{i})\mapsto\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(-1)
.

4.5.
Representations of
\widetilde{N}_{i}

We can construct pseudo-spherical representations of
\widetilde{N}_{i}
and show that they are all
\mathcal{W}
-invariant essentially in the same way.

Let us first mention

Lemma 4.12
.

The map

\mu_{2}\times\widetilde{Y}/\penalty 2Y\times\widetilde{Y}/\penalty 2Y\longrightarrow Z(\widetilde{N}_{i})

given by

(\epsilon,y_{1},y_{2})\mapsto\epsilon\,\tilde{y}_{1}(e_{i})\,\tilde{y}_{2}(f_{i})

is an isomorphism. Further, the group
\mathcal{W}
acts trivially on
Z(\widetilde{N}_{i})
.

Proof.

Let us omit the subscript
i
from the notation for
\widetilde{N}_{i}
, so
\{e,f\}
is a symplectic basis of a 2-dimensional subspace in
\mathcal{O}^{\times}/\penalty R
such that
(e,f)=-1
and
(e,e)=(f,f)=1
, and
\widetilde{N}
is the group generated by
\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(e)
and
\tilde{h}_{\beta}(f)
for all
\alpha\in\Phi
. Now, since
(e,e)=(f,f)=1
, we know from (MR4) that
\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(e)\tilde{h}_{\beta}(e)=\tilde{h}_{\beta}(e)\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(e)
and
\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(f)\tilde{h}_{\beta}(f)=\tilde{h}_{\beta}(f)\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(f)
for all
\alpha,\beta\in\Phi
. Hence each element in
\widetilde{N}
is uniquely written as

\epsilon\,\tilde{y}_{1}(e)\,\tilde{y}_{2}(f)=\epsilon\cdot\prod_{i}\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(e^{c_{i}})\cdot\prod_{i}\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(f^{d_{i}}),

where
y_{1}=\sum_{i}c_{i}\alpha_{i}
and
y_{2}=\sum_{i}d_{i}\alpha_{i}
.

Assume
\epsilon\,\tilde{y}_{1}(e)\,\tilde{y}_{2}(f)\in Z(\widetilde{N})
. Then it commutes with all the elements of the form
\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(f)
. But by (MR4) we know that

\epsilon\,\tilde{y}_{1}(e)\,\tilde{y}_{2}(f)\,\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(f)=(e,f^{\langle\alpha,y_{1}\rangle})\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(f)\cdot\epsilon\,\tilde{y}_{1}(e)\,\tilde{y}_{2}(f).

Hence we must have
\langle\alpha,y_{1}\rangle\in 2\mathbb{Z}
for all
\alpha\in\Phi
, which implies
y_{1}\in\widetilde{Y}
. Similarly, we have
y_{2}\in\widetilde{Y}
. If
y_{1},y_{2}\in\widetilde{Y}
then we already know that both
\tilde{y}_{1}(e)
and
\tilde{y}_{2}(f)
are in
Z(\widetilde{T})
and hence in
Z(\widetilde{N})
. Hence we have the surjective map

\mu_{2}\times\widetilde{Y}\times\widetilde{Y}\longrightarrow Z(\widetilde{N}),\quad(\epsilon,y_{1},y_{2})\mapsto\epsilon\,\tilde{y}_{1}(e)\,\tilde{y}_{2}(f).

To show this is a homomorphism, notice that for each
(\epsilon,y_{1},y_{2})
and
(\epsilon^{\prime},z_{1},z_{2})
, we know

\epsilon\,\tilde{y}_{1}(e)\,\tilde{y}_{2}(f)\cdot\epsilon^{\prime}\,\tilde{z}_{1}(e)\,\tilde{z}_{2}(f)=\epsilon\epsilon^{\prime}\,\tilde{y}_{1}(e)\tilde{z}_{1}(e)\,\tilde{y}_{2}(f)\tilde{z}_{2}(f),

because
\tilde{y}_{2}(f)\in Z(\widetilde{N})
. We then have
\tilde{y}_{1}(e)\tilde{z}_{1}(e)=\widetilde{y_{1}+z_{1}}(e)
because
(e,e)=1
and so all the
\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(e)
’s commute with each other, and similarly we have
\tilde{y}_{2}(f)\tilde{z}_{2}(f)=\widetilde{y_{2}+z_{2}}(f)
.

We need to show that the kernel of this map is
\{1\}\times 2Y\times 2Y
. Let
(1,y_{1},y_{2})
be in the kernel. Suppose
y_{1}=\sum_{i}c_{i}\alpha_{i}
. Then
\prod_{i}\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(e^{c_{i}})=1
, which implies
\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(e^{c_{i}})=1
for each
i
. Then we must have
e^{c_{i}}\in R
, which implies
c_{i}\in 2\mathbb{Z}
because
e\notin R
. Hence
y_{1}\in 2Y
. Similarly, we have
y_{2}\in 2Y
. Hence the kernel is
\{1\}\times 2Y\times 2Y
.

It remains to show that
\mathcal{W}
acts trivially on
Z(\widetilde{N})
. This can be proven in the same way as
\widetilde{M}_{i}
; the proof is indeed simpler because we always have
(e,e)=(f,f)=1
, and hence all the Hilbert symbols in the calculation for the
\widetilde{M}_{i}
case will automatically become
1
. The detail is left to the reader.
∎

We then have

Proposition 4.13
.

Every irreducible genuine representation of
\widetilde{N}_{i}
is
\mathcal{W}
-invariant and

\left|\operatorname{Irr}_{gen}Z(\widetilde{N}_{i})\right|=\left|\widetilde{Y}/\penalty 2Y\right|^{2}.

Further, for each
\pi\in\operatorname{Irr}_{gen}(\widetilde{N}_{i})
, we have

\dim\pi=\left|\widetilde{N}_{i}/\penalty Z(\widetilde{N}_{i})\right|=\left|Y/\penalty\widetilde{Y}\right|,

where the cardinality
\left|Y/\penalty\widetilde{Y}\right|
is listed in Table
1
.

Proof.

This follows from the above lemma, Lemma
4.7
, and (
4.8
).
∎

4.6.
Construction of pseudo-spherical representations

By putting together genuine representations of
\widetilde{M}_{i}
’s and
\widetilde{N}_{i}
’s, one can construct a genuine representation of
\widetilde{M}\cdot\widetilde{N}
as follows. Let
\delta_{i}
be a genuine representation of
\widetilde{M}_{i}
and
\tau_{i}
one of
\widetilde{N}_{i}
. Then consider the tensor product representation

\delta_{1}\otimes\cdots\delta_{k}\otimes\tau_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\tau_{\ell}

of the direct product

\widetilde{M}_{1}\times\cdots\times\widetilde{M}_{k}\times\widetilde{N}_{1}\times\cdots\times\widetilde{N}_{\ell}.

Since all the
\delta_{i}
’s and
\tau_{i}
’s are genuine, this tensor product representation is trivial on the kernel of the surjection

\widetilde{M}_{1}\times\cdots\times\widetilde{M}_{k}\times\widetilde{N}_{1}\times\cdots\times\widetilde{N}_{\ell}\longrightarrow\widetilde{M}\cdot\widetilde{N}

of (
4.1
), descending to a representation
\tau
of
\widetilde{M}\cdot\widetilde{N}
. Conversely, every genuine irreducible representation of
\widetilde{M}\cdot\widetilde{N}
arises in this way.

Assume
\delta_{i}
and
\tau_{i}
are all irreducible, so that they are all
\mathcal{W}
-invariant. Then
\tau
is also
\mathcal{W}
-invariant by (
4.4
). By pulling back
\tau
to a representation of
\widetilde{T}_{0}
via the surjection

\widetilde{T}_{0}\longrightarrow\widetilde{M}\cdot\widetilde{N}=\widetilde{T}_{0}/\penalty T_{R},

we obtain an irreducible genuine representation of
\widetilde{T}_{0}
trivial on
T_{R}
(
i.e.
a pseudo-spherical representation of
\widetilde{T}_{0}
). Since
T_{R}
is invariant under
\mathcal{W}
, the pseudo-spherical representation is
\mathcal{W}
-invariant. We denote this representation of
\widetilde{T}_{0}
by
\tau
.

To summarize, we have proven

Theorem 4.14
.

There exists a pseudo-spherical representation of
\widetilde{T}_{0}
. Moreover, every pseudo-spherical representation
\tau
is
\mathcal{W}
-invariant, namely
{{}^{\tilde{w}}\tau}\cong\tau
for all
\tilde{w}\in\mathcal{W}
.

4.7.
Dimension and number of pseudo-spherical representations

The dimension of each pseudo-spherical representation
\tau
is computed as follows.

Proposition 4.15
.

Let
\tau
be a pseudo-spherical representation. Then

\dim\tau=\left|Y/\penalty\widetilde{Y}\right|^{\frac{ef}{2}},

where the cardinality
\left|Y/\penalty\widetilde{Y}\right|
is as in Table
1
.

Proof.

From Propositions
4.11
and
4.13
, we already know

\dim\delta_{i}=\left|Y/\penalty\widetilde{Y}\right|^{\frac{1}{2}}{\quad\text{and}\quad}\dim\tau_{i}=\left|Y/\penalty\widetilde{Y}\right|

for each
\delta_{i}\in\operatorname{Irr}_{gen}(\widetilde{M}_{i})
and
\tau_{i}\in\operatorname{Irr}_{gen}(\widetilde{N}_{i})
. Hence to compute
\dim\tau
, we have to compute the number of
\widetilde{M}_{i}
’s and
\widetilde{N}_{i}
’s.

We know from Proposition
2.2
that

[\mathcal{O}^{\times}:R]=2^{ef}.

So, if we let
m
be the number of
\widetilde{M}_{i}
’s and
n
the number of
\widetilde{N}_{i}
’s, then
ef=m+2n
. Hence

\dim\tau=(\dim\delta_{i})^{m}\cdot(\dim\tau_{i})^{n}=\left|Y/\penalty\widetilde{Y}\right|^{\frac{m+2n}{2}}=\left|Y/\penalty\widetilde{Y}\right|^{\frac{ef}{2}}.

∎

Similarly, we have

Proposition 4.16
.

The number of the pseudo-spherical representations of
\widetilde{T}_{0}
is
\left|\widetilde{Y}/\penalty 2Y\right|^{ef}
.

Proof.

We know that

\left|\operatorname{Irr}_{gen}(\widetilde{M}_{i})\right|=\left|\widetilde{Y}/\penalty 2Y\right|{\quad\text{and}\quad}\left|\operatorname{Irr}_{gen}(\widetilde{N}_{i})\right|=\left|\widetilde{Y}/\penalty 2Y\right|^{2}.

One can then prove the proposition in the same way as the previous proposition.
∎

The dimension and the number of the pseudo-spherical representations of
\widetilde{T}_{0}
is summarized in Table
2
, where
A_{1}
and
D_{2}
are excluded.

4.8.
Branching

For later purposes, we need the following.

Proposition 4.17
.

Suppose that
\Phi
is a root system of type
A_{r}
or of type
D_{r}
. Let
\Phi^{\prime}\subseteq\Phi
be a subroot system of rank
r-1
and of the same type as
\Phi
. Let
\widetilde{T}_{0}^{\prime}\subseteq\widetilde{T}_{0}
be the normal subgroup generated by the elements
\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(u)
, where
u\in\mathcal{O}
and
\alpha\in\Phi^{\prime}
.

(1)

For a pseudo-spherical representation
\tau
of
\widetilde{T}_{0}
, the restriction
\tau|_{\widetilde{T}_{0}^{\prime}}
to
\widetilde{T}_{0}^{\prime}
is a multiplicity-free direct sum of pseudo-spherical representations of
\widetilde{T}_{0}^{\prime}
.

(2)

If
\tau^{\prime}
is a pseudo-spherical representation of
\widetilde{T}_{0}^{\prime}
, then there exists a pseudo-spherical representation
\tau
of
\widetilde{T}_{0}
such that
\tau^{\prime}\subseteq\tau|_{\widetilde{T}_{0}}
.

Proof.

The restriction
\tau|_{\widetilde{T}_{0}^{\prime}}
is a direct sum of pseudo-spherical representations of
\widetilde{T}_{0}^{\prime}

by definition
because
T_{R}^{\prime}\subseteq T_{R}
. Now we will break the proof of (1) up into cases.

First suppose that
\Phi
is of type
A_{2r+1}
or
D_{2r}
. From the dimension column of Table
2
, we see that this restriction to
\widetilde{T}_{0}^{\prime}
must remain irreducible. Thus the result follows in this case.

Second, suppose that
\Phi
is of type
A_{2r}
or
D_{2r+1}
. Let
\Delta\subseteq\Phi
be a set of simple roots. Then
\Delta^{\prime}:=\Delta\cap\Phi^{\prime}
is a set of simple roots in
\Phi^{\prime}
. Let
\alpha\in\Delta\smallsetminus\Delta^{\prime}
. Let
\widetilde{T}_{\alpha,0}\subseteq\widetilde{T}_{0}
be the subgroup generated by the elements of the form
\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(u)
, where
u\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
. Note that
\widetilde{T}_{\alpha,0}
is a normal subgroup of
\widetilde{T}_{0}
. By definition the restriction of
\tau
to
\widetilde{T}_{\alpha,0}
factors through the finite abelian group
\widetilde{T}_{\alpha,0}/(\widetilde{T}_{\alpha,0}\cap T_{R})
, which has size
2[\mathcal{O}^{\times}:R]=2^{ef+1}
.

Let
\tau^{\prime}
be a pseudo-spherical representation of
\widetilde{T}_{0}^{\prime}
. By a direct computation with the central character it follows that
\tau^{\prime}\cong\,^{\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(u)}\tau^{\prime}
if and only if
u\in R
. From this it follows that the restriction
\tau|_{\widetilde{T}_{0}^{\prime}}
must contain at least
[\mathcal{O}:R]=2^{ef}
distinct pseudo-spherical
\widetilde{T}_{0}^{\prime}
-modules. By comparing the dimensions in Table
2
it follows that the restriction of
\tau
to
\widetilde{T}_{0}^{\prime}
is a multiplicity-free direct sum of pseudo-spherical representations of
\widetilde{T}_{0}^{\prime}
.
Item (1) is proven.

To prove (2), let
\tau^{\prime}
be a pseudo-spherical representation of
\widetilde{T}^{\prime}_{0}
. Since
T_{R}^{\prime}=\widetilde{T}_{0}^{\prime}\cap T_{R}
acts trivially and
T_{R}
commutes with
\widetilde{T}_{0}^{\prime}
pointwise, we can extend
\tau^{\prime}
to a representation of
T_{R}\widetilde{T}_{0}^{\prime}
by letting
T_{R}
act trivially. Consider
\operatorname{Ind}_{T_{R}\widetilde{T}_{0}^{\prime}}^{\widetilde{T}_{0}}\tau^{\prime}
. Since
T_{R}
is in the center of
\widetilde{T}_{0}
, one can see that
T_{R}
is in the kernel of this induced representation. Hence any irreducible constituent of this induced representation is pseudo-spherical and contains
\tau^{\prime}
.
∎

4.9.
Square roots of
\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(-1)

In this subsection we record a few identities in the group algebra
\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{T}_{0}]
and their relation to each pseudo-spherical representation
\tau
of
\widetilde{T}_{0}
. In particular, we will compute a square root of
\tau(\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(-1))
. Later these will be important to our investigation of representations of
\widetilde{I}
. Also in this subsection, we set

(4.18)

U_{k}:=1+\varpi^{k}\mathcal{O}

for
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
with the convention that
U_{0}=\mathcal{O}
.

Let
\alpha\in\Phi
,
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
\chi\in\operatorname{Hom}(U_{j}/(U_{j}\cap R),\mathbb{C}^{\times})
. Define

X_{\alpha,\chi,j}:=\frac{1}{\left|U_{j}/U_{2e}\right|}\sum_{v\in U_{j}/U_{2e}}\chi(v)\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(v).

If
\chi
is such that
\chi(-)=(-,u)
for some
u\in F^{\times}
, then we write

(4.19)

X_{\alpha,u,j}:=X_{\alpha,\chi,j}.

Lemma 4.20
.

X_{-\alpha,u,j}=X_{\alpha,-u,j}

Proof.

This follows because
\tilde{h}_{-\alpha}(v)=(v,-1)\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(v^{-1})
.
∎

The next lemma shows that the elements
X_{\alpha,u,j}
define nontrivial endomorphisms of
\tau
.

Lemma 4.21
.

Let
\tau
be a pseudo-spherical representation of
\widetilde{T}_{0}
. Then

\operatorname{Tr}(\tau(X_{\alpha,u,j}))=|(U_{j}\cap R)/U_{j}|\dim\tau\neq 0.

Proof.

For
t\in\widetilde{T}_{0}
, we have
\operatorname{Tr}(\tau(t))\neq 0
if and only if
t\in Z(\widetilde{T}_{0})
. (This follows because if
t\in\widetilde{T}_{0}\smallsetminus Z(\widetilde{T}_{0})
, then there exists
s\in\widetilde{T}_{0}
such that
sts^{-1}=-t
, so
\operatorname{Tr}(\tau(sts^{-1}))=-\operatorname{Tr}(\tau(t))
.) The result now follows because
\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(v)\in Z(\widetilde{T}_{0})
if and only if
v\in R
. (Here we use that
\Phi
is simply-laced and not of type
A_{1}
or
D_{2}
.)
∎

Lemma 4.22
.

If
j=0
or
1
, then

X_{\alpha,\chi,j}^{2}=\chi(-1)|U_{j}/U_{2e}|^{-1}\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(-1)\sum_{v\in U_{j}\cap R/U_{2e}}\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(v).

Proof.

This follows by a direct calculation:

\displaystyle\left|U_{j}/U_{2e}\right|^{2}X_{\alpha,\chi,j}^{2}=

\displaystyle\sum_{v,w\in U_{j}/U_{2e}}\chi(vw)\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(v)\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{v,w}\chi(vw)(v,w)\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(vw)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{v,w}\chi(v)(vw^{-1},w)\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{v}\chi(v)\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(v)\sum_{w}(-v,w),

where we used
(vw^{-1},w)=(vw,w)=(v,w)(w,w)=(v,w)(-1,w)=(-v,w)
.

Because
j=0,1
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{v}\chi(v)\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(v)\sum_{w}(-v,w)=

\displaystyle\chi(-1)|U_{j}/U_{2e}|\sum_{-v\in U_{j}\cap R/U_{2e}}\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\chi(-1)|U_{j}/U_{2e}|\sum_{v\in U_{j}\cap R/U_{2e}}\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(-v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\chi(-1)|U_{j}/U_{2e}|\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(-1)\sum_{v\in U_{j}\cap R/U_{2e}}\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(v).

The lemma follows.
∎

Corollary 4.23
.

Let
\alpha\in\Phi
,
\chi\in\operatorname{Hom}(U_{j}/U_{j}\cap R,\mathbb{C}^{\times})
, and
j=0,1
. If
\tau
is a pseudo-spherical representation, then

\tau(X_{\alpha,\chi,j}^{2})=\chi(-1)|U_{j}\cap R/U_{j}|\tau(\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(-1)).

In particular,
\tau(X_{\alpha,\chi,j})
is invertible.

Remark
.

For
\alpha\in\Delta
, the element
\tau(X_{\alpha,1,1})
is proportional to the extension of the pseudo-spherical
\widetilde{T}
-module
\tau
to the element
w_{\alpha}(-1)\in\mathcal{W}
.

4.10.
Representation theory of
\widetilde{T}

Let
\tau
be a pseudo-spherical representation of
\widetilde{T}_{0}
. In this subsection, we will show that the pair
(\widetilde{T}_{0},\tau)
is a type of
\widetilde{T}
in the sense of Bushnell-Kutzko
[
BK98
, (4.1) Definition]
. For this purpose, we will construct a family of irreducible representations of
\widetilde{T}
which contain the pseudo-spherical representation.

First, we need to extend
\tau
to an irreducible representation of
Z(\widetilde{T})\widetilde{T}_{0}
by adding a central character. Namely, we need a character on
Z(\widetilde{T})
which agrees with
\tau
on
Z(\widetilde{T})\cap\widetilde{T}_{0}
. This can be done by using the following.

Lemma 4.24
.

We have the short exact sequence

1\longrightarrow\mu_{2}\xrightarrow{\;\Delta\;}\mu_{2}\widetilde{Y}(\varpi)\times\widetilde{T}_{0}\longrightarrow Z(\widetilde{T})\widetilde{T}_{0}\longrightarrow 1,

where the injection embeds
\mu_{2}
diagonally and the surjection is given by multiplication.

Proof.

One can readily see that
\widetilde{Y}(\varpi)\subseteq Z(\widetilde{T})
, so we have the map
\mu_{2}\widetilde{Y}(\varpi)\times\widetilde{T}_{0}\to Z(\widetilde{T})\widetilde{T}_{0}
. To show that this map is surjective, it suffices to show that each
z\in Z(\widetilde{T})
is written as
z=\epsilon y(\varpi)t_{0}
for some
\epsilon\in\mu_{2}
,
y\in\widetilde{Y}
and
t_{0}\in\widetilde{T}_{0}
. But we may assume
z
is of the form
\prod_{i}\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(t_{i})
, where the order of the product is fixed. For each
i
we can write
t_{i}=\varpi^{k_{i}}a_{i}
for some
k_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
a_{i}\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
, so

\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(t_{i})=(\varpi^{k_{i}},a_{i})\cdot\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(\varpi^{k_{i}})\cdot\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(a_{i}).

Let us set
y=\sum_{i}k_{i}{\check{\alpha}}_{i}
. Then by taking the product over
i
, we have

\prod_{i}\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(t_{i})=\epsilon\cdot\prod_{i}\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(\varpi^{k_{i}})\cdot\prod_{i}\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(a_{i})=\epsilon\cdot y(\varpi)\cdot\prod_{i}\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(a_{i})

for some
\epsilon\in\mu_{2}
. Thus it suffices to show
y\in\widetilde{Y}
.

Note that by Corollary
2.3
, there exists
r\in R
such that
(r,a_{i})=1
for all
i
and
(r,\varpi)=-1
. Then for each
\beta\in\Phi
, we have

\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(\varpi^{k_{i}})\cdot\tilde{h}_{\beta}(r)=(r,\varpi^{k_{i}\langle\alpha_{i},\beta\rangle})\cdot\tilde{h}_{\beta}(r)\cdot\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(\varpi^{k_{i}}){\quad\text{and}\quad}\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(a_{i})\cdot\tilde{h}_{\beta}(r)=\tilde{h}_{\beta}(r)\cdot\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(a_{i}).

Hence for each
i
, we have

\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(t_{i})\cdot\tilde{h}_{\beta}(r)=(r,\varpi^{k_{i}\langle\alpha_{i},\beta\rangle})\cdot\tilde{h}_{\beta}(r)\cdot\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(t_{i}).

Thus we have

\left(\prod_{i}\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(t_{i})\right)\cdot\tilde{h}_{\beta}(r)=(r,\varpi^{\sum_{i}k_{i}\langle\alpha_{i},\beta\rangle})\cdot\tilde{h}_{\beta}(r)\cdot\left(\prod_{i}\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(t_{i})\right).

Note that

(r,\varpi^{\sum_{i}k_{i}\langle\alpha_{i},\;\beta\rangle})=(r,\varpi)^{\sum_{i}k_{i}\langle\alpha_{i},\;\beta\rangle}=(-1)^{\sum_{i}k_{i}\langle\alpha_{i},\;\beta\rangle}=(-1)^{\langle\beta,\;y\rangle}.

Hence in order for
\prod_{i}\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(t_{i})
to be in
Z(\widetilde{T})
, we must have
\langle\beta,\;y\rangle\in 2\mathbb{Z}
for all
\beta\in\Phi
. Therefore we have
y\in\widetilde{Y}
by the definition (
3.5
) of
\widetilde{Y}
. Hence the map is surjective.

To show that the kernel is the diagonal
\mu_{2}
, let
(\epsilon\tilde{y}(\varpi),t)\in(\mu_{2}\widetilde{Y}(\varpi))\times\widetilde{T}_{0}
be such that
\epsilon\tilde{y}(\varpi)t=1
. Then
t=\epsilon\tilde{y}(\varpi)^{-1}
. But
t\in\widetilde{T}_{0}
is uniquely written as

t=\epsilon^{\prime}\prod_{i}\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{i}}(t_{i})\quad\text{for some $t_{i}\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}$}.

Hence we must have
y=0
, which gives
t=\epsilon
. Hence the kernel is the diagonal
\mu_{2}
.
∎

Let

\eta:\mu_{2}\widetilde{Y}(\varpi)\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}

be a genuine
W
-invariant character. (For example, one can restrict the character of
Z(\widetilde{T})
constructed in
[
Sav04
, page 118]
or
[
GG18
, §7]
.) Consider the representation
\eta\otimes\tau
of
\mu_{2}\widetilde{Y}(\varpi)\times\widetilde{T}_{0}
. Since both
\eta
and
\tau
are genuine, the diagonal
\mu_{2}
acts trivially, which allows

\tau_{\eta}:=\eta\otimes\tau

to descend to a representation of
Z(\widetilde{T})\widetilde{T}_{0}
, which we also denote by the same symbol
\tau_{\eta}
.

Now, for each unramified character
\chi:T\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, we define

i_{\eta,\tau}(\chi):=\chi\otimes\operatorname{Ind}_{Z(\widetilde{T})\widetilde{T}_{0}}^{\widetilde{T}}\tau_{\eta}.

In what follows, we will show that this induced representation is irreducible, and every irreducible representation of
\widetilde{T}
which contains the pseudo-spherical representation
\tau
is of this form, which will prove that the pair
(\widetilde{T}_{0},\tau)
is a type of
\widetilde{T}
.

Let us start with

Lemma 4.25
.

Let
y\in Y
. Then
{}^{\tilde{y}(\varpi)}\tau=\tau
if and only if
y\in\widetilde{Y}
.

Proof.

We know that there exists
u\in 1+\varpi^{2e}\mathcal{O}
such that
(u,\varpi)=-1
by Corollary
2.3
. But since
1+\varpi^{2e}\mathcal{O}\subseteq R
, for each root
\alpha\in\Phi
, we have
\tau(\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(u))=1
. Now for each
y\in Y

\tilde{y}(\varpi)^{-1}\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(u)\tilde{y}(\varpi)=(u,\varpi)^{\langle\alpha,y\rangle}\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(u)=(-1)^{\langle\alpha,y\rangle}\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(u)

by (
MR4
).
Hence one can see that
{}^{\tilde{y}(\varpi)}\tau=\tau
if and only if
\langle\alpha,y\rangle\in 2\mathbb{Z}
, namely
y\in\widetilde{Y}
.
∎

We can then prove

Proposition 4.26
.

The induced representation
i_{\eta,\tau}(\chi)
is irreducible and contains
\tau
with multiplicity one when restricted to
\widetilde{T}_{0}
.

Proof.

Both the irreducibility and the multiplicity one property follow from the basic Mackey theory together with the above lemma (Lemma
4.25
) and the fact that every element in
\widetilde{T}/\penalty Z(\widetilde{T})\widetilde{T}_{0}
is represented by an element of the form
\tilde{y}(\varpi)
for some
y\in Y
. The detail is left to the reader.
∎

Finally the following proposition shows that
(\widetilde{T}_{0},\tau)
is a type of
\widetilde{T}
.

Proposition 4.27
.

Let
\pi
be a smooth irreducible
\widetilde{T}
-module. The
\tau
-isotypic subspace of
\pi|_{\widetilde{T}_{0}}
is nonzero if and only if
\pi\cong i_{\eta,\tau}(\chi)
for some unramified character
\chi:T\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}
.

Proof.

The if direction is clear.
Suppose that the
\tau
-isotypic subspace of
\pi|_{\widetilde{T}_{0}}
is nonzero. Thus by the Frobenius reciprocity we have a surjective
\widetilde{T}
-module homomorphism
\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde{T}_{0}}^{\widetilde{T}}(\tau)\twoheadrightarrow\pi
. Let
\chi^{\prime}
be the central character of
\pi
restricted to
\mu_{2}\widetilde{Y}(\varpi)
. Then the surjective map must factor through the
\chi^{\prime}
-coinvariants

\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde{T}_{0}}^{\widetilde{T}}(\tau)_{\chi^{\prime}}\cong\operatorname{Ind}_{Z(\widetilde{T})\widetilde{T}_{0}}^{\widetilde{T}}((\chi^{\prime}\cdot\eta^{-1})\otimes\tau_{\eta}).

Now let
\chi:T\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}
be an unramified character extending the non-genuine character
\chi^{\prime}\cdot\eta^{-1}
. By comparison of central characters and the Stone-Von Neumann Theorem for
\widetilde{T}
we see that

\operatorname{Ind}_{Z(\widetilde{T})\widetilde{T}_{0}}^{\widetilde{T}}((\chi^{\prime}\cdot\eta^{-1})\otimes\tau_{\eta})\cong\chi\otimes\operatorname{Ind}_{Z(\widetilde{T})\widetilde{T}_{0}}^{\widetilde{T}}\tau_{\eta}.

Now the result follows because this induced representation is irreducible and maps onto
\pi
.
∎

5.
Pseudo-spherical representations of
\widetilde{K}

Now that we have constructed a pseudo-spherical representation
\tau
of
\widetilde{T}_{0}
, the next task is to construct an irreducible representation
\sigma
of the preimage
\widetilde{I}
of the Iwahori subgroup
I\subseteq G
that is to be used to define our Iwahori Hecke algebra. We achieve this by applying what we call the
parahoric induction
, which is developed by Crisp, Meir and Onn in
[
CMO19
]
1
1

1

In
[
CMO19
]
, this induction is called a variant of the Harish-Chandra induction instead of the parahoric induction.
, based on the parahoric induction of Dat
[
Dat09
]
. We call this representation
\sigma
of
\widetilde{I}
a pseudo-spherical representation of
\widetilde{I}
. This representation is essentially “Weyl invariant”, which allows us to extend it to a representation of
\widetilde{K}
, the preimage of the hyperspecial maximal compact subgroup
K\subseteq G
. We also call this extension a pseudo-spherical representation of
\widetilde{K}
.

Also at the end, we establish a finite group Shimura correspondence, generalizing the work of Savin
[
Sav12
]
which proves the case when
F
is unramified over
\mathbb{Q}_{2}
. More precisely, tensoring by the pseudo-spherical representation defines an equivalence of categories between the category of representations of
G(\mathcal{O}/\varpi\mathcal{O})
and a certain subcategory of
\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}}
-modules.

5.1.
Structure of
\widetilde{K}

In this subsection we prove that certain subgroups of
K
split to
\widetilde{G}
. In particular, we identify the maximal principal congruence subgroup of
K
which splits with a normal image in
\widetilde{K}
.

To begin we set up some notation for congruence subgroups of
G
. For
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, define

\displaystyle\Gamma(k)

\displaystyle:=\langle T_{k},\,x_{\alpha}(t)\,:\,t\in\varpi^{k}\mathcal{O},\,\alpha\in\Phi\rangle;

\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}(k)

\displaystyle:=\langle\Gamma(k),\,x_{\alpha}(t)\,:\,t\in\mathcal{O},\,\alpha\in\Phi^{+}\rangle;

\displaystyle\Gamma_{0}(k)

\displaystyle:=\langle\Gamma_{1}(k),\,T_{0}\rangle,

where we recall

T_{k}=T_{1+\varpi^{k}\mathcal{O}}=\langle\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(t)\,:\,t\in 1+\varpi^{k}\mathcal{O},\alpha\in\Phi\rangle.

Note that

\Gamma(k)\subseteq\Gamma_{1}(k)\subseteq\Gamma_{0}(k).

Alternatively, if
\mathbb{G}
is an
\mathcal{O}
-model for
G
such that
K=\mathbb{G}(\mathcal{O})
, then one can consider the surjective mod
\varpi^{k}
map (
[
Abe69
, Proposition 1.6]
)

\mathbb{G}(\mathcal{O})\longrightarrow\mathbb{G}(\mathcal{O}/\penalty\varpi^{k}\mathcal{O}),

and the groups
\Gamma(k),\Gamma_{1}(k)
and
\Gamma_{0}(k)
are the preimages of the trivial group, the unipotent radical
U(\mathcal{O}/\penalty\varpi^{k}\mathcal{O})
and the Borel subgroup
B(\mathcal{O}/\penalty\varpi^{k}\mathcal{O})
, respectively.

We are mostly concerned with the groups

\Gamma(2e),\quad\Gamma(1),\quad\Gamma_{1}(2e),\quad\Gamma_{0}(1),\quad\Gamma_{0}(2),

where we often write

(5.1)

I:=\Gamma_{0}(1){\quad\text{and}\quad}I_{2}:=\Gamma_{0}(2).

The former is, of course, the Iwahori subgroup. Note the inclusions

\Gamma_{1}(2e)\subseteq I_{2}\subseteq I\subseteq K{\quad\text{and}\quad}\Gamma(2e)\subseteq\Gamma(1)\subseteq I\subseteq K.

Let
\Gamma
be any of the groups
\Gamma_{0}(k),\Gamma_{1}(k),\Gamma(k)
. The group
\Gamma
admits an Iwahori factorization, meaning the multiplication map

\mu_{\Gamma}:(U^{-}_{0}\cap\Gamma)\times(T_{0}\cap\Gamma)\times(U_{0}\cap\Gamma)\rightarrow\Gamma

is a homeomorphism. Using this factorization we can define a set-theoretic section of the exact sequence (
3.1
) for these congruence subgroups.

First, recall the the sections
\mathbf{s}^{\pm}
as in (
3.4
). Second, recall that every element of
T
can be uniquely written in the form
\prod_{\alpha}h_{\alpha}(u_{\alpha})
, where
u_{\alpha}\in F^{\times}
. If we fix a linear order on
\Delta
we can define a section
\mathbf{s}_{T}:T\rightarrow\widetilde{T}
by
\prod_{\alpha}h_{\alpha}(u_{\alpha})\mapsto\prod_{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(u_{\alpha})
.

Now we can define a section of the congruence subgroups. Define

\mathbf{s}_{\Gamma}:=(\mathbf{s}^{-}\times\mathbf{s}_{T}\times\mathbf{s}^{+})\circ\mu_{\Gamma}^{-1}:\Gamma\rightarrow\widetilde{G}.

The map
\mathbf{s}_{\Gamma}
is not a group homomorphism in general, but for
\Gamma=\Gamma_{1}(2e)
it is a homomorphism.

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
\Gamma=\Gamma_{1}(2e)
. The section

\mathbf{s}_{\Gamma}:=(\mathbf{s}^{-}\times\mathbf{s}_{T}\times\mathbf{s}^{+})\circ\mu_{\Gamma}^{-1}:\Gamma\longrightarrow\widetilde{G}

is a splitting of the exact sequence (
3.1
).

Proof.

We already know that each of
\mathbf{s}^{+},\mathbf{s}_{T}|_{T_{2e}},\mathbf{s}^{-}
is a homomorphism. Hence, it suffices to show that the image is closed under multiplication. Note that
\Gamma
is generated by the subgroups
T_{2e}
,
U_{\alpha_{0},2e}
and
U_{\alpha,0}
with
\alpha\in\Delta
, where we recall the notation
U_{\alpha_{0},2e}
and
U_{\alpha,0}
from (
2.8
). It is clear that the image is closed under multiplication on the left by
T_{2e}
and
U_{\alpha_{0},2e}
, and under multiplication on the right by
T_{2e}
and
U_{0}
. So it remains to show that for any
\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(a)
, where
\alpha\in\Delta
and
a\in\mathcal{O}
, and any
y\in U^{-}_{2e}
, we have

\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(a)\mathbf{s}_{\Gamma}(y)\in\mathbf{s}_{\Gamma}(\Gamma).

Let
