V=U_{\Phi^{-}\smallsetminus\{-\alpha\}}(4\mathcal{O})\subseteq U^{-}_{2e}
. Since
\alpha
is a simple root, the multiplication map defines a set bijection
V\times U_{-\alpha,2e}\cong U^{-}_{2e}
, and
\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(a)
normalizes
V
.

Now let
v\in V
and
b\in\mathcal{O}
be such that
y=v\tilde{x}_{-\alpha}(4b)
. Because
\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(a)
normalizes
\mathbf{s}^{-}(V)
by (
MR2
), we have

\displaystyle\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(a)\mathbf{s}_{\Gamma}(y)=

\displaystyle[\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(a)\mathbf{s}^{-}(v)\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(-a)]\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(a)\tilde{x}_{-\alpha}(4b)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(a)\mathbf{s}^{-}(v)\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(-a)]\tilde{x}_{-\alpha}(\frac{4b}{1+4ab})\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(1+4ab)\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(\frac{a}{1+4ab})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{s}_{\Gamma}\left(x_{\alpha}(a)vx_{\alpha}(-a)x_{-\alpha}(\frac{4b}{1+4ab})\right)\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(1+4ab)\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(\frac{a}{1+4ab}),

where we used Lemma
3.3
for the second equality. The result follows.
∎

Remark
.

Proposition
5.2
generalizes the splitting results in type
A_{2}
of
[
Kar21
, Theorem 3.3]
, which are local counterparts to the splitting studied in
[
Kar22
]
.

Now, let us denote the splitting
\mathbf{s}_{\Gamma_{1}(2e)}
simply by
\mathbf{s}
, so we have a homomorphism

\mathbf{s}:\Gamma_{1}(2e)\longrightarrow\widetilde{K}.

The following proposition follows directly from the Steinberg relations, Corollary
3.3
, and our results on the Hilbert symbol.

Proposition 5.3
.

(1)

The subgroup
\mathbf{s}(\Gamma_{1}(2e))\subseteq\widetilde{\Gamma}_{0}(2e)
is normal.

(2)

The subgroup
\mathbf{s}(\Gamma(2e))\subseteq\widetilde{K}
is normal.

(3)

If
\Gamma(2e-1)
splits, then its image cannot be normal in
\widetilde{K}
.

Remark
.

Let
e^{\prime}
be the even element in the set
\{e,e+1\}
. A more detailed analysis of the Hilbert symbol should show that
\mathbf{s}_{\Gamma(e^{\prime})}
is a splitting of
\Gamma(e^{\prime})
. However, one can readily see, by comparing the relations (
R3
) and (
R4
) with (
MR3
) and (
MR4
), that the image of any splitting of
\Gamma(2e-1)
cannot be normal in
\widetilde{K}
, and thus
\Gamma(2e)
is the maximal principal congruence subgroup of
K
which splits with normal image in
\widetilde{K}
.

Now, since the group
\mathbf{s}(\Gamma(2e))
is normal in
\widetilde{K}
, we can let

(5.4)

\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}}:=\widetilde{K}/\mathbf{s}(\Gamma(2e)).

Then this finite group is a nontrivial
\mu_{2}
-central extension of
K/\Gamma(2e)
; namely we have

(5.5)

1\rightarrow\mu_{2}\rightarrow\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}}\rightarrow K/\Gamma(2e)\rightarrow 1.

We know the extension is nontrivial because
\widetilde{T}_{0}/\mathbf{s}(T_{2e})
is nonabelian.

Remark
.

Suppose that
\mathbb{G}
is an
\mathcal{O}
-model for
G
such that
K=\mathbb{G}(\mathcal{O})
. Then the group
K/\Gamma(2e)
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{G}(\mathcal{O}/4\mathcal{O})
. Chevalley groups over local rings were studied by Abe
[
Abe69
]
. Central extensions of such groups, which include
\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}}
, have been studied by Stein
[
Ste73
]
.

5.2.
Parahoric induction

In this subsection we review the parahoric induction of
[
CMO19
]
, which generalizes the parabolic induction of
[
Dat09
]
, and prove some basic results.

For this subsection we let
G
denote an arbitrary profinite group instead of our Chevalley group, and let
\mathcal{M}(G)
be the category of smooth representations of
G
.

Let us recall a couple of definitions from
[
Dat09
,
CMO19
]
.

Definition 5.6
.

The group
G
is said to have a
virtual Iwahori decomposition

(U,T,V)
if
U,T,V\subseteq G
are closed subgroups such that

•

T
normalizes
U
and
V
,

•

the multiplication map
U\times T\times V\rightarrow G
is an open embedding,

•

G
contains arbitrarily small open normal subgroups
K
such that the multiplication map
(U\cap K)\times(T\cap K)\times(V\cap K)\rightarrow K
is a homeomorphism.

A virtual Iwahori decomposition is call an
Iwahori decomposition
if the multiplication map
U\times T\times V\rightarrow G
is a homeomorphism.

Let
\mathcal{H}(G)
be the space of locally constant
\mathbb{C}
-valued functions on
G
. The convolution product gives
\mathcal{H}(G)
the structure of an idempotented
\mathbb{C}
-algebra, and each smooth representation is viewed as an
\mathcal{H}(G)
-module, so that the category
\mathcal{M}(G)
is identified with the category of smooth nondegenerate
\mathcal{H}(G)
-modules. We write
e_{G}\in\mathcal{H}(G)
for the idempotent of the trivial representation of
G
. We then define the parahoric induction and restriction from
[
Dat09
,
CMO19
]
as follows.

Definition 5.7
.

Let
(U,T,V)
be a virtual Iwahori decomposition of a profinite group
G
. Let
\tau
be a smooth
T
-module and let
\sigma
be a smooth
G
-module. Let
e_{U}\in\mathcal{H}(U)
and
e_{V}\in\mathcal{H}(V)
be the idempotents of the trivial representation of
U
and
V
respectively.

Define functors
i_{U,V}:\mathcal{M}(T)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(G)
and
r_{U,V}:\mathcal{M}(G)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(T)
by

\displaystyle i_{U,V}:=

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(G)e_{U}e_{V}\otimes_{\mathcal{H}(T)}(-);

\displaystyle r_{U,V}:=

\displaystyle e_{U}e_{V}\mathcal{H}(G)\otimes_{\mathcal{H}(G)}(-).

We may write
i_{U,V}^{G}=i_{U,V}
and
r_{U,V}^{G}=r_{U,V}
if we want to emphasize
G
.

Let us list a couple of important properties of these functors proved in
[
CMO19
,
Dat09
]
.

Proposition 5.8
.

Let
(U,T,V)
be a virtual Iwahori decomposition of a profinite group
G
.

(1)

There are natural isomorphisms

i_{U,V}\cong i_{V,U}{\quad\text{and}\quad}r_{U,V}\cong r_{V,U}.

(2)

i_{U,V}
is both left and right adjoint to
r_{U,V}
; namely we have

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{H}(G)}(\sigma,i_{U,V}\tau)\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{H}(T)}(r_{U,V}\sigma,\tau){\quad\text{and}\quad}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{H}(G)}(i_{U,V}\tau,\sigma)\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{H}(T)}(\tau,r_{U,V}\sigma)

for all
\sigma\in\mathcal{M}(G)
and
\tau\in\mathcal{M}(T)
.

Proof.

This is
[
CMO19
, Theorem 2.18 (1), (2) and (3)]
.
∎

If
(U,T,V)
is an Iwahori decomposition (instead of merely virtual), the above two functors have particularly nice properties as follows.

Proposition 5.9
.

Suppose that
(U,T,V)
is an Iwahori decomposition of
G
. Then we have the following:

(1)

i_{U,V}
sends
\operatorname{Irr}(T)
to
\operatorname{Irr}(G)
, and
r_{U,V}
sends
\operatorname{Irr}(G)
to
\operatorname{Irr}(T)\cup\{0\}
.

(2)

r_{U,V}\circ i_{U,V}\cong id_{\mathcal{M}(T)}
.

(3)

If
\sigma\in\operatorname{Irr}(G)
is such that
r_{U,V}\sigma\neq 0
, then
i_{U,V}r_{U,V}\sigma\cong\sigma
.

(4)

If
\sigma\in\operatorname{Irr}(G)
, then

\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\operatorname{Hom}_{T}(\sigma^{U},\sigma^{V})\leq 1.

Further, if this dimension is zero then
r_{U,V}\sigma=0
, and if it is 1, then the
\operatorname{Hom}
space is generated by an isomorphism
e_{V}e_{U}
and
r_{U,V}\sigma\cong\sigma^{U}\cong\sigma^{V}
.

Proof.

This is
[
CMO19
, Theorem 2.23]
.
∎

The following definition is motivated by the well known result that we will recall in Lemma
5.20
.

Definition 5.10
.

Suppose that
G
has two virtual Iwahori decompositions
(U,T,V)
and
(U^{\prime},T,V^{\prime})
. We call the two virtual decompositions
compatible
if the following four identities hold.

\displaystyle U=

\displaystyle(U\cap U^{\prime})(U\cap V^{\prime}),

\displaystyle V=

\displaystyle(V\cap\,U^{\prime})(V\cap V^{\prime}),

\displaystyle U^{\prime}=

\displaystyle(U^{\prime}\cap U)(U^{\prime}\cap V),

\displaystyle V^{\prime}=

\displaystyle(V^{\prime}\cap U)(V^{\prime}\cap V).

The following is an important property of compatible decompositions.

Proposition 5.11
.

Assume
(U,T,V)
and
(U^{\prime},T,V^{\prime})
are compatible virtual Iwahori decompositions of
G
. Then we have

i_{U,V}\cong i_{U^{\prime},V^{\prime}}{\quad\text{and}\quad}r_{U,V}\cong r_{U^{\prime},V^{\prime}}.

Proof.

This is
[
CMO19
, Theorem 2.18 (4)]
.
∎

Lemma 5.12
.

Let
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
be profinite groups and assume each
H_{i}
has a virtual Iwahori decomposition
(U_{i},T_{i},V_{i})
. Let
\phi:H_{1}\rightarrow H_{2}
be a group isomorphism such that
\phi(U_{1})=U_{2}
,
\phi(T_{1})=T_{2}
, and
\phi(V_{1})=V_{2}
. Let
\tau\in\operatorname{Irr}(T_{1})
. Then as
H_{2}
-modules

\,{}^{\phi}i_{U_{1},V_{1}}\tau\cong i_{\phi(U_{1}),\phi(V_{1})}\,^{\phi}\tau.

Proof.

One can directly check that there is a well-defined linear map
\,{}^{\phi}i_{U_{1},V_{1}}\tau\rightarrow i_{\phi(U_{1}),\phi(V_{1})}\,^{\phi}\tau
defined by
f(g)e_{U_{1}}e_{V_{1}}\otimes v\mapsto f(\phi^{-1}(g))e_{U_{2}}e_{V_{2}}\otimes v
, where
g\in G
and
v\in\tau
, and this map is an isomorphism of
H_{2}
-modules.
∎

Proposition 5.13
.

Let
G
be a profinite group with an Iwahori decomposition
(U,T,V)
. Let
\phi\in\operatorname{Aut}(G)
be such that
\phi(T)=T
. Let
\tau\in\operatorname{Irr}(T)
.
Suppose that

•

\,{}^{\phi}\tau\cong\tau
,

•

the two Iwahori factorizations
(U,T,V)
and
(\phi(U),T,\phi(V))
are compatible.

Then

\,{}^{\phi}i_{U,V}\tau\cong i_{U,V}\tau.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.12
we have
\,{}^{\phi}i_{U,V}\tau\cong i_{\phi(U),\phi(V)}\,^{\phi}\tau
. Since
\,{}^{\phi}\tau\cong\tau
we have
i_{\phi(U),\phi(V)}\,^{\phi}\tau\cong i_{\phi(U),\phi(V)}\tau
. But since
(U,T,V)
and
(\phi(U),T,\phi(V))
are compatible by our assumption, the proposition follows from Proposition
5.11
.
∎

Lemma 5.14
.

Let
G
be a profinite group with an Iwahori decomposition
(U,T,V)
. Let
H\subseteq G
be an open subgroup with an Iwahori decomposition
(U^{\prime}=H\cap U,T^{\prime}=H\cap T,V)
. Let
\tau
be an irreducible representation of
T
that remains irreducible when restricted to
T^{\prime}
.

The
\widetilde{T}^{\prime}
-module homomorphism
\phi:r_{U^{\prime},V}^{H}i_{U,V}^{G}\tau\rightarrow r_{U,V}^{G}i_{U,V}^{G}\tau
defined by

e_{U^{\prime}}e_{V}f\otimes_{\mathcal{H}(H)}v\mapsto e_{U}e_{V}f\otimes_{\mathcal{H}(G)}v

is a well defined isomorphism of
T^{\prime}
-modules.

Proof.

To begin we show that the map
\phi
is well defined by constructing it directly. We do this in several steps.

Because
H\subseteq G
is of finite index, extension by
0
defines a
(H,\mathcal{H}(H))
-bimodule inclusion
\mathcal{H}(H)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}(G)
. (Here we are fixing a Haar measure on
G
and restricting it to a Haar measure on
H
.) Thus the map
A:e_{U^{\prime}}e_{V}\mathcal{H}(H)\rightarrow e_{U}e_{V}\mathcal{H}(G)
defined by
e_{U^{\prime}}e_{V}f\mapsto e_{U}e_{V}f
(i.e. left convolution by
e_{U}
) is a
(T^{\prime},\mathcal{H}(H))
-bimodule homomorphism.We tensor by
\otimes_{H}i_{U,V}^{G}\tau
and get a map
A\otimes id_{i_{U,V}^{G}\tau}:e_{U^{\prime}}e_{V}\mathcal{H}(H)\otimes_{\mathcal{H}(H)}i_{U,V}^{G}\tau\rightarrow e_{U}e_{V}\mathcal{H}(G)\otimes_{\mathcal{H}(H)}i_{U,V}^{G}\tau
by functoriality. Finally since the right
\mathcal{H}(G)
-module action on
e_{U}e_{V}\mathcal{H}(G)
and the left
\mathcal{H}(G)
action on
i_{U,V}^{G}\tau
extends the respective
\mathcal{H}(H)
action, we have a surjective map
B:e_{U}e_{V}\mathcal{H}(G)\otimes_{\mathcal{H}(H)}i_{U,V}^{G}\tau\twoheadrightarrow e_{U}e_{V}\mathcal{H}(G)\otimes_{\mathcal{H}(G)}i_{U,V}^{G}\tau
defined by
e_{U}e_{V}f\otimes_{\mathcal{H}(H)}v\mapsto e_{U}e_{V}f\otimes_{\mathcal{H}(G)}v
. Then
\phi=B\circ(A\otimes_{\mathcal{H}(H)}id_{i_{U,V}^{G}\tau})

Let
\Gamma
be the kernel of
i_{U,V}^{G}\tau
, so that
\Gamma
is a normal subgroup which acts trivially on
i_{U,V}^{G}\tau
. Note that in
\mathcal{H}(G)
we have
e_{\Gamma\cap H}*e_{\Gamma}=e_{\Gamma}
. Thus under the map
\phi
we have

e_{U^{\prime}}e_{V}e_{\Gamma\cap H}\otimes_{\mathcal{H}(H)}v\mapsto e_{U}e_{V}e_{\Gamma}\otimes_{\mathcal{H}(G)}v

Since
r_{U_{0},V}^{\widetilde{I}_{2}}i_{U,V}^{G}\tau
is spanned by vectors of the form
e_{U}e_{V}e_{\Gamma}\otimes v
, where
v\in i_{U,V}^{G}\tau
it follows that the map
\phi
is surjective. By Proposition
5.9
(
2
)
r_{U,V}^{G}i_{U,V}^{G}\tau\cong\tau
, so the map is nonzero. By Lemma
5.16
and Proposition
5.9
,
\dim r_{U^{\prime},V}^{H}i_{U,V}^{G}\tau\leq\dim\tau
. Thus the map is an isomorphism of
T^{\prime}
-modules.

∎

The next proposition provides a sufficient condition for when parahoric induction is preserved under restriction.

Proposition 5.15
.

Let
G
be a profinite group with an Iwahori decomposition
(U,T,V)
. Let
H\subseteq G
be an open subgroup with an Iwahori decomposition
(U^{\prime}=H\cap U,T^{\prime}=H\cap T,V)
. Suppose that

•

\tau
is a representation of
T
such that
\tau
restricted to
T^{\prime}
remains irreducible;

•

(i_{U,V}\tau)^{U^{\prime}}=(i_{U,V}\tau)^{U}
.

Then as
H
-modules

(i_{U,V}\tau)|_{H}\cong i_{U^{\prime},V}\tau.

Proof.

To begin we show that
\operatorname{Hom}_{H}(i_{U^{\prime},V}\tau,i_{U,V}\tau)\cong\mathbb{C}
. By Proposition
5.8
,

\operatorname{Hom}_{H}(i_{U^{\prime},V}\tau,\,i_{U,V}\tau)\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{T^{\prime}}(\tau,\,r_{U^{\prime},V}i_{U,V}\tau).

By Lemma
5.14
, as
T^{\prime}
-modules
r_{U^{\prime},V}i_{U,V}\tau\cong r_{U,V}i_{U,V}\tau
. By Proposition
5.9
(
2
), we know
r_{U,V}i_{U,V}\tau\cong\tau
as
T
-modules. This is also true as
T^{\prime}
-modules since
\tau
restricted to
T^{\prime}
remains irreducible. Thus
r_{U^{\prime},V}i_{U,V}\tau\cong\tau
as
T^{\prime}
-modules. Therefore
\operatorname{Hom}_{H}(i_{U^{\prime},V}\tau,\,i_{U,V}\tau)\cong\mathbb{C}
.

Now let
\phi\in\operatorname{Hom}_{H}(i_{U^{\prime},V}\tau,\,i_{U,V}\tau)
be nonzero. We want to show that
\phi
is an
H
-module isomorphism. The map
\phi
is injective because
i_{U^{\prime},V}\tau
is an irreducible
H
-module and
\phi\neq 0
. Thus it remains to show that
\phi
is surjective.

By the Iwahori factorization, the space
i_{U,V}\tau
is generated as a
V
-module by elements of the form
e_{U}e_{V}\otimes w
, where
w\in\tau
. So, in particular
i_{U,V}\tau
is generated as an
H
-module by the subspace
(i_{U,V}\tau)^{U}
. By assumption
(i_{U,V}\tau)^{U}=(_{U,V}\tau)^{U^{\prime}}
, so
i_{U,V}\tau
is
H
-generated by
(i_{U,V}\tau)^{U^{\prime}}
.

We apply the
U^{\prime}
-invariants functor to the injection

i_{U^{\prime},V}\tau\xhookrightarrow{\;\phi\;}i_{U,V}\tau

to get

\left(i_{U^{\prime},V}\tau\right)^{U^{\prime}}\xhookrightarrow{\;\phi^{U^{\prime}}\;}\left(i_{U,V}\tau\right)^{U^{\prime}}=\left(i_{U,V}\tau\right)^{U}.

By Proposition
5.9
(2) and (4), this injection must be an isomorphism of
T^{\prime}
-modules, as the domain and codomain are isomorphic to
\tau
. Thus the image of
\phi
contains
\left(i_{U,V}\tau\right)^{U}
. Therefore as an
H
-module the image of
\phi
generates
i_{U,V}\tau
. But
\phi
is an
H
-modules homomorphism, so
\phi
is surjective.
∎

Lemma 5.16
.

Let
G
be a profinite group with an Iwahori decomposition
(U,T,V)
. Let
\pi
be a finite-dimensional representation of
G
. Then

\dim r_{U,V}\pi\leq\dim\pi^{V}.

Proof.

Since
\pi
is finite dimensional there exists
N\subseteq G
a normal subgroup such that
N
acts trivially on
\pi
. Note that for any
f\in\mathcal{H}
we have
e_{N}*f=f*e_{N}
.

By definition
r_{U,V}\pi
is spanned by vectors of the form
e_{U}e_{V}f\otimes v
, where
f\in\mathcal{H}(G)
and
v\in\pi
. But
e_{U}e_{V}f\otimes v=e_{U}e_{V}e_{N}\otimes e_{V}f\cdot v
. Now the result follows because
e_{V}f\cdot v\in\pi^{V}
.
∎

If
G
is finite, then it is convenient to make the identification

\mathcal{H}(G)\cong\mathbb{C}[G],\quad\delta_{g}\mapsto g,

of algebras, where
\mathbb{C}[G]
is the group algebra of
G
and
\delta_{g}
is the delta function at
g\in G
. Under this identification, we have

(5.17)

e_{H}=\frac{1}{|H|}\sum_{h\in H}h

for each subgroup
H\subseteq G
. Note that for each
g\in G
such that
gHg^{-1}=H
we have
ge_{H}=e_{H}g
. Also if
h\in H
, then
he_{H}=e_{H}h=e_{H}
; namely
e_{H}
“absorbs”
h
. Note also that the third condition for virtual Iwahori decomposition is trivial because one can just choose
K
to be the trivial group.

Let us prove a few facts for finite groups.

Lemma 5.18
.

Assume
G
is a finite group with an Iwahori decomposition
(U,T,V)
and
\tau\in\mathcal{M}(T)
. Then
i_{U,V}\tau
(as a
\mathbb{C}
-vector space) is spanned by the vectors of the form

ye_{U}e_{V}\otimes v\qquad(y\in V,\;v\in\tau).

Proof.

By definition, the space
i_{U,V}\tau
is generated by the vectors of the form
ge_{U}e_{V}\otimes v
for
g\in G
and
v\in\tau
. Now, by the Iwahori decomposition, each
g^{-1}
is written as
g^{-1}=xty
for some
x\in U,t\in T,y\in V
, so that
g=y^{-1}t^{-1}x^{-1}
. But
x^{-1}e_{U}=e_{U}
by absorbing
x^{-1}
, and

t^{-1}e_{U}e_{V}\otimes v=e_{U}e_{V}t^{-1}\otimes v=e_{U}e_{V}\otimes\tau(t^{-1})v.

Hence we can write
ge_{U}e_{V}\otimes v=y^{-1}e_{U}e_{V}\otimes\tau(t^{-1})v
. The lemma follows.
∎

The final lemma in this subsection relates the parahoric induction with induction in stages where one step involves standard induction.

Lemma 5.19
.

Let
G
be a finite group with an Iwahori decomposition
(U,T,V)
. Let
H\subseteq G
be a subgroup with an Iwahori decomposition
(U^{\prime}=H\cap U,T,V)
. Let
\tau
be a representation of
T
. Then

\operatorname{Hom}_{G}(\operatorname{Ind}_{H}^{G}i_{V,U^{\prime}}\tau,\,i_{V,U}\tau)\neq 0.

Proof.

The surjective
(G,T)
-bimodule map
\mathcal{H}(G)\otimes_{H}\mathcal{H}(H)e_{V}e_{U^{\prime}}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}(G)e_{V}e_{U}
defined by
g\otimes_{H}he_{V}e_{U^{\prime}}\mapsto ghe_{V}e_{U}
induces a surjective
G
-module map

\mathcal{H}(G)\otimes_{H}\mathcal{H}(H)e_{V}e_{U^{\prime}}\otimes_{T}\tau\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}(G)e_{V}e_{U}\otimes_{T}\tau=i_{V,U}\tau.

Since
i_{V,U}\tau\neq 0
by
[
CMO19
, Theorem 2.18 (6)]
, the result follows.
∎

5.3.
Construction of Iwahori types

Now, we apply the above described theory of parahoric induction to our pseudo-spherical representations
\tau
. The basic idea is that we will construct a representation of the Iwahori subgroup
\widetilde{I}
by “parahorically inducing”
\tau
to
\widetilde{I}
, and prove that this representation behaves well with respect to the Weyl group by using the machinery of the parahoric induction. The main results are stated in Proposition
5.24
. (We will later prove in Theorem
7.8
that this representation is an Iwahori type of
\widetilde{G}
in the sense of Bushnell-Kutzko
[
BK98
, (4.1) Definition]
.)

Recall from §
5.1
that we have a splitting
\mathbf{s}:\Gamma(2e)\to\widetilde{K}
whose image is normal in
\widetilde{K}
. The kernel of the representation we will construct will contain
\mathbf{s}(\Gamma(2e))
. Hence we almost always work with “representations modulo
{\Gamma(2e)}
”, so that we view these representations as representations of subgroups of the finite quotient
\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}=\widetilde{K}/\penalty\mathbf{s}(\Gamma(2e))
.

For this purpose, let us introduce the following notational convention. For each subgroup
H\subseteq K
, we denote its image in
K/\penalty\Gamma(2e)
by the corresponding sans-serif font
\mathsf{H}
. To be precise

\mathsf{H}:=H/\penalty(\Gamma(2e)\cap H)\subseteq K/\penalty\Gamma(2e)=\mathsf{K}.

Further, denote the image of
\widetilde{H}
in
\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}
by
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}
, so that we have

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}}
\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}=\widetilde{K}/\penalty\mathbf{s}(\Gamma(2e))
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{H}}
\mathsf{K}=K/\penalty\Gamma(2e)
.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\supseteq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\supseteq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}

In particular, we often use the groups

\mathsf{U}_{j}^{\pm}{\quad\text{and}\quad}\mathsf{U}_{\alpha,j}\qquad(j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}),

where the corresponding
U_{j}^{\pm}
and
U_{\alpha,j}
are recalled from (
2.8
). We often set

\mathsf{U}_{j}=\mathsf{U}_{j}^{+}.

We are particularly interested in the case when
j=0,1
.
These groups are usually viewed as subgroups of
\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}
via the splittings
\mathbf{s}^{\pm}:U_{0}^{\pm}\to\widetilde{K}
, so that we have

\mathbf{s}^{\pm}:\mathsf{U}_{0}^{\pm}\xhookrightarrow{\hskip 16.38895pt}\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}.

The images of the elements
\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(u)
and
\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(t)
in
\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}
are to be denoted by the same symbol.

We should mention that the three groups

\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1),\quad\widetilde{\mathsf{I}}=\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}_{0}(1),\quad\widetilde{\mathsf{I}}_{2}=\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}_{0}(2)

have Iwahori decompositions

(\mathsf{U}_{1},\widetilde{\mathsf{T}}_{1},\mathsf{U}_{1}^{-}),\quad(\mathsf{U}_{0},\widetilde{\mathsf{T}}_{0},\mathsf{U}_{1}^{-}),\quad(\mathsf{U}_{0},\widetilde{\mathsf{T}}_{0},\mathsf{U}_{2}^{-}),

respectively. Also the group
\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}
has a virtual Iwahori decomposition
(\mathsf{U}_{0},\widetilde{\mathsf{T}}_{0},\mathsf{U}_{0}^{-})
.

Now, let
\tau
be a pseudo-spherical representation of
\widetilde{T}_{0}
. Since
T_{2e}\subseteq T_{R}
and
T_{R}
is in the kernel of
\tau
, we view
\tau
as a representation of

\widetilde{\mathsf{T}}_{0}=\widetilde{T}_{0}/\penalty T_{2e}=\widetilde{T}_{0}/\penalty\left(\widetilde{T}_{0}\cap\mathbf{s}(\Gamma(2e))\right).

We continue to write
\tau
for this representation of
\widetilde{{\mathsf{T}}}_{0}
.

We then parahorically induce
\tau
to a representation of
\widetilde{\mathsf{I}}
; namely we set

\sigma:=i_{{\mathsf{U}},{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{1}}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{I}}}\tau,

which is an irreducible representation of
\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}
by Proposition
5.9
. We also consider

\sigma_{1}:=i_{{\mathsf{U}}_{1},{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{1}}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)}\tau,

which is an irreducible representation of
\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)
by Proposition
5.9
. Note that
\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)\subseteq\widetilde{\mathsf{I}}
. One of the main results in this section is to show that

\sigma|_{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)}\cong\sigma_{1},

which will be proven in Proposition
5.24
among other things.

We begin with a few technical lemmas.

Lemma 5.20
.

Let
w\in W
. Then
({\mathsf{U}}_{0},{\mathsf{T}}_{0},{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{0})
and
(\,^{w}{\mathsf{U}}_{0},{\mathsf{T}}_{0},\,^{w}{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{0})
are compatible virtual Iwahori decompositions of
{\mathsf{K}}
. Specifically,

\displaystyle{\mathsf{U}}_{0}=

\displaystyle({\mathsf{U}}_{0}\cap\,^{w}{\mathsf{U}}_{0})({\mathsf{U}}_{0}\cap\,^{w}{\mathsf{U}}_{0}^{-}),

\displaystyle{\mathsf{U}}_{0}^{-}=

\displaystyle({\mathsf{U}}_{0}^{-}\cap\,^{w}{\mathsf{U}}_{0})({\mathsf{U}}_{0}^{-}\cap\,^{w}{\mathsf{U}}_{0}^{-}),

\,{}^{w}{\mathsf{U}}_{0}=

\displaystyle(\,^{w}{\mathsf{U}}_{0}\cap{\mathsf{U}}_{0})(\,^{w}{\mathsf{U}}_{0}\cap{\mathsf{U}}_{0}^{-}),

\,{}^{w}{\mathsf{U}}_{0}^{-}=

\displaystyle(\,^{w}{\mathsf{U}}_{0}^{-}\cap{\mathsf{U}}_{0})(\,^{w}{\mathsf{U}}_{0}^{-}\cap{\mathsf{U}}_{0}^{-}).

The same also holds for
{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)
with the Iwahori factorization
({\mathsf{U}}_{1},{\mathsf{T}}_{1},{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{1})
.

Proof.

This follows because
{\mathsf{U}}_{0}\cap\,^{w}{\mathsf{U}}_{0}=\prod_{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}\cap w\Phi^{+}}{\mathsf{U}}_{\alpha,0}
, along with similar identities for the other intersections.
∎

Lemma 5.21
.

Let
\alpha\in\Delta
and
u\in\mathcal{O}\smallsetminus\{0\}
. Then in the group algebra
\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathsf{I}}]

e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{-\alpha,1}}\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(u)e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}^{-}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}=e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}^{-}}\frac{1}{\left|\varpi\mathcal{O}/\penalty 4\mathcal{O}\right|}\sum_{v\in\varpi\mathcal{O}/4\mathcal{O}}(1+uv,-u)\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(1+uv)e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}.

If
u\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
, note that
\frac{1}{\left|\varpi\mathcal{O}/\penalty 4\mathcal{O}\right|}\sum_{v\in\varpi\mathcal{O}/4\mathcal{O}}(1+uv,-u)\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(1+uv)=X_{\alpha,-u,1}
. (Recall §
4.9
.)

Proof.

Let
{\mathsf{U}}_{\Phi^{-}_{\alpha},1}\subseteq{\mathsf{K}}
be the subgroup generated by the subgroups
{\mathsf{U}}_{\beta,1}
, where
\beta\in\Phi^{-}\smallsetminus\{-\alpha\}
. Note that the multiplication map defines a set bijection
{\mathsf{U}}_{\Phi^{-}_{\alpha},1}\times{\mathsf{U}}_{-\alpha,1}\cong{\mathsf{U}}_{1}^{-}
, and the order of the factors can be reversed. Thus one can readily see from (
5.17
) that the idempotent
e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}^{-}}
can be factored as

e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}^{-}}=e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{\Phi^{-}_{\alpha},1}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{-\alpha,1}}=e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{-\alpha,1}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{\Phi^{-}_{\alpha},1}}.

Hence we have

e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{-\alpha,1}}\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(u)e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{\Phi^{-}_{\alpha},1}}=e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{-\alpha,1}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{\Phi^{-}_{\alpha},1}}\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(u)=e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}^{-}}\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(u),

where we used that
\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(u)
normalizes
{\mathsf{U}}_{\Phi^{-}_{\alpha},1}
.

Using Proposition
3.2
we have

\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(u)e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{-\alpha,1}}=\frac{1}{\left|\varpi\mathcal{O}/\penalty 4\mathcal{O}\right|}\sum_{v\in\varpi\mathcal{O}/4\mathcal{O}}(1+uv,-u)\tilde{x}_{-\alpha}(\frac{v}{1+uv})\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(1+uv)\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(\frac{u}{1+uv}).

Now the result follows because

\displaystyle e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{-\alpha,1}}\sum_{v\in\varpi\mathcal{O}/4\mathcal{O}}(1+uv,-u)\tilde{x}_{-\alpha}(\frac{v}{1+uv})\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(1+uv)\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(\frac{u}{1+uv})e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}

\displaystyle=e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{-\alpha,1}}\sum_{v\in\varpi\mathcal{O}/4\mathcal{O}}(1+uv,-u)\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(1+uv)e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}

by letting
e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{-\alpha,1}}
absorb
\tilde{x}_{-\alpha}(\frac{v}{1+uv})
.
∎

Lemma 5.22
.

Let
\alpha\in\Delta
,
u\in\mathcal{O}
, and
w\in\tau
. Then in the
\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}
-module
\sigma=i_{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}^{-},{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}\tau

e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}}\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(u)e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}^{-}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}\otimes w=e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}^{-}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}\otimes w,

where we recall that the tensor product is over
\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathsf{T}}_{0}]
.

Proof.

This is trivial if
u\in\varpi\mathcal{O}
. So assume
u\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
. Let
X=X_{-\alpha,u,1}=X_{\alpha,-u,1}\in\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathsf{T}}_{0}]
be as in (
4.19
). By Corollary
4.23
, the operator
\tau(X)
is invertible. Thus

\displaystyle e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}}\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(u)e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}^{-}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}\otimes w=

\displaystyle e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}}\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(u)e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}^{-}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}X\otimes\tau(X)^{-1}w

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}}\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(u)Xe_{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}^{-}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}\otimes\tau(X)^{-1}w

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}}\frac{1}{\left|\varpi\mathcal{O}/\penalty 4\mathcal{O}\right|}\sum_{v\in\varpi\mathcal{O}/4\mathcal{O}}(1+uv,u)\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(u)\tilde{h}_{-\alpha}(1+uv)e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}^{-}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}\otimes\tau(X)^{-1}w,

where for the second equality we used
e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}^{-}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}X=Xe_{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}^{-}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}
, noting that
\tilde{h}_{-\alpha}(1+uv)
normalizes both
\mathsf{U}_{1}^{-}
and
\mathsf{U}_{0}
.

Note that

e_{\mathsf{U}_{1}}\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(u)=e_{\mathsf{U}_{1}}\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(\frac{u}{1+uv}),

because for
v\in\varpi\mathcal{O}
we have
u\equiv\frac{u}{1+uv}
modulo
\varpi
. Also

e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}^{-}}=\tilde{x}_{-\alpha}(\frac{v}{1+uv})e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}^{-}}

by absorbing
\tilde{x}_{-\alpha}(\frac{v}{1+uv})
. Hence we can write

\displaystyle e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}}\sum_{v\in\varpi\mathcal{O}/4\mathcal{O}}(1+uv,u)\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(u)\tilde{h}_{-\alpha}(1+uv)e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}^{-}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}\otimes\tau(X)^{-1}w

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}}\sum_{v}(1+uv,u)\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(\frac{u}{1+uv})\tilde{h}_{-\alpha}(1+uv)\tilde{x}_{-\alpha}(\frac{v}{1+uv})e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}^{-}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}\otimes\tau(X)^{-1}w

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}}\left(\sum_{v}\tilde{x}_{-\alpha}(v)\right)\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(u)e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}^{-}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}\otimes\tau(X)^{-1}w\quad(\text{Proposition \ref{PMRootSwap}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\varpi\mathcal{O}/\penalty 4\mathcal{O}\right|e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{-\alpha,1}}\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(u)e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}^{-}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}\otimes\tau(X)^{-1}w.

Now we apply Lemmas
5.21
and
4.20
to get

e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{-\alpha,1}}\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(u)e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}^{-}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}\otimes\tau(X)^{-1}w=e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}^{-}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}\otimes w.

The lemma is proven.
∎

This lemma implies

Proposition 5.23
.

Let
\alpha\in\Delta
. Then we have

\sigma^{{\mathsf{U}_{0}}}=\sigma^{{\mathsf{U}_{0}}\cap\,^{w_{\alpha}}\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}}=\sigma^{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}}.

Proof.

Since
\mathsf{U}_{0}\supseteq(\mathsf{U}_{0}\cap\,^{w_{\alpha}}\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}})\supseteq\mathsf{U}_{1}
, the inclusions
\sigma^{{\mathsf{U}_{0}}}\subseteq\sigma^{{\mathsf{U}_{0}}\cap\,^{w_{\alpha}}\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}}\subseteq\sigma^{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}}
are immediate. It remains to show
\sigma^{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}}\subseteq\sigma^{{\mathsf{U}_{0}}}
. Note that that
\sigma=i_{\mathsf{U}_{0},\mathsf{U}_{1}^{-}}\tau\cong i_{{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{1},{\mathsf{U}_{0}}}\tau
by Proposition
5.8
(1). By the Iwahori factorization and Lemma
5.18
,
(i_{{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{1},{\mathsf{U}_{0}}}\tau)^{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}}
is spanned by elements of the form
e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}}ue_{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}^{-}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}\otimes w
, where
u\in{\mathsf{U}}_{0}
and
w\in\tau
. We also know that
{\mathsf{U}}_{0}
is generated by the elements of the form
x_{\alpha}(u)
, where
\alpha\in\Delta
and
u\in\mathcal{O}
. The result follows by Lemma
5.22
, induction and the identity

e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}}xx_{\alpha}(u)e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}^{-}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}\otimes w=xe_{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}}x_{\alpha}(u)e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{1}^{-}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}\otimes w

for
x\in{\mathsf{U}}_{0}
.
∎

Now we can state and prove the main theorem of this subsection. Let

\sigma_{\alpha}:=i^{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}\cap\,^{w_{\alpha}}\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}}_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}\cap\,^{w_{\alpha}}\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}},{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{1}}\tau,

and recall that

\sigma=i_{{\mathsf{U}_{0}},{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{1}}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{I}}}\tau{\quad\text{and}\quad}\sigma_{1}=i_{{\mathsf{U}}_{1},{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{1}}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)}\tau,

all of which are irreducible representations of
\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}\cap\,^{w_{\alpha}}\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}
,
\widetilde{\mathsf{I}}
and
\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)
, respectively, by Proposition
5.9
.

Proposition 5.24
.

Let
\alpha\in\Delta
.

(1)

The
\widetilde{{\mathsf{\Gamma}}}(1)
-module
\sigma_{1}
is
W
-invariant, i.e.
{}^{w}\sigma_{1}\cong\sigma_{1}
for all
w\in W
.

(2)

As
\widetilde{{\mathsf{\Gamma}}}(1)
-modules,
\sigma_{1}\cong\sigma|_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{\Gamma}}}(1)}
.

(3)

As
\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}\cap\,^{w_{\alpha}}\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}
-modules,
\sigma_{\alpha}\cong\sigma|_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}\cap\,^{w_{\alpha}}\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}}
.

(4)

As
\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}\cap\,^{w_{\alpha}}\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}
-modules ,
\,{}^{w_{\alpha}}\sigma_{\alpha}\cong\sigma_{\alpha}
.

Proof.

(
1
) and (
4
) follow from Proposition
5.13
by setting
\phi
to be the conjugation by the Weyl group element. Indeed, the two conditions of Proposition
5.13
are satisfied by the
W
-invariance of
\tau
and by Lemma
5.20
.

(
2
) and (
3
) follow from Proposition
5.15
. Indeed, the two conditions of Proposition
5.15
are satisfied by Lemma
4.6
and Proposition
5.23
.
∎

5.4.
Extending
\sigma
to
\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}

Next we show that the
W
-invariance of the
\widetilde{{\mathsf{\Gamma}}}(1)
-module
\sigma_{1}
allows us to prove

Theorem 5.25
.

The
\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)
-module
\sigma_{1}
extends uniquely to a
\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}}
-module.

Since we know that
\sigma_{1}
is
W
-invariant, this is actually almost immediate by using the following known results on the simply-connected simply-laced Chevalley groups over finite fields of characteristic
2
, except for a few low rank cases.

Proposition 5.26
.

Let
\kappa
be a finite field of characteristic
2
. Let
G_{\kappa}
be the
\kappa
-points of a simply-connected simply-laced Chevalley group defined over
\kappa
with an irreducible root system. Then
G_{\kappa}
is centrally closed except the following five cases:

(5.27)

\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{4}),\quad\operatorname{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}_{2}),\quad\operatorname{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}_{4}),\quad\operatorname{SL}_{4}(\mathbb{F}_{2}),\quad D_{4}(\mathbb{F}_{2}).

Hence except these five cases,
G_{\kappa}
has no nontrivial double cover.

Proof.

This follows from
[
Ste16
, Corollary 2 and Remark (b), page 58]
.
∎

Remark
.

The Schur multiplier of the above five groups can be found in
[
Ste81
]
. Note that
[
Ste81
, Theorem 1.1]
contains a typo. The group
A_{3}(2)\cong\operatorname{SL}_{4}(\mathbb{F}_{2})\cong A_{8}
should be listed under (a) in that theorem. Also note other isomorphisms
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{4})\cong A_{5}
and
\operatorname{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}_{2})\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}_{7})
.

Let
\kappa=\mathcal{O}/\varpi\mathcal{O}
and let
G_{\kappa}
be the group of
\kappa
-points of the simply-connected Chevalley group over
\kappa
of the same type as
G
. Note that
G_{\kappa}\cong K/\Gamma(1)\cong{\mathsf{K}}/{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)
, where the isomorphisms are induced by the natural maps on Chevalley generators. We also write
T_{\kappa}\subseteq B_{\kappa}\subseteq G_{\kappa}
for the
\kappa
-points of a maximal torus and Borel subgroup of the Chevalley group. We choose these groups so that they are compatible with the Chevalley pinning of
G
.

With this said, we can give the proof of the above theorem

Proof of Theorem
5.25
.

Since we are assuming that
\Phi\neq A_{1}
or
D_{2}
and it is simply-laced, we can see that
\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}
is perfect from the relation (
MR2
). Hence the uniqueness follows.

From Proposition
5.24
(
1
) and (
2
), we know that the irreducible
\widetilde{{\mathsf{\Gamma}}}(1)
-module
\sigma_{1}
is invariant under conjugation by
\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}}
, because
\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}}
is generated by
\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}
and
W
. Hence, if
G_{\kappa}
is not among the five exceptional cases of (
5.27
), one can just invoke
[
CR81
, (11.45) Proposition]
, which immediately proves the theorem.

It remains to show that the representation extends for the five exceptional cases of (
5.27
). Our argument for these exceptional cases is inspired by Savin
[
Sav12
, Proposition 4.1]
. The basic idea is to embed
G
to the group
G^{\prime}
of the same type with one rank larger. Then since we already know that the result holds for
G^{\prime}
, we will obtain the result for
G
by restriction.

To be precise, by Proposition
5.26
, we may assume
G
is the simply-laced simply-connected Chevalley group of type
A_{r}
or
D_{r}
. (Though we have only to consider when
G=\operatorname{SL}_{3},\operatorname{SL}_{4}
or
D_{4}
, our argument works in general.) Define
G^{\prime}
be the simply-laced simply-connected Chevalley group of the same type as that of
G
with rank
r+1
, so that we have the natural embedding
G\subseteq G^{\prime}
. This embedding is obtained as follows. Let
\Phi^{\prime}
be the root system of
G^{\prime}
, where the set of simple roots
\Delta^{\prime}
is so that
\Delta\subseteq\Delta^{\prime}
and the unique root in
\Delta^{\prime}\smallsetminus\Delta
is the root at the left endpoint of the Dynkin diagram. Then
\Phi\subseteq\Phi^{\prime}
is a subroot system, and the group
G
viewed as a subgroup of
G
is the group generated by
\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(t)
for
\alpha\in\Phi
and
t\in F
. (If
G=\operatorname{SL}_{r}
then
G^{\prime}=\operatorname{SL}_{r+1}
, and
\operatorname{SL}_{r}
is embedded in
\operatorname{SL}_{r+1}
in the lower right corner.) We denote by
T^{\prime}
,
K^{\prime}
,
\Gamma(k)^{\prime}
, etc, the corresponding groups for
G^{\prime}
.

Let
\mathsf{N}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}^{\prime}
be the group generated by the subgroups
\mathsf{U}_{\alpha,1}
for
\alpha\in{\Phi^{\prime}}^{+}\smallsetminus\Phi^{+}
. (If
G=\operatorname{SL}_{r}
then
\mathsf{N}
is the unipotent group of the “first rows” in
\operatorname{SL}_{r+1}
.) Similarly we denote
\mathsf{N}^{-}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}^{\prime}
be the group generated by the subgroups
\mathsf{U}_{\alpha,1}
for
\alpha\in{\Phi^{\prime}}^{-}\smallsetminus\Phi^{-}
. Let
\sigma
be our pseudo-spherical representation of
\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)
constructed from a pseudo-spherical representation
\tau
of
\widetilde{T}_{1}
. We know from Proposition
4.17
that there is a pseudo-spherical representation
\tau^{\prime}
of
\widetilde{T}_{1}^{\prime}
such that
\tau^{\prime}|_{\widetilde{T}_{1}}
contains
\tau
with multiplicity one. Let
\sigma^{\prime}
be the pseudo-spherical representation of
\mathsf{\Gamma}(1)^{\prime}
constructed from
\tau^{\prime}
. We claim that

(5.28)

\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)}(\sigma,\,{\sigma^{\prime}}^{\mathsf{N}})\cong\mathbb{C}.

Note that
\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)^{\prime}
has an Iwahori factorization relative to the triple
(\mathsf{N}^{-},\widetilde{\mathsf{T}}_{1}^{\prime}\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1),\mathsf{N})
. Thus as
\widetilde{\mathsf{T}}_{1}^{\prime}\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)
-modules we have
\sigma^{\mathsf{N}}\cong r_{\mathsf{N}^{-},\mathsf{N}}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{T}}_{1}^{\prime}\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)}(\sigma^{\prime})
by Proposition
5.9
. On the other hand, as
\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)^{\prime}
-modules, we have
\sigma^{\prime}\cong i_{\mathsf{N}^{-},\mathsf{N}}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)^{\prime}}\left(i_{\mathsf{U}^{-}_{1},\mathsf{U}_{1}}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{T}}_{1}^{\prime}\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)}\tau^{\prime}\right)
by
[
CMO19
, Theorem 2.17 (7)]
. If we combine the last two isomorphisms and apply Proposition
5.9
(
2
), we see that as
\widetilde{\mathsf{T}}_{1}^{\prime}\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)
-modules
{\sigma^{\prime}}^{\mathsf{N}}\cong i_{\mathsf{U}^{-}_{1},\mathsf{U}_{1}}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{T}}_{1}^{\prime}\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)}\tau^{\prime}
. Thus by Proposition
5.8
,

\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)}(\sigma,{\sigma^{\prime}}^{\mathsf{N}})\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{T}}_{1}}(\tau,r_{\mathsf{U}_{1}^{-},\,\mathsf{U}_{1}}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{T}}_{1}}\left(i_{\mathsf{U}^{-}_{1},\mathsf{U}_{1}}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{T}}_{1}^{\prime}\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)}\tau^{\prime}\right)).

Now as
\widetilde{\mathsf{T}}_{1}
-modules we have
r_{\mathsf{U}_{1},\mathsf{U}_{1}}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{T}}_{1}}\left(i_{\mathsf{U}^{-}_{1},\mathsf{U}_{1}}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{T}}_{1}^{\prime}\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)}\tau^{\prime}\right)\cong\tau^{\prime}
by Lemma
5.14
and Proposition
5.9
(
2
). The isomorphism (
5.28
) follows because
\tau
appears in
\tau^{\prime}|_{\widetilde{\mathsf{T}}_{1}}
with multiplicity one.

By isomorphism (
5.28
), we can identify
\sigma
with the
\sigma
-isotypic subspace of
{\sigma^{\prime}}^{\mathsf{N}}
, up to scaling. Then for any
k\in\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}
we can consider the
\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)
-submodule
k\sigma\subseteq{\sigma^{\prime}}^{\mathsf{N}}
. As
\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)
-modules we have
k\sigma\cong{}^{k}\!\sigma
. But we know that
{}^{k}\!\sigma\cong\sigma
as
\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)
-modules by Proposition
5.24
. Since
\sigma
is identified with the
\sigma
-isotypic subspace of
{\sigma^{\prime}}^{\mathsf{N}}
, it follows that
k\sigma=\sigma
as subspaces of
{\sigma^{\prime}}^{\mathsf{N}}
. Thus
\sigma\subseteq{\sigma^{\prime}}^{\mathsf{N}}
is a
\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}
-submodule. This proves the theorem.
∎

Recall we have the inclusions

\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)\subseteq\widetilde{\mathsf{I}}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathsf{K}},

and the
\widetilde{\mathsf{I}}
-module
\sigma
is an extension of
\sigma_{1}
. Now we prove that the extension of
\sigma_{1}
to
\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}}
is also an extension of
\sigma
, namely

Proposition 5.29
.

Let
\sigma_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}}}
be an arbitrary extension of
\sigma_{1}
to
\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}
. Then

\sigma_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}}}|_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}}\cong\sigma.

Proof.

First note that the set of extensions of
\sigma_{1}
to
\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}
is a torsor under the group
\operatorname{Hom}(\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}/\widetilde{{\mathsf{\Gamma}}}(1),\mathbb{C}^{\times})
. Since
\Phi
is simply-laced, any such character must factor through
T_{\kappa}
. Thus every extension of
\sigma_{1}
to
\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}
is of the form
\chi\otimes\sigma
for some
\chi\in\operatorname{Hom}(T_{\kappa},\mathbb{C}^{\times})
.

Hence, the restriction of
\sigma_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}}}
to
\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}
is of the form
\chi\otimes\sigma
for some
\chi\in\operatorname{Hom}(T_{\kappa},\mathbb{C}^{\times})
. We will show
\chi=1
. Let
\alpha\in\Delta
. We restrict further to
\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}\cap\,^{w_{\alpha}}\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}
and apply Proposition
5.24
(
3
) to see that the restriction of
\sigma_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}}}
to
\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}\cap\,^{w_{\alpha}}\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}
is isomorphic to
\chi\otimes\sigma_{\alpha}
. Since
\sigma_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}}}
and
\sigma_{\alpha}
are
w_{\alpha}
invariant by Theorem
5.24
(
1
) and (
4
), it follows that
\chi
must be
w_{\alpha}
invariant. Since
\Phi
is simply-laced with rank
>1
the character
\chi
must be trivial on elements of the form
h_{\alpha}(t)
for all
t\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}/(1+\varpi^{2e}\mathcal{O})
, which implies that
\chi
is trivial since
G
is simply-connected.
∎

5.5.
Additional properties of
\sigma

The
\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}
-module
\sigma=i_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0},{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{1}}\tau
exhibits some additional properties that will be useful in our investigation of a Hecke algebra on
\widetilde{G}
. We record these in this subsection. Recall from (
5.1
) that
{\mathsf{I}}_{2}\subseteq{\mathsf{I}}
is the subgroup generated by the subgroups
{\mathsf{T}}_{0}
,
{\mathsf{U}}_{0}
and
{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{2}
. Let

\sigma_{2}:=i_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0},{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{2}}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{I}}_{2}}\tau,

which is an irreducible
\widetilde{\mathsf{I}}_{2}
-module by Proposition
5.9
.

Lemma 5.30
.

Let
\alpha\in\Phi^{+}
and
t\in\mathcal{O}
. Then in the group algebra of
\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}}
, we have

e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{2}}x_{\alpha}(\varpi^{2e-1}t)=e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{2}}.

Proof.

For all
y\in{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{2}
the commutator
[y,x_{\alpha}(\varpi^{2e-1}t)]
is trivial mod
\Gamma(2e)
, which gives

e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{2}}x_{\alpha}(\varpi^{2e-1}t)=x_{\alpha}(\varpi^{2e-1}t)e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{2}}.

The result follows because
x_{\alpha}(\varpi^{2e-1}t)\in{\mathsf{U}}_{0}
.
∎

Proposition 5.31
.

The
\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}
-module
\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}}^{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}}\sigma_{2}
is irreducible.

Proof.

It suffices to show
\operatorname{End}_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}}(\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}}^{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}}\sigma_{2})\cong\mathbb{C}
. But by the Frobenius reciprocity and Proposition
5.8
(2), we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{End}_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}}(\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}}^{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}}\sigma_{2})\cong

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}}(\sigma_{2},\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}}^{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}}\sigma_{2})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{T}}}_{0}}(\tau,r_{{\mathsf{U}}^{+}_{0},{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{2}}^{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}}\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}}^{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}}\sigma_{2}).

By definition

r_{{\mathsf{U}}^{+}_{0},{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{2}}^{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}}\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}}^{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}}\sigma_{2}=e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{+}_{0}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{2}}\mathcal{H}(\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}_{2})\otimes_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}}\mathcal{H}(\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}})\otimes_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}}\mathcal{H}(\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}_{2})e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{+}_{0}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{2}}\otimes_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{T}}}_{0}}\tau.

Thus by the Iwahori factorization and Lemma
5.18
,
r_{{\mathsf{U}}^{+}_{0},{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{2}}^{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}}\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}}^{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}}\sigma_{2}
is spanned by elements of the form

e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{+}_{0}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{2}}\otimes_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}}y\otimes_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{+}_{0}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{2}}\otimes_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{T}}}_{0}}w,

where
y\in{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{1}
and
w\in\tau
.

We claim that if
y\in{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{1}\smallsetminus{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{2}
, then
e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{+}_{0}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{2}}\otimes_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}}y\otimes_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{+}_{0}}\otimes_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{T}}}_{0}}w=0
. To prove the claim, let us write
y=y^{\prime}x_{-\alpha}(u)
, where
u\in\varpi\mathcal{O}^{\times}
and
y^{\prime}\in\prod_{\beta}{\mathsf{U}}_{-\beta,1}
, where the product is over
\beta\in\Phi^{+}\smallsetminus\{\alpha\}
. Since
v\varpi\mathcal{O}\subseteq 4\mathcal{O}
, there exists
v\in\varpi^{2e-1}\mathcal{O}^{\times}
such that
(1+uv,\varpi)=-1
by Corollary
2.3
. Because
v\in\varpi^{2e-1}\mathcal{O}
and
y^{\prime}\in\prod_{\beta}{\mathsf{U}}_{-\beta,1}
where
\beta\in\Phi^{+}\smallsetminus\{\alpha\}
, we have
y^{\prime}\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(v)=\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(v)y^{\prime}
.

We then have

\displaystyle e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{+}_{0}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{2}}\otimes_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}}y\otimes_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{+}_{0}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{2}}\otimes_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{T}}}_{0}}w

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{+}_{0}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{2}}\otimes_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}}y\otimes_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}}\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(v)e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{+}_{0}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{2}}\otimes_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{T}}}_{0}}w\quad(\text{because $\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(v)e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{+}_{0}}=e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{+}_{0}}$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{+}_{0}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{2}}\otimes_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}}y^{\prime}\tilde{x}_{-\alpha}(u)\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(v)\otimes_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{+}_{0}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{2}}\otimes_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{T}}}_{0}}w

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{+}_{0}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{2}}\otimes_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}}y^{\prime}\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(v)\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(1+uv)\tilde{x}_{-\alpha}(u)\otimes_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{+}_{0}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{2}}\otimes_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{T}}}_{0}}w\quad(\text{Lemma \ref{+-IdemSwap}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{+}_{0}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{2}}\otimes_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}}\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(v)y^{\prime}\tilde{x}_{-\alpha}(u)\otimes_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{+}_{0}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{2}}\otimes_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{T}}}_{0}}w\quad(\text{by $y^{\prime}\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(v)=\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(v)y^{\prime}$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{+}_{0}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{2}}\otimes_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}}y\otimes_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{+}_{0}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{2}}\otimes_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{T}}}_{0}}w,

where for the last equality we used

e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{+}_{0}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{2}}\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(v)=e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{+}_{0}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{2}},

which holds because
\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(v)
normalizes
\mathsf{U}_{2}^{-}
and
\tilde{x}_{\alpha}(v)\in\mathsf{U}_{0}^{+}
. Thus
e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{+}_{0}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{2}}\otimes_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}}ye_{{\mathsf{U}}^{+}_{0}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{2}}\otimes_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{T}}}_{0}}w=0
, and so we have
r_{{\mathsf{U}}^{+}_{0},{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{2}}^{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}}\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}}^{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}}\sigma_{2}\cong\tau
as
\widetilde{{\mathsf{T}}}_{0}
-modules.

Therefore
\operatorname{End}_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}}(\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}}^{\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}}\sigma_{2})\cong\mathbb{C}
and the result follows.
∎

Proposition 5.32
.

As
\widetilde{\mathsf{I}}
-modules

\sigma\cong\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{I}}_{2}}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{I}}}\sigma_{2}.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.19
we know that
\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{I}}}(\sigma,\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{I}}_{2}}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{I}}}\sigma_{2})\neq 0
. By Lemma
5.31
we know that
\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{I}}_{2}}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{I}}}\sigma_{2}
is irreducible. Since
\sigma
is also irreducible the result follows by Schur’s Lemma.
∎

Lemma 5.33
.

Let
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}
be such that
0\leq\ell\leq e
. Then the groups
{\mathsf{U}}_{2e-2\ell}
and
{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{2\ell}
commute.

Proof.

Let
\alpha,\beta\in\Phi^{+}
be such that
\beta\neq\alpha
. Then for each
u,v\in\mathcal{O}
, one can readily derive the following two identities.

\displaystyle[x_{\alpha}(\varpi^{2e-2\ell}u),x_{-\beta}(\varpi^{2\ell}v)]=

\displaystyle 1\quad(\text{by \eqref{E:MR2'} viewed mod $\Gamma(2e)$});

\displaystyle x_{\alpha}(\varpi^{2e-2\ell}u)x_{-\alpha}(\varpi^{2\ell}v)=

\displaystyle x_{-\alpha}(\varpi^{2\ell}v)x_{\alpha}(\varpi^{2e-2\ell}u)\quad(\text{by Proposition \ref{+-IdemSwap}}).

We note that in the second identity there is no Hilbert symbol
(1+\varpi^{2e}uv,\varpi^{2\ell}v)
because
1+\varpi^{2e}uv\in R
and if
v\in\varpi\mathcal{O}
then
1+\varpi^{2e}uv\in 1+\varpi^{2e+1}\mathcal{O}
.
∎

Proposition 5.34
.

In the
\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}_{2}
-module
\sigma_{2}
we have

\sigma_{2}^{{\mathsf{U}}_{2e-2}}=\sigma_{2}.

Proof.

By the Iwahori factorization and Lemma
5.18
,
\sigma_{2}
is spanned by elements of the form
ye_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{2}}\otimes_{\widetilde{T}_{0}}w
, where
y\in{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{2}
and
w\in\tau
. Let
x\in\mathsf{U}_{2e-2}
, so that
xy=yx
by Lemma
5.33
. Hence

xye_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{2}}\otimes_{\widetilde{T}_{0}}w=yxe_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{2}}\otimes_{\widetilde{T}_{0}}w=ye_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{2}}\otimes_{\widetilde{T}_{0}}w,

because
e_{\mathsf{U}_{0}}
absorbs
x
. The result follows.
∎

5.6.
Brief wrap-up

Let us briefly summarize what we have achieved so far. We have first constructed a pseudo-spherical representation
\tau
of the compact group
\widetilde{T}_{0}
, which is a finite dimensional irreducible representation of
\widetilde{T}_{0}
that is trivial on
T_{R}
. We showed that the pseudo-spherical representations are Weyl invariant.

Next using parahoric induction, we obtained an irreducible representation
\sigma:=i_{\mathsf{U},\mathsf{U}_{1}^{-}}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{I}}}\tau
of the finite group
\widetilde{\mathsf{I}}\cong\widetilde{I}/\mathbf{s}(\Gamma(2e))
, which we can inflate to the Iwahori subgroup
\widetilde{I}
. This representation
\sigma
has two important properties: first, it remains irreducible when restricted to
\widetilde{\Gamma}(1)
, and in fact can be viewed as being parahorically induced; second,
\sigma
extends to
\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}=\widetilde{K}/\mathbf{s}(\Gamma(2e))
, which we also call a pseudo-spherical representation and denote it by the same symbol
\sigma
.

We can inflate
\sigma
to a representation of the compact group
\widetilde{K}
. As in Remark
Remark
, the group
\Gamma(2e)
is the maximal congruence subgroup of the hyperspecial maximal compact subgroup
K
that splits to
\widetilde{K}
with normal image. Hence the representation
\sigma
of
\widetilde{K}
can be considered as the covering analog of the trivial representation. Hence it makes sense to call
\sigma
a “pseudo-spherical” representation of
\widetilde{K}
. Indeed,
\sigma
does play a role of the trivial representation in the representation theory of covering groups.

5.7.
Finite Shimura correspondence

A first instance where one can see how our pseudo-spherical representation plays the role of the trivial representation is a “finite Shimura correspondence” originally established by Savin in
[
Sav12
, Theorem 4.1]
, when
F/\mathbb{Q}_{2}
is unramified. In this section, we generalize it to an arbitrary
F
. Indeed, this correspondence establishes an equivalence of the categories of representations of the finite group
K/\penalty\Gamma(1)
with a certain category of representations of
\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}
, and this equivalence of categories is precisely given by tensoring the pseudo-spherical representation as
\pi\mapsto\pi\otimes\sigma
for a representation
\pi
of
K/\penalty\Gamma(1)
.

To be precise, let
\mathcal{M}(G_{\kappa})
be the category of representations of
G_{\kappa}
, where we recall
\kappa
is the residue field of our
2
-adic field
F
. Let
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}})
be the full subcategory of smooth
\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}}
-modules
\widetilde{\pi}
such that
\widetilde{\pi}|_{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)}
is a nontrivial
\sigma
-isotypic
\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)
-module, or
\tilde{\pi}=0
. Let us then define two functors

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}

\displaystyle:\mathcal{M}(G_{\kappa})\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}}),\qquad\pi\mapsto\sigma\otimes\pi;

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\sigma}

\displaystyle:\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}})\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(G_{\kappa}),\qquad\widetilde{\pi}\mapsto\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)}(\sigma,\widetilde{\pi}).

Here,
\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}}
acts diagonally on
\sigma\otimes\pi
. Also
G_{\kappa}=\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}/\penalty\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)
acts on
\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)}(\sigma,\widetilde{\pi})
by “conjugation”, namely for each
g\in\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}/\penalty\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)
and
\phi\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)}(\sigma,\widetilde{\pi})

(g\cdot\phi)(v)=g\phi(g^{-1}v)

for all
v\in\widetilde{\pi}
, which is indeed well-defined.

The main theorem of this subsection is the following “finite Shimura correspondence”.

Theorem 5.35
.

The functors
\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}
and
\mathcal{G}_{\sigma}
are mutual quasi-inverses and hence define an equivalence of categories between
\mathcal{M}(G_{\kappa})
and
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}})
.

We first need a couple of elementary lemmas.

Lemma 5.36
.

Let
V_{1},V_{2},W
be vector spaces. If
V_{1},V_{2}
are finite dimensional, then the map

\operatorname{Hom}(V_{1},V_{2})\otimes W\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(V_{1},V_{2}\otimes W),\quad f\otimes w\mapsto(v\mapsto f(v)\otimes w),

is an isomorphism of vector spaces.

Furthermore, suppose that
G
is a group and
V_{1},V_{2},W
are
G
-modules. Let
G
act diagonally on tensor products and on Hom spaces via“conjugation”. Then this isomorphism is a
G
-module isomorphism.

Proof.

Elementary exercise.
∎

Lemma 5.37
.

Let
M\subseteq L
be finite groups. Let
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}
be (not necessarily finite dimensional)
L
-modules which are trivial on
M
, and let
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}
be irreducible (hence necessarily finite dimensional)
L
-modules which remain irreducible when restricted to
M
. If
\phi:\sigma_{1}\to\sigma_{2}
is an
L
-module isomorphism, then the map

\operatorname{Hom}_{L}(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{L}(\pi_{1}\otimes\sigma_{1},\pi_{2}\otimes\sigma_{2}),\quad f\mapsto f\otimes\phi,

is an isomorphism of vector spaces.

Proof.

By the tenor-Hom adjunction, we have

\operatorname{Hom}(\pi_{1}\otimes\sigma_{1},\pi_{2}\otimes\sigma_{2})\cong\operatorname{Hom}(\pi_{1},\operatorname{Hom}(\sigma_{1},\pi_{2}\otimes\sigma_{2})),

which gives

\operatorname{Hom}_{L}(\pi_{1}\otimes\sigma_{1},\pi_{2}\otimes\sigma_{2})\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{L}(\pi_{1},\operatorname{Hom}(\sigma_{1},\pi_{2}\otimes\sigma_{2})).

Since
M
acts trivially on
\pi_{1}
, the image of each element in
\operatorname{Hom}_{L}(\pi_{1},\operatorname{Hom}(\sigma_{1},\pi_{2}\otimes\sigma_{2}))
lies in the
M
-invariant subspace of
\operatorname{Hom}(\sigma_{1},\pi_{2}\otimes\sigma_{2})
, implying

\operatorname{Hom}_{L}(\pi_{1}\otimes\sigma_{1},\pi_{2}\otimes\sigma_{2})\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{L}(\pi_{1},\operatorname{Hom}_{M}(\sigma_{1},\pi_{2}\otimes\sigma_{2})).

Noting that both
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
are finite dimensional, Lemma
5.36
gives

\operatorname{Hom}_{M}(\sigma_{1},\pi_{2}\otimes\sigma_{2})\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{M}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\otimes\pi_{2}\cong\mathbb{C}\otimes\pi_{2}\cong\pi_{2},

where we used
\operatorname{Hom}_{M}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\cong\mathbb{C}
by Schur’s lemma. Therefore, we have

\operatorname{Hom}_{L}(\pi_{1}\otimes\sigma_{1},\pi_{2}\otimes\sigma_{2})\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{L}(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}).

All of the preceding isomorphisms are explicit once
\phi
is used to normalize the isomorphism
\operatorname{Hom}_{M}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\cong\mathbb{C}
coming from Schur’s lemma, and it can be checked directly that
f\in\operatorname{Hom}_{L}(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
corresponds to
f\otimes\phi\in\operatorname{Hom}_{L}(\pi_{1}\otimes\sigma_{1},\pi_{2}\otimes\sigma_{2})
.
∎

Now, we are ready to prove the theorem.

Proof of Theorem
5.35
.

Noting that
G_{\kappa}=\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}/\penalty\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)
, we can identify the category
\mathcal{M}(G_{\kappa})
with the category of
\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}
-modules which are trivial on
\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)
. Then the above lemma (with
L=\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}
,
M=\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)
and
\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{2}=\sigma
) means that the functor
\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}
is fully faithful. (Note that we necessarily have
\operatorname{Hom}_{L}(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})=\operatorname{Hom}_{L/\penalty M}(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
in the above lemma.)

Hence to show
\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}
is an equivalence of the categories, it suffices to show that the functor
\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}
is essentially surjective on the objects. But for each
\tilde{\pi}\in\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\widetilde{\mathsf{K}})
, we have

\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}\circ\mathcal{G}_{\sigma}(\tilde{\pi})=\sigma\otimes\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)}(\sigma,\tilde{\pi}).

Now, we have the natural
\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}
-isomorphism

\sigma\otimes\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)}(\sigma,\tilde{\pi})\longrightarrow\tilde{\pi},\quad v\otimes f\mapsto f(v),

because
\tilde{\pi}|_{\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)}
is
\sigma
-isotypic. Hence the functor
\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}
is essentially surjective on the objects.

Now that we have shown that
\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}\circ\mathcal{G}_{\sigma}\cong\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\widetilde{\mathsf{K}})}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}
is an equivalence of the categories, we know that
\mathcal{G}_{\sigma}
is a quasi-inverse of
\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}
.

∎

The next proposition shows that the equivalence of categories of Theorem
5.35
commutes with induction in certain cases.

Proposition 5.38
.

Assume
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}
is such that
\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)\subseteq\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}
. Let
\pi
be a representation of
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}
that is trivial on
\widetilde{{\mathsf{\Gamma}}}(1)
, so that
\pi
is really a representation of
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}/\widetilde{{\mathsf{\Gamma}}}(1)\hookrightarrow G_{\kappa}=\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}/\penalty\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)
.

(1)

The
\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}}
-module
\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}}^{\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}}}(\pi\otimes\sigma)
is an object in
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}})
.

(2)

The map

\phi:\left(\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}}^{\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}}}\pi\right)\otimes\sigma\longrightarrow\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}}^{\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}}}(\pi\otimes\sigma),\quad f\otimes w\mapsto(k\mapsto f(k)\otimes kw),

is a surjective map of
\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}}
-modules. Furthermore, if
\pi
is finite dimensional, then both sides have the same dimension, so that
\pi
is an isomorphism.

(3)

The map

\operatorname{End}_{G_{\kappa}}(\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}}^{\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}}}\pi)\rightarrow\operatorname{End}_{\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}}}(\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}}^{\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}}}(\pi\otimes\sigma)),\quad F\mapsto\phi\circ(F\otimes id_{\sigma})\circ\phi^{-1},

is an isomorphism of
\mathbb{C}
-algebras.

Proof.

(
1
) follows from the Mackey theory and
\widetilde{{\mathsf{\Gamma}}}(1)\cap\,^{g}\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}=\widetilde{{\mathsf{\Gamma}}}(1)
for any
g\in\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}}
.

We prove (
2
). A direct computation shows that the map is well defined and a
\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}}
-module homomorphism. We will show this is surjective. Let
W
be the subspace of
\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}}^{\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}}}\pi
consisting of functions with support contained in
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}
. Let
W^{\prime}
be the subspace of
\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}}^{\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}}}(\pi\otimes\sigma)
consisting of functions with support contained in
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}
. Recall that as
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}
-modules
W\cong\pi
and
W^{\prime}\cong\pi\otimes\sigma
. In both cases the isomorphism is given by evaluation at the identity. Under these identifications, one can see that
\phi:W\otimes\sigma\rightarrow W^{\prime}
becomes the identity map. Thus
W^{\prime}
is in the image of
\phi
. Since the index
[\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}}:\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}]
is finite and
\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}}^{\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}}}(\pi\otimes\sigma)
is generated by
W^{\prime}
as a
\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}}
-module, it follows that
\phi
is surjective.

(
3
) follows from (
2
) and Theorem
5.35
.
∎

The isomorphism of Proposition
5.38
(
3
) has a very important property in that it preserves support, when viewed as an isomorphism of Hecke algebras. We return to this point in §
6.2
after we introduce the notation for Hecke algebras.

5.8.
Whittaker Invariants

In this subsection we record a few simple results on twisted
{\mathsf{U}}_{0}
-invariants for representations in
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}})
. (Though we will not use it for our main theorems, it is certainly of some interest.) Recall that
\sigma^{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}\cong\tau
as
\widetilde{\mathsf{T}}_{0}
-modules.

Corollary 5.39
.

Let
\psi\in\operatorname{Hom}({\mathsf{U}}_{0},\mathbb{C}^{\times})
be a nontrivial character that is trivial on
{\mathsf{U}}_{1}
. Then

\sigma^{({\mathsf{U}}_{0},\psi)}=0.

Proof.

This follows directly from Lemma
5.23
because
\sigma^{({\mathsf{U}}_{0},\psi)}\subseteq\sigma^{\mathsf{U}_{1}}
.
∎

Thus we see that for
\sigma
to support a
\psi
-Whittaker functional the conductor of
\psi
must be strictly larger than
1
.

Corollary 5.40
.

Let
\pi
be a
G_{\kappa}
-module and let
\psi\in\operatorname{Hom}({\mathsf{U}}_{0},\mathbb{C}^{\times})
. Then

(\pi\otimes\sigma)^{({\mathsf{U}}_{0},\psi)}=\oplus_{\psi=\psi_{1}\psi_{2}}\pi^{({\mathsf{U}}_{0},\psi_{1})}\otimes\sigma^{({\mathsf{U}}_{0},\psi_{2})},

where the direct sum is over all
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\in\operatorname{Hom}({\mathsf{U}}_{0},\mathbb{C}^{\times})
such that
\psi=\psi_{1}\psi_{2}
and
\psi_{1}
is trivial on
{\mathsf{U}}_{1}
.
In particular, if
\psi_{1}
is nondegenerate,
\psi_{2}
is trivial, and
\pi
is
\psi_{1}
-generic, then

\dim(\pi\otimes\sigma)^{({\mathsf{U}}_{0},\psi)}\geq\dim\tau=\left|Y/\penalty\widetilde{Y}\right|^{\frac{ef}{2}}.

From Corollary
5.40
we see that multiplicity one for Whittaker functionals fails for the finite group
\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}
. Thus it would be interesting to study the associated Gelfand-Graev representation in this finite group setting. Such an investigation will likely been needed to generalize the results of
[
GGK24
,
GGK25
]
to the wild double covers studied in this paper.

6.
Hecke algebras

Once we have constructed the Iwahori type
(\widetilde{I},\sigma)
, which will be shown to be indeed a type in the sense of Bushnell-Kutzko, we can form the Iwahori Hecke algebra

\mathcal{H}(\widetilde{G},\widetilde{I};\sigma).

In this section, we will prove that the support of this Hecke algebra is
\widetilde{W}_{\mathrm{ea}}
(see §
3.5
for
\widetilde{W}_{\mathrm{ea}}
) and establish its IM-presentation.

6.1.
Basic theory of Hecke algebras

In this subsection, we catalog basics of representations of compact groups and Hecke algebras to the extent necessary for our purposes, and fix our notation and conventions.

Let
G
be a locally compact group and
K\subseteq G
an open compact subgroup. Let
(\pi,V)
be a finite dimensional (not necessarily irreducible) representation of
K
. We define the
\pi
-spherical Hecke algebra of
G
by

\mathcal{H}(G,K;\pi):=\{f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G,\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb{C}}(\pi^{\vee}))\,:\,f(k_{1}gk_{2})=\pi^{\vee}(k_{1})f(g)\pi^{\vee}(k_{2})\text{ for all $k_{1},k_{2}\in K$}\}.

This is an algebra under convolution. Specifically, for
f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathcal{H}(G,K;\pi)
we have

f_{1}*f_{2}:=\int_{G}f_{1}(h)f_{2}(h^{-1}g)dg=\int_{G}f_{1}(gh^{-1})f_{2}(h)dg,

where the Haar measure on
G
is normalized so that the measure of
K
is
1
.

Let
(\rho,W)
be a
G
-module. The Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}(G,K;\pi^{\vee})
acts on
\operatorname{Hom}_{K}(V,W)
on the right in the following way. Let
f\in\mathcal{H}(G,K;\pi^{\vee})
and
A\in\operatorname{Hom}_{K}(V,W)
. Then

(A*f)(v):=\int_{G}\rho(g^{-1})A(f(g)v)dg.

The Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}(G,K;\pi^{\vee})
also has a a canonical left action on
\operatorname{ind}_{K}^{G}\pi
defined by

f*F(g):=\int_{G}f(x)F(x^{-1}g)dx,

where
f\in\mathcal{H}(G,K;\pi^{\vee})
and
F\in\operatorname{ind}_{K}^{G}\pi
. The following lemma is well-known. (See, for example,
[
BK98
, (2.6)]
.)

Lemma 6.1
.

The canonical left action of
\mathcal{H}(G,K;\pi^{\vee})
on
\operatorname{ind}_{K}^{G}\pi
induces an isomorphism of
\mathbb{C}
-algebras
\mathcal{H}(G,K;\pi)\cong\operatorname{End}_{G}(\operatorname{ind}_{K}^{G}\pi)
.

For each
g\in G
, we let

\mathcal{H}(G,K;\pi)_{g}

be the vector space of all
f\in\mathcal{H}(G,K;\pi)
that vanish outside the double coset
KgK
.

A very important fact we repeatedly use in this paper is

Lemma 6.2
.

For each
g\in G
, we have a canonical isomorphism

\operatorname{Hom}_{K\cap{{}^{g}K}}(\;{{}^{g}\pi},\pi)=\operatorname{Hom}_{K\cap K^{g}}(\;\pi,\pi^{g})\cong\mathcal{H}(G,K;\pi^{\vee})_{g}\cong\mathcal{H}(G,K;\pi)_{g^{-1}}

as vector spaces.

Proof.

See
[
BK93
, (4.1.1) Proposition, p.144]
.
∎

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
K\subseteq G
be as above and let
H
be an open compact group such that
H\subseteq K\subseteq G
. Let
\rho
be a finite dimensional representation of
H
. We have a natural isomorphism

\mathcal{H}(G,K;\operatorname{Ind}_{H}^{K}\rho)\cong\mathcal{H}(G,H;\rho)

such that for each
g\in G

\mathcal{H}(G,K;\operatorname{Ind}_{H}^{K}\rho)_{g}\cong\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}h\in H\backslash G/\penalty H\\
KhK=KgK\end{subarray}}\mathcal{H}(G,H;\rho)_{h},

where the sum is over all the double cosets
HhH
such that
KhK=KgK
.

Proof.

See
[
BK93
, (4.1.5) Corollary, p.146]
.
∎

The next lemma will be useful for relating some of the Hom-spaces introduced above.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
G
and
H
be groups and let
\phi:G\rightarrow H
be a group isomorphism. Let
\sigma,\tau
be
G
-modules. Then the Hom-spaces
\operatorname{Hom}_{G}(\sigma,\tau)
and
\operatorname{Hom}_{H}(\,^{\phi}\sigma,\,^{\phi}\tau)
are equal as subspaces of
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(\sigma,\tau)
.

Next we describe some results on twisting Hecke algebras by automorphisms.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
\phi\in\operatorname{Aut}(G)
. Then

(1)

The map
\mathcal{H}(G,K,\pi)\rightarrow\mathcal{H}(G,\phi(K),\,^{\phi}\pi)
defined by
f\mapsto f\circ\phi^{-1}
is an isomorphism of
\mathbb{C}
-algebras.

Suppose further that
\phi(K)=K
and
\,{}^{\phi}\pi\cong\pi
, and let
A_{\phi}\in\operatorname{GL}(\pi^{\vee})
be such that
A_{\phi}\pi^{\vee}(k)=\pi^{\vee}(\phi^{-1}(k))A_{\phi}
. Then

(2)

\mathcal{H}(G,K,\,^{\phi}\pi)=A_{\phi}\mathcal{H}(G,K,\pi)A_{\phi}^{-1}
(we emphasize that this is an equality of spaces of functions and not just an isomorphism of algebras);

(3)

\mathcal{H}(G,K,\,\pi)_{\phi(g)}\neq 0
if and only if
\mathcal{H}(G,K,\pi)_{g}\neq 0
.

6.2.
Finite Hecke algebra Shimura Correspondence

In this subsection we prove that the finite Shimura correspondence of Theorem
5.35
preserves the support of Hecke algebras. Let us set up the notation first. Let
H\subseteq G
be such that
\Gamma(1)\subseteq H\subseteq K
, so that
\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)\subseteq\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}
. Denote by
H_{\kappa}
the image of
H
in
G_{\kappa}=K/\penalty\Gamma(1)
, so that we have

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{K}/\penalty\mathbf{s}(\Gamma(2e))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{\kappa}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K/\penalty\Gamma(1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}/\penalty\mathbf{s}(\Gamma(2e))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\kappa}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H/\penalty\Gamma(1)\hbox to0.0pt{\,.\hss}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}

Fix a representation
\pi
of
H_{\kappa}
, which we also view as a representation of
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}
by inflating via the above surjection
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}\twoheadrightarrow H_{\kappa}
.

For each
x\in G_{\kappa}
, we let
\tilde{x}\in\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}
be a preimage under the surjection
\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}\twoheadrightarrow G_{\kappa}
. The choice of
\tilde{x}
is not unique, but if
\tilde{x}^{\prime}
is another choice, then

(6.6)

\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}\,\tilde{x}\,\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}=\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}\,\tilde{x}^{\prime}\,\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}.

because
\tilde{x}^{\prime}=\tilde{x}\gamma
for some
\gamma\in\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)
.

We then have

Theorem 6.7
.

With the above notation, we have a
\mathbb{C}
-algebra isomorphism

\mathcal{H}(G_{\kappa},H_{\kappa};\pi)\cong\mathcal{H}(\widetilde{\mathsf{K}},\widetilde{\mathsf{H}};\pi\otimes\sigma)

which restricts to an isomorphism

\mathcal{H}(G_{\kappa},H_{\kappa};\pi)_{x}\cong\mathcal{H}(\widetilde{\mathsf{K}},\widetilde{\mathsf{H}};\pi\otimes\sigma)_{\tilde{x}}

of
\mathbb{C}
-vector spaces for all
x\in G_{\kappa}
; namely the isomorphism is a support-preserving. (Note that the space
\mathcal{H}(\widetilde{\mathsf{K}},\widetilde{\mathsf{H}};\pi\otimes\sigma)_{\tilde{x}}
is independent of the choice of
\tilde{x}
thanks to (
6.6
).)

Proof.

We already know the isomorphisms

(6.8)

\mathcal{H}(G_{\kappa},H_{\kappa};\pi)\xrightarrow{\;\sim\;}\operatorname{End}_{G_{\kappa}}(\operatorname{Ind}_{H_{\kappa}}^{G_{\kappa}}\pi)\xrightarrow{\;\sim\;}\operatorname{End}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}(\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}(\pi\otimes\sigma))\xrightarrow{\;\sim\;}\mathcal{H}(\widetilde{\mathsf{K}},\widetilde{\mathsf{H}};\pi\otimes\sigma),

where the first and the third isomorphisms are as in Lemma
6.1
, and the second isomorphism is by Theorem
5.35
and Proposition
5.38
(
3
). Hence we have the isomorphism of the two Hecke algebras.

By Lemma
6.2
, we know that

\mathcal{H}(G_{\kappa},H_{\kappa};\pi)_{x^{-1}}\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{H_{\kappa}\cap{\,{}^{x}H_{\kappa}}}(\,^{x}\pi,\pi){\quad\text{and}\quad}\mathcal{H}(\widetilde{\mathsf{K}},\widetilde{\mathsf{H}};\pi\otimes\sigma)_{\tilde{x}^{-1}}\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}\cap{\,{}^{\tilde{x}}\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}}}(\,^{\tilde{x}}\pi\otimes\sigma,\pi\otimes\sigma).

Since
\operatorname{Hom}_{H_{\kappa}\cap{\,{}^{x}H_{\kappa}}}(\,^{x}\pi,\pi)\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}_{\kappa}\cap{\,{}^{x}\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}_{\kappa}}}(\,^{x}\pi,\pi)
, Lemma
5.37
(with
M=\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)
and
L=\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}_{\kappa}\cap{\,{}^{x}\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}_{\kappa}}
) gives

\operatorname{Hom}_{H_{\kappa}\cap{\,{}^{x}H_{\kappa}}}(\,^{x}\pi,\pi)\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}\cap{\,{}^{\tilde{x}}\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}}}(\,^{\tilde{x}}\pi\otimes\sigma,\pi\otimes\sigma).

Here, it should be mentioned that
{}^{\tilde{x}}\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)=\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)
, so
\widetilde{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(1)\subseteq\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}_{\kappa}\cap{\,{}^{x}\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}_{\kappa}}
. Hence we have

\mathcal{H}(G_{\kappa},H_{\kappa};\pi)_{x^{-1}}\cong\mathcal{H}(\widetilde{\mathsf{K}},\widetilde{\mathsf{H}};\pi\otimes\sigma)_{\tilde{x}^{-1}}.

It remains to show that this isomorphism is induced by the composite of the three isomorphisms of (
6.8
). But this is a matter of book keeping since these three isomorphisms are fairly explicit, and is left to the reader.
∎

This support preserving property has significant consequences when we specialize to the case of parabolic induction. Let

P_{\kappa}=M_{\kappa}N_{\kappa}\subseteq G_{\kappa}

be a parabolic subgroup of
G_{\kappa}
, where
M_{\kappa}
and
N_{\kappa}
are the Levi part and the unipotent subgroup, respectively. Then there exists a subgroup
P\subseteq K
such that
P\supseteq\Gamma(1)
and the corresponding
\widetilde{\mathsf{P}}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}
is the preimage of
P_{\kappa}
under the surjection
\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}\twoheadrightarrow G_{\kappa}
. We then have

Corollary 6.9
.

Let
\pi
be a representation of
M_{\kappa}
which we inflate to a representation of
P_{\kappa}
. We then have a support-preserving
\mathbb{C}
-algebra algebra isomorphism

\mathcal{H}(G_{\kappa},P_{\kappa};\pi)\cong\mathcal{H}(\widetilde{\mathsf{K}},\widetilde{\mathsf{P}};\pi\otimes\sigma).

In particular, if
B_{\kappa}
is the Borel subgroup, we have

\mathcal{H}(G_{\kappa},B_{\kappa};\pi)\cong\mathcal{H}(\widetilde{\mathsf{K}},\widetilde{\mathsf{I}};\pi\otimes\sigma).

Proof.

Just let
H=P
in the above theorem.
∎

Hecke algebras of the form
\mathcal{H}(G_{\kappa},P_{\kappa};\pi)
, where
\pi
is cuspidal, were thoroughly studied in
[
HL80
]
. We highlight a couple of their results. (Here we will be translating the statements of
[
HL80
]
which primarily use the language of endomorphism algebras into the language of Hecke algebras.) First,
[
HL80
, Proposition (3.9)]
(for which they refer to Springer
[
Spr75
, pp. 635-636]
) states that the Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}(G_{\kappa},P_{\kappa};\pi)
has a basis indexed by a subset of
P_{\kappa}
-double cosets such that the support of a basis function
T_{w}
is the corresponding
P_{\kappa}
-double coset
P_{\kappa}\,w\,P_{\kappa}
. Second, in terms of this basis,
[
HL80
, Theorem (4.14)]
describes a presentation for
\mathcal{H}(G_{\kappa},P_{\kappa};\pi)
(roughly braid relations and quadratic relations). The isomorphism of Corollary
6.9
then implies that
\mathcal{H}(\widetilde{\mathsf{K}},\widetilde{\mathsf{P}};\pi\otimes\sigma)
inherits the presentation of
\mathcal{H}(G_{\kappa},P_{\kappa};\pi)
, but this isomorphism also preserves support. Thus if
\widetilde{T}_{\tilde{w}}\in\mathcal{H}(\widetilde{\mathsf{K}},\widetilde{\mathsf{P}};\pi\otimes\sigma)
corresponds to
T_{w}\in\mathcal{H}(G_{\kappa},P_{\kappa};\pi)
, then we know that
\operatorname{supp}(\widetilde{T}_{\tilde{w}})=\widetilde{\mathsf{P}}\,\tilde{w}\,\widetilde{\mathsf{P}}
and under the surjection
\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}\twoheadrightarrow G_{\kappa}
we have
\tilde{w}\mapsto w
.

6.3.
Two isomorphic Hecke algebras
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{H}_{2}

Now, let us introduce the Iwahori Hecke algebra we will study. Recall that we have first constructed a pseudo-spherical representation
\tau
of the compact group
\widetilde{T}_{0}
. The kernel of
\tau
contains
T_{R}
, which allows us to view
\tau
as a representation of the finite group
\widetilde{\mathsf{T}}=\widetilde{T}_{0}/\penalty T_{R}
, which we also denote by
\tau
. By parahoric induction, we have constructed a representation
\sigma=i_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0},{\mathsf{U}}_{1}^{-}}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{I}}}\tau
of the finite group
\widetilde{\mathsf{I}}=\widetilde{I}/\penalty\mathbf{s}(\Gamma(2e))
. By inflating
\sigma
by the surjection
\widetilde{I}\twoheadrightarrow\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}}
, we view
\sigma
as a representation of
\widetilde{I}
, which we also call
\sigma
. We then define

\mathcal{H}:=\mathcal{H}(\widetilde{G},\widetilde{I};\sigma).

For our analysis, however, it will be convenient to also consider another Hecke algebra constructed using the congruence subgroup

I_{2}:=\Gamma_{0}(2).

Note the inclusions
I_{2}\subseteq I\subseteq K
. By using the Iwahori decomposition
(U_{0},T_{0},U_{2}^{-})
of
I_{2}
, we consider another parahoric induction

\sigma_{2}:=i_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0},{\mathsf{U}}_{2}^{-}}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{I}}_{2}}\tau,

which we often view as a representation of
\widetilde{I}_{2}
by inflating via
\widetilde{I}_{2}\twoheadrightarrow\widetilde{\mathsf{I}}_{2}
. We then define

\mathcal{H}_{2}:=\mathcal{H}(\widetilde{G},\widetilde{I}_{2};\sigma_{2}).

These two Hecke algebras are actually isomorphic:

Proposition 6.10
.

As
\mathbb{C}
-algebras

\mathcal{H}\cong\mathcal{H}_{2}.

Proof.

The claim follows directly from Proposition
5.32
and Lemma
6.3
. ∎

Remark
.

We work with
\mathcal{H}_{2}
primarily because the group
\widetilde{I}_{2}
behaves better with the affine reflection
w_{\mathrm{af}}
than
\widetilde{I}
. For example, the group generated by
\widetilde{I}_{2}
and
w_{\mathrm{af}}
is compact, whereas the one generated by
\widetilde{I}
and
w_{\mathrm{aff}}
is not. Accordingly, when we work with
w_{\mathrm{af}}
, we often work with
\mathcal{H}_{2}
.

6.4.
Support of
\mathcal{H}
: multiplicity one

In this subsection we begin our study of the support of
\mathcal{H}
. Let us start with

Proposition 6.11
.

For each
g\in G
, we have

\dim\mathcal{H}_{g}\leq 1.

Proof.

First note that by Lemma
6.2
it suffices to compute the dimension of the Hom-space
\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{I}\cap\,^{g}\widetilde{I}}(\,^{g}\sigma,\sigma)
. Second, note that if
\widetilde{I}g\widetilde{I}=\widetilde{I}g^{\prime}\widetilde{I}
then
\dim\mathcal{H}_{g^{-1}}=\dim\mathcal{H}_{{g^{\prime}}^{-1}}
, and hence by the Iwahori-Bruhat decomposition, we may assume
g\in N_{\widetilde{G}}(\widetilde{T})
.

Let
\Gamma:=\Gamma(1)
. Since
\Gamma\subseteq I
we have
\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{I}\cap\,^{g}\widetilde{I}}(\,^{g}\sigma,\sigma)\subseteq\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{\Gamma}\cap\,^{g}\widetilde{\Gamma}}(\,^{g}\sigma,\sigma)
. Thus it suffices to prove

\dim\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{\Gamma}\cap\,^{g}\widetilde{\Gamma}}(\,^{g}\sigma,\sigma)\leq 1,

where we recall from Proposition
5.24
(
2
) that
\sigma|_{\widetilde{\Gamma}}
is still irreducible. (The advantage of
\Gamma
over
I
is that
\Gamma
is normalized by
\mathcal{W}
.)

As
g\in N_{\widetilde{G}}(\widetilde{T})
there exists
w\in\mathcal{W}
and
t\in\widetilde{T}
such that
g=tw
. Then by Proposition
5.24
we know
{}^{w}\sigma\cong\sigma
as
\widetilde{\Gamma}
-modules, which implies that
\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{\Gamma}\cap\,^{g}\widetilde{\Gamma}}(\,^{g}\sigma,\sigma)\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{\Gamma}\cap\,^{t}\widetilde{\Gamma}}(\,^{t}\sigma,\sigma)
. Hence we may assume
g=t\in\widetilde{T}
.

Next, note that there exists
w_{0}\in\mathcal{W}
such that

(w_{0}tw_{0})U_{j}(w_{0}tw_{0})^{-1}\subseteq U_{j}

for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, where we recall the notation
U_{j}=U_{j}^{+}
from (
2.8
). By Lemma
6.4
and Proposition
5.24
(
1
) and (
2
), we have

\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{\Gamma}\cap\,^{t}\widetilde{\Gamma}}(\,^{t}\sigma,\sigma)\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{\Gamma}\cap\,^{w_{0}tw_{0}^{-1}}\widetilde{\Gamma}}(\,^{w_{0}tw_{0}^{-1}}\sigma,\sigma).

Thus we may assume
g=t\in\widetilde{T}
such that
tU_{j}t^{-1}\subseteq U_{j}
for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}
.
