In this case, we see that
\widetilde{\Gamma}\cap\,^{t}\widetilde{\Gamma}
has an Iwahori decomposition of the form

\widetilde{\Gamma}\cap\,^{t}\widetilde{\Gamma}\cong U^{-}_{1}\times\widetilde{T}_{1}\times\,^{t}U_{1}.

Specifically, we have
U^{-}_{1}\subseteq\widetilde{\Gamma}\cap\,^{t}\widetilde{\Gamma}
, and
\,{}^{t}U_{1}\subseteq U_{1}
.

Let
A\subseteq\,^{t}\sigma
be the span of the elements of the form
e_{U_{1}}e_{U^{-}_{1}}\otimes_{\widetilde{T}_{1}}w
, where
w\in\tau
. Note that
A
is a
\widetilde{T}_{1}
-module such that
A\subseteq\,^{t}\sigma^{\,{}^{t}U_{1}}=\sigma^{U_{1}}
because the idempotent
e_{U_{1}}
absorbs the elements in
U_{1}
. Also note that, as a
\widetilde{\Gamma}\cap\,^{t}\widetilde{\Gamma}
-module,
\,{}^{t}\sigma
is generated by
A
, because of the above Iwahori decomposition of
\widetilde{\Gamma}\cap\,^{t}\widetilde{\Gamma}
and the containment
\widetilde{\Gamma}\cap\,^{t}\widetilde{\Gamma}\supset U^{-}_{1}
. Thus by restriction from
\,{}^{t}\sigma
to
A
we get an injective map

\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{\Gamma}\cap\,^{t}\widetilde{\Gamma}}(\,^{t}\sigma,\sigma)\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{T}_{1}}(A,\sigma^{U_{1}}).

Now, as
\widetilde{T}_{1}
-modules,
\sigma^{U_{1}}\cong\tau
by Proposition
5.9
(4). Therefore
\dim\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{T}_{1}}(A,\sigma^{U_{1}})\leq 1
, since
\dim A\leq\dim\tau
, proving the proposition.
∎

The following gives a sufficient condition for
\mathcal{H}_{g}
to be
0
.

Proposition 6.12
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{H}
,
w\in\mathcal{W}
and
y\in Y\smallsetminus\widetilde{Y}
. Let
\tilde{y}(\varpi)\in\widetilde{T}
be any preimage of
y(\varpi)\in T
. Then
f(\tilde{y}(\varpi)w)=0
. In particular,

\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{y}(\varpi)w}=0.

Proof.

Since
y\notin\widetilde{Y}
, there exists a simple root
\alpha\in\Delta
such that
\langle\alpha,y\rangle\notin 2\mathbb{Z}
. Now, let
t\in(1+\varpi^{2e}\mathcal{O})\smallsetminus\mathcal{O}^{\times 2}
. Then since
1+\varpi^{2e}\mathcal{O}\subseteq R
, we know
(t,\varpi)=-1
by Corollary
2.3
. Thus

\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(t)\tilde{y}(\varpi)=(t,\varpi^{\langle\alpha,y\rangle})\tilde{y}(\varpi)\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(t)=-\tilde{y}(\varpi)\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(t).

Recalling that
\widetilde{K}
normalizes
\Gamma(2e)
, we have

w^{-1}\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(t)w\in T_{2e}.

Since
T_{2e}
acts trivially on
\sigma
, we have

f(\tilde{y}(\varpi)w)=f(\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(t)\tilde{y}(\varpi)w)=-f(\tilde{y}(\varpi)ww^{-1}\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(t)w)=-f(\tilde{y}(\varpi)w).

Hence
f(\tilde{y}(\varpi)w)=0
.
∎

6.5.
Linear Hecke algebra for
I_{2}

Here we take an interlude to study a linear Hecke algebra which will help us investigate several aspects of
\mathcal{H}_{2}
. (A similar idea is used by Savin
[
Sav04
]
in the tame case.) In particular, we record generalizations of the results of
[
Kar21
, Section 5]
to the present setting. All the results generalize, but we only record those that we directly use later. The proofs carry over without change once the correct generalization is used, so we will be brief and refer the reader to
[
Kar21
, Section 5]
for more details. The main point is that the mod
4
congruence conditions in the
F=\mathbb{Q}_{2}
setting of
[
Kar21
]
should become mod
\varpi^{2}
congruence conditions in the present setting of an arbitrary
2
-adic field (as opposed to mod
\varpi^{2e}
). Note also that the congruence subgroup
I_{2}:=\Gamma_{0}(2)
in the current paper corresponds to
\Gamma_{0}(4)
in
[
Kar21
]
.

Let
\underline{\mathcal{H}}:=C_{c}^{\infty}(I_{2}\backslash G/I_{2})
, the algebra of
I_{2}
-biinvariant compactly supported functions on
G
. The space
\underline{\mathcal{H}}
has multiplication given by convolution where we normalize the Haar measure so that
I_{2}
has measure
1
.

We consider a length function
\ell_{2}:G\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
defined by

q^{\ell_{2}(g)}:=[I_{2}gI_{2}:I_{2}].

By composing with any set theoretic section
W_{\mathrm{af}}\rightarrow N_{G}(T)
, we can consider the function

\ell_{2}:W_{\mathrm{af}}\rightarrow N_{G}(T)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},

which we also denote by
\ell_{2}
, because one can see that this composite does not depend on the choice of the section because
T_{0}\subseteq I_{2}
.

Now we state relevant generalizations as follows, for which we recall the notation
\widetilde{\Omega},\widetilde{W}_{\mathrm{aff}},\widetilde{W}_{\mathrm{ea}}
and
\tilde{\ell}
from §
3.5
.

Lemma 6.13
.

Let
w_{1}\in\widetilde{\Omega}
and
w_{2}\in\widetilde{W}_{\mathrm{af}}
. Then
\ell_{2}(w_{1}w_{2})=\ell_{2}(w_{2})
and
\mathbf{s}(w_{1})\in N_{G}(I_{2})
.

Proof.

This generalizes
[
Kar21
, Lemma 5.4]
and can be proved in the same way.
∎

Proposition 6.14
.

Let
g_{1},g_{2},g,\delta\in G
be such that
\ell_{2}(g_{1}g_{2})=\ell_{2}(g_{1})+\ell_{2}(g_{2})
. Suppose that the coset
\delta I_{2}
satisfies the two conditions

•

\delta I_{2}\subseteq I_{2}g_{1}I_{2}
;

•

(\delta I_{2})^{-1}g\subseteq I_{2}g_{2}I_{2}
.

Then

•

I_{2}gI_{2}=I_{2}g_{1}g_{2}I_{2}
;

•

\delta I_{2}=g_{1}I_{2}
.

Proof.

This follows by adapting the proof of
[
Kar21
, Proposition 5.2]
and the subsequent remark. We note that this proof uses
\underline{\mathcal{H}}
.
∎

Theorem 6.15
.

Let
w\in\widetilde{W}_{\mathrm{ea}}
. Then

\ell_{2}(w)=2\tilde{\ell}(w).

Proof.

This is proved as in
[
Kar21
, Theorem 5.5]
by studying the structure of
\underline{\mathcal{H}}
.
∎

6.6.
Support: affine simple reflections

Recall that the group
\widetilde{W}_{\mathrm{af}}
is generated by the simple affine reflections
w_{\mathrm{af}},w_{\alpha_{1}},\dots,w_{\alpha_{n}}
, where
w_{\mathrm{af}}
is defined as in (
3.15
). In this section, we will show that our Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}
is supported on these elements, namely

Proposition 6.16
.

We have

\mathcal{H}_{w_{\alpha}}\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{I}\cap{{}^{w_{\alpha}}\widetilde{I}}}(\,^{w_{\alpha}}\sigma,\sigma)\cong\mathbb{C}

for all
\alpha\in\Delta\cup\{\mathrm{af}\}
.

Since we already know
\dim\mathcal{H}_{w_{\alpha}}\leq 1
(Proposition
6.11
) and
\mathcal{H}\cong\mathcal{H}_{2}
(Proposition
6.10
), and since
\widetilde{I}w_{\alpha}^{-1}\widetilde{I}=\widetilde{I}w_{\alpha}\widetilde{I}
and so
\mathcal{H}_{w_{\alpha}}=\mathcal{H}_{w_{\alpha}^{-1}}
, we know from Lemma
6.3
that Proposition
6.16
above is implied by the following proposition.

Proposition 6.17
.

We have

(\mathcal{H}_{2})_{w_{\alpha}}\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{I}_{2}\cap{{}^{w_{\alpha}}\widetilde{I}_{2}}}(\,^{w_{\alpha}}\sigma_{2},\sigma_{2})\cong\mathbb{C}

for all
\alpha\in\Delta\cup\{\mathrm{af}\}
.

Before we begin the proof we will set up some preliminary results. We focus on the case of
\alpha=\mathrm{af}
. The case of
\alpha\in\Delta
can be proved similarly. We omit these details because Proposition
6.16
also follows directly from the finite Shimura correspondence. Alternatively, since we know that the
\widetilde{I}
-module
\sigma
extends to a
\widetilde{K}
-module, we may apply the method of
[
TW18
]
to prove Proposition
6.16
for
\alpha\in\Delta
.

It will be convenient to have a slightly different model for the representation
\sigma_{2}
. Let

(6.18)

\sigma_{2}^{p}:=i_{U_{0},U^{-}_{2}}^{\widetilde{I}_{2}}\tau

be the
\widetilde{I}_{2}
-module constructed by parahoric induction, where
\tau
is the representation
\widetilde{T}_{0}
. Here, the superscript
p
is for “profinite” as opposed to “finite”. (Recall that
\sigma_{2}
was first defined as a representation of the
finite
group
\widetilde{\mathsf{I}}_{2}
via the finite group version of parahoric induction and then inflated to the
profinite
group
\widetilde{I}_{2}
. Here,
\sigma_{2}^{p}
is defined by the profinite version of parahoric induction.) But this is indeed another model of
\sigma_{2}
.

Lemma 6.19
.

As
\widetilde{I}_{2}
-modules

\sigma_{2}\cong\sigma_{2}^{p}.

Proof.

This is a consequence of the following identity in
\sigma_{2}^{p}

f(g)e_{U_{0}}e_{U^{-}_{2}}\otimes_{\widetilde{T}_{0}}w=f(g)e_{U_{0}}e_{\textbf{s}(\Gamma_{2e})}e_{U^{-}_{2}}\otimes_{\widetilde{T}_{0}}w=f(g)e_{\textbf{s}(\Gamma_{2e})}e_{U_{0}}e_{U^{-}_{2}}\otimes_{\widetilde{T}_{0}}w,

where
w\in\tau
. The main input needed for this identity is Proposition
5.3
(
2
).
∎

Hence by abuse of notation, we often denote this profinite model simply by
\sigma_{2}

Remark
.

The reason for switching to this profinite model is that conjugation by
w_{\mathrm{af}}
does not stabilize
\Gamma(2e)
and thus does not interact well with the quotients by
\mathbf{s}(\Gamma(2e))
that we used previously.

Proof of Theorem
6.17
.

As mentioned above, we only prove the proposition for
\alpha=\mathrm{af}
. Let

I_{2}^{\mathrm{af}}:=I_{2}\cap\,^{w_{\mathrm{af}}}I_{2}{\quad\text{and}\quad}\sigma_{2}^{\mathrm{af}}:=i_{U^{-}_{2}\cap I_{2}^{\mathrm{af}},U_{0}}^{I_{2}^{\mathrm{af}}}\tau.

Since
\tau
is
W
-invariant and
Z(\widetilde{T})
-invariant, Proposition
5.13
implies

\,{}^{w_{\mathrm{af}}}\sigma_{2}^{\mathrm{af}}\cong\sigma_{2}^{\mathrm{af}}.

We also know by Proposition
5.8
and Lemma
5.14
that

\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{I}_{2}^{\mathrm{af}}}(\sigma_{2}^{\mathrm{af}},\sigma_{2})\cong\mathbb{C}.

Hence, we can identify
\sigma_{2}^{\mathrm{af}}
with the
\sigma_{2}^{\mathrm{af}}
-isotypic subspace of
\sigma_{2}
, and this identification is unique up to nonzero scaling. Let
A:\sigma_{2}^{\mathrm{af}}\hookrightarrow\sigma_{2}
be the canonical
\widetilde{I}_{2}^{\mathrm{af}}
-inclusion of this isotypic subspace. Let
C:\sigma_{2}\twoheadrightarrow\sigma_{2}^{\mathrm{af}}
be the canonical
\widetilde{I}_{2}^{\mathrm{af}}
-projection onto this isotypic subspace. Let
\,{}^{w_{\mathrm{af}}}C:\,^{w_{\mathrm{af}}}\sigma_{2}\twoheadrightarrow\,^{w_{\mathrm{af}}}\sigma_{2}^{\mathrm{af}}
be the
\widetilde{I}_{2}^{\mathrm{af}}
-homomorphism obtained by twisting
C
by
w_{\mathrm{af}}
. Note that
\,{}^{w_{\mathrm{af}}}C=C
as elements of
\operatorname{End}(\sigma_{2})
. Let
B:\,^{w_{\mathrm{af}}}\sigma_{2}^{\mathrm{af}}\rightarrow\sigma_{2}^{\mathrm{af}}
be an
\widetilde{I}_{2}^{\mathrm{af}}
-isomorphism, which is also unique up to nonzero scaling.

Now by construction
A\circ B\circ C\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{I}_{2}^{\mathrm{af}}}(\,^{w_{\alpha}}\sigma_{2},\sigma_{2})
and this map is nonzero. (We note though that this map may not be an isomorphism.)
∎

Remark
.

Suppose that
e\geq 2
. Since
U_{\alpha_{0},2e}
acts trivially on
\sigma_{2}^{\mathrm{af}}
and
\,{}^{w_{\mathrm{af}}}\sigma_{2}^{\mathrm{af}}\cong\sigma_{2}^{\mathrm{af}}
the canonical image of
\sigma_{2}^{\mathrm{af}}
in
\sigma_{2}
actually must be contained in
\sigma_{2}^{U_{-\alpha_{0},2e-4}}
. (When
e=1
the group
U_{-\alpha_{0},2e-4}
is not contained in
I_{2}
, but
U_{-\alpha_{0},0}
already acts trivially on
\sigma_{2}
.)

6.7.
Support: length
0
elements

In this subsection we show that
\mathcal{H}_{2}
is supported on the length
0
elements
\widetilde{\Omega}\subseteq\widetilde{W}_{\mathrm{ea}}
. Let us first mention

Lemma 6.20
.

The elements of
\widetilde{\Omega}
are represented by elements in
N_{\widetilde{G}}(\widetilde{T})\cap N_{\widetilde{G}}(\widetilde{I}_{2})
.

Proof.

This readily follows from Lemma
6.13
and Theorem
6.15
.
∎

We then have

Proposition 6.21
.

For each
n\in N_{\widetilde{G}}(\widetilde{T})\cap N_{\widetilde{G}}(\widetilde{I}_{2})
, we have

(\mathcal{H}_{2})_{n}\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{I}_{2}\cap{{}^{n}\widetilde{I}_{2}}}(\,^{n}\sigma_{2},\sigma_{2})\cong\mathbb{C}{\quad\text{and}\quad}\mathcal{H}_{n}\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{I}\cap{{}^{n}\widetilde{I}}}(\,^{n}\sigma,\sigma)\cong\mathbb{C}.

Hence both
\mathcal{H}_{2}
and
\mathcal{H}
are supported on the length 0 elements.

Proof.

For the proof, we need to use the profinite model (
6.18
) for
\sigma_{2}
. Then the argument for
\mathcal{H}_{2}
is similar to Proposition
5.24
(
4
) along with the observation that modulo
\widetilde{T}_{0}
the element
n
represents an element of
\widetilde{\Omega}\subseteq\widetilde{W}_{\mathrm{ea}}\cong W\ltimes\widetilde{Y}
. Again we apply Proposition
5.13
. The first bullet is satisfied because
\tau
is
W
-invariant and
\widetilde{Y}
-invariant. The second bullet is satisfied by a minor variant of Lemma
5.20
. The assertion for
\mathcal{H}
follows from Proposition
6.11
, Proposition
6.10
and Lemma
6.3
.
∎

6.8.
Quadratic relations

In this subsection we prove that the elements of
\mathcal{H}
constructed in §
6.6
and
6.7
satisfy quadratic relations. Given our current tool set, we must consider several cases. We begin with the quadratic relations for the elements corresponding to simple linear reflections. For these elements we work with the Iwahori model
\mathcal{H}
.

Let
\alpha\in\Delta
. By Lemma
6.17
we know that
\mathcal{H}_{w_{\alpha}}\cong\mathbb{C}
. We need to identify a suitably normalized generator of this one dimensional vector space. To do this we will use the finite Shimura correspondence.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{\widetilde{K}}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
be the subalgebra of functions supported on
\widetilde{K}
. Pulling back along the surjective group homomorphism
\widetilde{K}\twoheadrightarrow\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}}
defines a
\mathbb{C}
-algebra isomorphism
\mathcal{H}(\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}},\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}},\sigma)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathcal{H}_{\widetilde{K}}
such that it is support-preserving with respect to the surjection
\widetilde{K}\twoheadrightarrow\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}}
. Now we can apply the finite Shimura correspondence of Hecke algebras to describe the structure of
\mathcal{H}(\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}},\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}},\sigma)
. We make this precise in the next proposition.

Proposition 6.22
.

For every
\alpha\in\Delta
there exists
T_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{H}_{w_{\alpha}}
such that the follow identities hold. For all
\alpha,\beta\in\Delta
we have

(1)

T_{\alpha}^{2}=(q-1)T_{\alpha}+q
;

(2)

if
\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle=0
, then
T_{\alpha}T_{\beta}=T_{\beta}T_{\alpha}
;

(3)

if
\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle=-1
, then
T_{\alpha}T_{\beta}T_{\alpha}=T_{\beta}T_{\alpha}T_{\beta}
.

In particular, for each
\alpha\in\Delta
, the element
T_{\alpha}
is invertible.

Proof.

Corollary
6.9
describes a support preserving isomorphism

\mathcal{H}(\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}},\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}},\sigma)\cong\mathcal{H}(G_{\kappa},B_{\kappa},1).

Iwahori
[
Iwa64
]
proved that for each
\alpha\in\Delta
there exists a unique element
t_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{H}(G_{\kappa},B_{\kappa},1)_{w_{\alpha}}
satisfying the identities (
1
), (
2
), (
3
). For each
\alpha\in\Delta
, define
T_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{H}_{\widetilde{K}}
to be the image of
t_{\alpha}
under the chain of algebra isomorphisms
\mathcal{H}_{\widetilde{K}}\cong\mathcal{H}(\widetilde{{\mathsf{K}}},\widetilde{{\mathsf{I}}},\sigma)\cong\mathcal{H}(G_{\kappa},B_{\kappa},1)
prescribed above. As the two isomorphisms preserve support, it follows that
T_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{H}_{w_{\alpha}}
.
∎

It remains to consider the simple affine reflection
w_{\mathrm{af}}
. Here it will be convenient to work with the
I_{2}
-model
\mathcal{H}_{2}
. Let
T_{\mathrm{af}}\in(\mathcal{H}_{2})_{w_{\mathrm{af}}}
be any nonzero element. First we will prove that
T_{\mathrm{af}}
satisfies a weak quadratic relation, which will be valid for all Cartan types. Then we will refine this in types
A_{r},D_{r},E_{6},E_{7}
, by normalizing
T_{\mathrm{af}}
so that it satisfies the same quadratic relation that appears in Proposition
6.22
. The same quadratic relation should also hold in type
E_{8}
, but we are not able to prove it in this paper. We will make some comments about this at the end of this subsection.

Proposition 6.23
.

Let
T_{\mathrm{af}}\in(\mathcal{H}_{2})_{w_{\mathrm{af}}}
be nonzero. Then there exists
a,b\in\mathbb{C}
with
b\neq 0
such that

T_{\mathrm{af}}^{2}=aT_{\mathrm{af}}+b.

In particular,
T_{\mathrm{af}}
is invertible.

Proof.

In this proof we will occasionally abuse notation and write
w_{\mathrm{af}}
for the element
\tilde{w}_{\alpha_{0}}(\varpi^{2})\in N_{\widetilde{G}}(\widetilde{T})
.

By the definition of convolution and the Steinberg relations,

\displaystyle\operatorname{supp}\,T_{\mathrm{af}}^{2}\subseteq

\displaystyle\widetilde{I}_{2}\tilde{w}_{\alpha_{0}}(\varpi^{2})\widetilde{I}_{2}\tilde{w}_{\alpha_{0}}(\varpi^{2})\widetilde{I}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{I}_{2}\tilde{w}_{\alpha_{0}}(\varpi^{2})U_{\alpha_{0},2}\tilde{w}_{\alpha_{0}}(-\varpi^{2})\widetilde{I}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{I}_{2}U_{-\alpha_{0},-2}\widetilde{I}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{I}_{2}\cup\widetilde{I}_{2}\tilde{w}_{\alpha_{0}}(\varpi)\widetilde{I}_{2}\cup\widetilde{I}_{2}\tilde{w}_{\alpha_{0}}(\varpi^{2})\widetilde{I}_{2}.

By Proposition
6.12
and the support preserving isomorphism
\mathcal{H}\cong\mathcal{H}_{2}
, we know that
T_{\mathrm{af}}^{2}(g)=0
for any
g\in\widetilde{I}_{2}\tilde{w}_{\alpha_{0}}(\varpi)\widetilde{I}_{2}
. Therefore there exists
a,b\in\mathbb{C}
such that

T_{\mathrm{af}}^{2}=aT_{\mathrm{af}}+b.

We claim that
T_{\mathrm{af}}^{2}(1)=b\neq 0
. First, note that for arbitrary
g\in G
, we have

\displaystyle T_{\mathrm{af}}^{2}(g)=

\displaystyle\int_{\widetilde{I}_{2}w_{\mathrm{af}}\widetilde{I}_{2}}T_{\mathrm{af}}(h)T_{\mathrm{af}}(h^{-1}g)dh

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{h\in\widetilde{I}_{2}w_{\mathrm{af}}\widetilde{I}_{2}/\widetilde{I}_{2}}T_{\mathrm{af}}(h)T_{\mathrm{af}}(h^{-1}g)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{h\in U_{\alpha_{0},2}/U_{\alpha_{0},4}}T_{\mathrm{af}}(hw_{\mathrm{af}})T_{\mathrm{af}}(w_{\mathrm{af}}^{-1}h^{-1}g).

Now we take
g=1
. Then

\displaystyle T_{\mathrm{af}}^{2}(1)=

\displaystyle\sum_{h\in U_{\alpha_{0},2}/U_{\alpha_{0},4}}T_{\mathrm{af}}(hw_{\mathrm{af}})T_{\mathrm{af}}(w_{\mathrm{af}}^{-1}h^{-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{h\in U_{\alpha_{0},2}/U_{\alpha_{0},4}}\sigma_{2}(h)T_{\mathrm{af}}(w_{\mathrm{af}})T_{\mathrm{af}}(w_{\mathrm{af}}^{-1})\sigma_{2}(h^{-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{h\in U_{\alpha_{0},2}/U_{\alpha_{0},4}}\sigma_{2}(h)T_{\mathrm{af}}(w_{\mathrm{af}})T_{\mathrm{af}}(w_{\mathrm{af}}\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{0}}(-1))\sigma_{2}(h^{-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{h\in U_{\alpha_{0},2}/U_{\alpha_{0},4}}\sigma_{2}(h)T_{\mathrm{af}}(w_{\mathrm{af}})^{2}\sigma_{2}(\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{0}}(-1)h^{-1}),

where we used
w_{\mathrm{af}}^{-2}=\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{0}}(-1)
for the second equality.

Next we apply Proposition
6.17
to compute
T_{\mathrm{af}}(w_{\mathrm{af}})^{2}\in\operatorname{End}(\sigma_{2})
. From the proof of Proposition
6.17
we know that the map
T_{\mathrm{af}}(w_{\mathrm{af}})
factors into three pieces,
T_{\mathrm{af}}(w_{\mathrm{af}})=A\circ B\circ C
, where
A,B,C
are as in the proof of Proposition
6.17
. Thus we have
T_{\mathrm{af}}(w_{\mathrm{af}})^{2}=A\circ B\circ C\circ A\circ B\circ C
. Now by definition
C\circ A=id_{\sigma_{2}^{\mathrm{af}}}
. Thus
A\circ B\circ C\circ A\circ B\circ C=A\circ B^{2}\circ C
. Now
B\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{I}_{2}^{\mathrm{af}}}(\,^{w_{\mathrm{af}}}\sigma_{2}^{\mathrm{af}},\sigma_{2}^{\mathrm{af}})
, where we have put
I_{2}^{\mathrm{af}}=I_{2}\cap\,^{w_{\mathrm{af}}}I_{2}
. Thus

\displaystyle B^{2}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{I}_{2}^{\mathrm{af}}}(\,^{w_{\mathrm{af}}}\sigma_{2}^{\mathrm{af}},\,^{w_{\mathrm{af}}^{-1}}\sigma_{2}^{\mathrm{af}})=

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{I}_{2}^{\mathrm{af}}}(\sigma_{2}^{\mathrm{af}},\,^{w_{\mathrm{af}}^{-2}}\sigma_{2}^{\mathrm{af}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{I}_{2}^{\mathrm{af}}}(\sigma_{2}^{\mathrm{af}},\,^{\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{0}}(-1)}\sigma_{2}^{\mathrm{af}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}\sigma_{2}^{\mathrm{af}}(\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{0}}(-1)).

Therefore
T_{\mathrm{af}}(w_{\mathrm{af}})^{2}=cA\circ\sigma_{2}^{\mathrm{af}}(\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{0}}(-1))\circ C
for some
c\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. Since
\sigma_{2}^{\mathrm{af}}(h_{\alpha_{0}}(-1))\circ C=C\circ\sigma_{2}(\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{0}}(-1))
we have

\displaystyle T_{\mathrm{af}}(w_{\mathrm{af}})^{2}=

\displaystyle\sum_{h\in U_{\alpha_{0},2}/U_{\alpha_{0},4}}\sigma_{2}(h)T_{\mathrm{af}}(w_{\mathrm{af}})^{2}\sigma_{2}(\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{0}}(-1)h^{-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c\sum_{h\in U_{\alpha_{0},2}/U_{\alpha_{0},4}}\sigma_{2}(h)\circ A\circ\sigma_{2}^{\mathrm{af}}(\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{0}}(-1))\circ C\circ\sigma_{2}(\tilde{h}_{\alpha_{0}}(-1)h^{-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1,-1)c\sum_{h\in U_{\alpha_{0},2}/U_{\alpha_{0},4}}\sigma_{2}(h)\circ A\circ C\circ\sigma_{2}(h^{-1}).

By taking the trace and noting that
A\circ C
is just a projection operator we see that

\operatorname{Tr}(T_{\mathrm{af}}^{2}(1))=(-1,-1,)c\left|\mathcal{O}/\penalty\varpi\mathcal{O}\right|^{2}\operatorname{Tr}(A\circ C)\neq 0.

Thus
b=T_{\mathrm{af}}^{2}(1)\neq 0
. This implies
T_{\mathrm{af}}
is invertible.
∎

Now we specialize to the Cartan types
A_{r},D_{r},E_{6},E_{7}
and prove an exact quadratic relation. The feature of these types that we exploit is the existence of an inner automorphism of
\widetilde{G}
with good properties, essentially coming from a length
0
element in
\widetilde{W}_{\mathrm{ea}}
.

Let us make this inner automorphism explicit. When
\Phi
is simply-laced and not of type
E_{8}
, then there exists a simple root
\alpha_{j_{0}}
such that in the expression
-\alpha_{0}=\sum c_{j}\alpha_{j}
, we have
c_{j_{0}}=1
. The set of possible
\alpha_{j_{0}}
is exactly the orbit of
\alpha_{0}
under the automorphisms of the affine Dynkin diagram induced by the length
0
elements of the extended affine group
\widetilde{W}_{\mathrm{ea}}
, excluding
\alpha_{0}
itself. Alternatively, this set is in bijection with the nontrivial cosets in
\widetilde{Y}/2Y
. In particular, the simple root
\alpha_{j_{0}}
corresponds to the coset
2\omega_{j_{0}}^{\vee}+2Y\in\widetilde{Y}/2Y
, where
\omega_{j_{0}}^{\vee}
is the fundamental coweight associated with
\alpha_{j_{0}}
. (See
[
Bou02
, Ch. 6, §2.3, Prop. 6]
for details.)

Lemma 6.24
.

Suppose that
\Phi
is of type
A_{r},D_{r}
,
E_{6}
, or
E_{7}
. Let
\varepsilon\in\widetilde{\Omega}
be nontrivial. Let
\alpha_{j_{0}}:=\varepsilon(\alpha_{0})\in\Delta
. Then there exists
n\in N_{\widetilde{G}}(\widetilde{T})\cap N_{\widetilde{G}}(I_{2})
representing
\varepsilon\in\widetilde{\Omega}
such that the inner automorphism
\nu:=\operatorname{Int}(n)\in\operatorname{Aut}(\widetilde{G})
satisfies

(1)

\nu(\widetilde{T})=\widetilde{T}
,

(2)

\nu(\widetilde{I}_{2})=\widetilde{I}_{2}
,

(3)

\nu(\widetilde{I}_{2}\tilde{w}_{\alpha_{j_{0}}}\widetilde{I}_{2})=\widetilde{I}_{2}w_{\mathrm{af}}\widetilde{I}_{2}
.

Proof.

All three properties follow directly by construction.
∎

With this lemma, we can prove

Proposition 6.25
.

Let
\Phi
be of type
A_{r},D_{r}
,
E_{6}
,
E_{7}
. Then there exists
T_{\mathrm{af}}\in(\mathcal{H}_{2})_{w_{\mathrm{af}}}
such that

T_{\mathrm{af}}^{2}=(q-1)T_{\mathrm{af}}+q.

Proof.

Let
n\in N_{\widetilde{G}}(\widetilde{T})\cap N_{\widetilde{G}}(I_{2})
represent a nontrivial element in
\widetilde{\Omega}
. Such
n
exists by Lemma
6.20
. Set
\nu=\operatorname{Int}(n)
in the above lemma. Since
\,{}^{n}\sigma_{2}\cong\sigma_{2}
by Proposition
6.21
, there exists
A_{n}\in\operatorname{GL}(\sigma_{2})
such that
A_{n}\,^{n}\sigma_{2}(g)=\sigma_{2}(g)A_{n}
for all
g\in I_{2}
.

Define

T_{\mathrm{af}}:=A_{n}(T_{\alpha_{j_{0}}}\circ\nu^{-1})A_{n}^{-1}\in\mathcal{H}_{2},

by which we mean
T_{\mathrm{af}}
is such that
T_{\mathrm{af}}(g)=A_{n}(T_{\alpha_{j_{0}}}\circ\nu^{-1}(g))A_{n}^{-1}
. One can then see from Lemmas
6.5
and
6.24
that

T_{0}\in(\mathcal{H}_{2})_{w_{\mathrm{af}}}.

The quadratic relation follows from the quadratic relation for
T_{\alpha_{j_{0}}}
(Proposition
6.22
).
∎

Finally we prove that the length
0
elements satisfy a quadratic relation.

Proposition 6.26
.

Let
\varepsilon\in\widetilde{\Omega}
. There exists
T_{\varepsilon}\in(\mathcal{H}_{2})_{\varepsilon}
(unique up to sign) such that

T_{\varepsilon}^{2}=1.

In particular,
T_{\varepsilon}
is invertible.

Proof.

Let
t_{\varepsilon}\in(\mathcal{H}_{2})_{\varepsilon}
be nonzero. Note that
\widetilde{\Omega}\cong\widetilde{Y}/2Y
is an elementary abelian
2
-group. With this and the definition of convolution we have
\operatorname{supp}(t_{\varepsilon}^{2})\subseteq\widetilde{I}_{2}\varepsilon^{2}=\widetilde{I}_{2}
. Thus we see that
t_{\varepsilon}^{2}=C
for some
C\in\mathbb{C}
.

Note that the subgroup
\langle\widetilde{I}_{2},\varepsilon\rangle\subseteq\widetilde{G}
generated by
\widetilde{I}_{2}
and
\varepsilon
has
\widetilde{I}_{2}
as a subgroup of index
2
. Thus by Proposition
6.21
and Clifford theory we know that
\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde{I}_{2}}^{\langle\widetilde{I}_{2},\varepsilon\rangle}(\sigma_{2})
decomposes into two distinct irreducible representations
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}
extending
\sigma_{2}
. Then because
t_{\varepsilon}
can be viewed as an element of the semisimple algebra
\operatorname{End}_{\langle\widetilde{I}_{2},\varepsilon\rangle}(\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde{I}_{2}}^{\langle\widetilde{I}_{2},\varepsilon\rangle}(\sigma_{2}))
it follows that
C\neq 0
. Thus if we define
T_{\varepsilon}:=(C)^{-\frac{1}{2}}t_{\varepsilon}
(unique up to sign), then
T_{\varepsilon}^{2}=1
.
∎

6.9.
Braid relations

In this subsection we investigate the braid relations. We begin by proving the braid relations up to scaling.

For
w\in\widetilde{W}_{\mathrm{ea}}
, we let
t_{w}\in(\mathcal{H}_{2})_{w}
. By Proposition
6.11
the element
t_{w}
is unique up to scaling. Later we will be concerned with normalizing the elements
t_{w}
, but for now this is not important.

Proposition 6.27
.

Let
w_{1},w_{2}\in\widetilde{W}_{\mathrm{ea}}
. If
\ell(w_{1}w_{2})=\ell(w_{1})+\ell(w_{2})
, then in
\mathcal{H}_{2}
we have

t_{w_{1}}*t_{w_{2}}=C(w_{1},w_{2})t_{w_{1}w_{2}},

for some
C(w_{1},w_{2})\in\mathbb{C}
. Furthermore, if
t_{w_{1}}
is invertible and
t_{w_{2}}\neq 0
, then
C(w_{1},w_{2})\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
.

Proof.

Our proof follows
[
Kar21
, Proposition 6.8]
, which adapted the argument of
[
Sav04
, Proposition 6.2]
.

For any
g\in\widetilde{G}
we need to compute
t_{w_{1}}*t_{w_{2}}(g)
. By the definition of convolution we need to identify the cosets
\delta\widetilde{I}_{2}\subseteq\widetilde{G}
such that

\displaystyle\delta\widetilde{I}_{2}\subseteq

\displaystyle\widetilde{I}_{2}w_{1}\widetilde{I}_{2}{\quad\text{and}\quad}

\displaystyle(\delta\widetilde{I}_{2})^{-1}g\subseteq

\displaystyle\widetilde{I}_{2}w_{2}\widetilde{I}_{2}.

These containments only depend on
G
. Thus by Theorem
6.15
and Proposition
6.14
, which uses the length hypothesis, it follows that
\operatorname{supp}\,t_{w_{1}}*t_{w_{2}}\subseteq\widetilde{I}_{2}w_{1}w_{2}\widetilde{I}_{2}
.

By Proposition
6.11
there exists
C\in\mathbb{C}
such that
t_{w_{1}}*t_{w_{2}}=Ct_{w_{1}w_{2}}
. If we further assume that
t_{w_{1}}
is invertible and
t_{w_{2}}\neq 0
, then
C
cannot be
0
.
∎

Using Proposition
6.27
we can define a basis of
\mathcal{H}_{2}
such that each element is invertible.

Lemma 6.28
.

Let
w\in\widetilde{W}_{\mathrm{ea}}
. Let
\varepsilon\in\widetilde{\Omega}
and let
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{k}\in\Delta\cup\{\alpha_{0}\}
be a finite sequence such that

w=\varepsilon w_{\beta_{1}}\cdots w_{\beta_{k}}

is a reduced expression for
w
. Then the element
T_{\varepsilon}T_{\beta_{1}}\cdots T_{\beta_{k}}\in(\mathcal{H}_{2})_{w}
is nonzero and invertible.

Proof.

This follows by induction on the length of
w
and by Propositions
6.22
,
6.25
,
6.26
, and
6.27
.
∎

Proposition 6.29
.

The set of subspaces
(\mathcal{H}_{2})_{w}
, where
w\in\widetilde{W}_{\mathrm{ea}}
, is a line decomposition for
\mathcal{H}_{2}
, namely

\mathcal{H}_{2}=\bigoplus_{w\in\widetilde{W}_{\mathrm{ea}}}(\mathcal{H}_{2})_{w}.

Furthermore, any nonzero element of
(\mathcal{H}_{2})_{w}
is invertible.

Proof.

This follows from Theorem
6.11
, Proposition
6.12
and Lemma
6.28
, along with the fact that the distinct
(\mathcal{H}_{2})_{w}
’s have disjoint support.
∎

For the case of type
A_{r},D_{r}
,
E_{6}
,
E_{7}
, we can refine Proposition
6.29
to produce a normalized basis for
\mathcal{H}_{2}
for which the usual braid relations are satisfied.

From §
6.8
we have the following normalized elements. For
\alpha\in\Delta\cup\{\mathrm{af}\}
there is a unique
T_{\alpha}\in(\mathcal{H}_{2})_{w_{\alpha}}
such that
T_{\alpha}^{2}=(q-1)T_{\alpha}+q
. For
\varepsilon\in\widetilde{\Omega}
there is a unique up to sign
T_{\varepsilon}\in(\mathcal{H}_{2})_{\varepsilon}
such that
T_{\varepsilon}^{2}=1
. To begin we show that the elements indexed by
\Delta\cup\{\mathrm{af}\}
satisfy the braid relations.

Let us first prove the following elementary fact.

Lemma 6.30
.

Let
a,b,c\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. Let
\mathcal{A}
be an associative
\mathbb{C}
-algebra with unit. Suppose that
X,Y\in\mathcal{A}
such that

•

1,X,Y\in\mathcal{A}
are linearly independent;

•

X^{2}=aX+b
;

•

Y^{2}=aY+b
;

•

XYX=cYXY
, or
XY=cYX
.

Then
c=1
.

Proof.

This follows by a direct calculation. We will prove the case where
XYX=cYXY
. The other case is easier.

Since
b\neq 0
, the elements
X
and
Y
are invertible. Thus we know that
X=cYXYX^{-1}Y^{-1}
. Squaring and applying the quadratic relations gives

aX+b=c^{2}YX(aY+b)X^{-1}Y^{-1}.

The right hand side simplifies to

c^{2}YX(aY+b)X^{-1}Y^{-1}=c^{2}(aYXYX^{-1}Y^{-1}+b)=acX+c^{2}b.

Now since
X
and
1
are linearly independent we have
a=ac
and
b=bc^{2}
. Since
a\neq 0
it follows that
c=1
.
∎

Proposition 6.31
.

Let
\Phi
be of type
A_{r},D_{r}
,
E_{6}
,
E_{7}
. Let
\alpha,\beta\in\Delta\cup\{\mathrm{af}\}
be distinct.

(1)

If
\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle=0
, then
T_{\alpha}T_{\beta}=T_{\beta}T_{\alpha}
.

(2)

If
\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle=-1
, then
T_{\alpha}T_{\beta}T_{\alpha}=T_{\beta}T_{\alpha}T_{\beta}
.

Proof.

This follows from Proposition
6.27
and Lemma
6.30
.
∎

Proposition 6.32
.

Let
\Phi
be of type
A_{r},D_{r}
,
E_{6}
,
E_{7}
. Let
w\in\widetilde{W}_{\mathrm{af}}
and let
w=w_{1}\cdots w_{k}
be a reduced expression for
w
in terms of the affine simple reflections
w_{\alpha}
, where
\alpha\in\Delta\cup\{\mathrm{af}\}
. Define
T_{w}:=T_{w_{1}}\cdots T_{w_{k}}\in\mathcal{H}_{2}
. Then the element
T_{w}
is independent of the reduced expression for
w
.

Proof.

This follows from
[
Tit69
, Théorème 3]
and Proposition
6.31
.
∎

Lemma 6.33
.

Let
\Phi
be of type
A_{r},D_{r}
,
E_{6}
,
E_{7}
. Let
\varepsilon\in\widetilde{\Omega}
and
\alpha\in\Delta\cup\{\mathrm{af}\}
. Then
T_{\varepsilon}T_{\alpha}T_{\varepsilon}^{-1}=T_{\varepsilon(\alpha)}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
6.27
we know that
T_{\varepsilon}T_{\alpha}T_{\varepsilon}^{-1}=cT_{\varepsilon(\alpha)}
for some
c\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. Because
T_{\alpha}
and
T_{\varepsilon(\alpha)}
satisfy the same quadratic relation with nonzero linear coefficient it follows that
c=1
by Lemma
6.30
.
∎

6.10.
IM-presentation

Finally we can prove that when
\Phi
has Cartan type
A_{r},D_{2r+1},E_{6},E_{7}
the Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}
has an Iwahori-Matsumoto presentation (IM-presentation for short). The same result is expected to hold for
D_{2r}
and
E_{8}
, though we are not able to prove it in this paper.

Let us first define an abstract algebra derived from the extended Weyl group
\widetilde{W}_{\mathrm{ea}}
defined via generators and relations as in
[
IM65
, Section 3.1]
. Let

H_{\mathrm{af}}=\langle 1,t_{\alpha}\,:\,\alpha\in\Delta\cup\{\mathrm{af}\}\rangle

be the unital
\mathbb{C}
-algebra generated by the symbols
t_{\alpha}
, where
\alpha\in\Delta\cup\{\mathrm{af}\}
, subject to the following relations:

\displaystyle t_{\alpha}^{2}=(q-1)t_{\alpha}+q;

\displaystyle t_{\alpha}t_{\beta}=t_{\beta}t_{\alpha},\quad\text{if }\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle=0;

\displaystyle t_{\alpha}t_{\beta}t_{\alpha}=t_{\beta}t_{\alpha}t_{\beta},\quad\text{if }\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle=-1.

Note that for
w\in\widetilde{W}_{\mathrm{af}}
with a reduced expression
w=w_{\alpha_{i_{1}}}\cdots w_{\alpha_{i_{k}}}
we can define
t_{w}=t_{\alpha_{i_{1}}}\cdots t_{\alpha_{i_{k}}}
. This definition is independent of the choice of reduced word by the same argument as in Proposition
6.32
.

The group
\widetilde{\Omega}\subseteq\widetilde{W}_{\mathrm{ea}}
acts on
H_{\mathrm{af}}
by algebra automorphisms as follows. Recall that the elements of
\widetilde{\Omega}
act on
\Delta\cup\{\mathrm{af}\}
by permutation. Then for
\varepsilon\in\widetilde{\Omega}
define
\varepsilon(t_{\alpha})=t_{\varepsilon(\alpha)}
. This induces an algebra automorphism of
H_{\mathrm{af}}
. Moreover, this is compatible with the action of
\widetilde{\Omega}
on
\widetilde{W}_{\mathrm{af}}
in the sense that
\varepsilon(t_{w})=t_{\varepsilon w\varepsilon^{-1}}
. Let

H_{\mathrm{ea}}:=\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\Omega}]\otimes H_{\mathrm{af}}.

This
\mathbb{C}
vector space can be given the structure of a
\mathbb{C}
-algebra as follows. For
\varepsilon_{j}\in\widetilde{\Omega}
and
w_{j}\in\widetilde{W}_{\mathrm{af}}
define

(\varepsilon_{1}\otimes t_{w_{1}})\cdot(\varepsilon_{2}\otimes t_{\alpha_{2}}):=\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}\otimes t_{\varepsilon_{2}^{-1}(\alpha_{1})}t_{\alpha_{2}}

Theorem 6.34
.

Assume that
G
has Cartan type
A_{r},D_{2r+1},E_{6},E_{7}
. Then the linear map
H_{\mathrm{ea}}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
defined by
\varepsilon\otimes t_{w}\mapsto T_{\varepsilon}T_{w}
, where
\varepsilon\in\widetilde{\Omega}
and
w\in\widetilde{W}_{\mathrm{af}}
, is an isomorphism of
\mathbb{C}
-algebras.

Proof.

The map is a linear isomorphism because the set
\{\varepsilon\otimes t_{w}\,:\,\varepsilon\in\widetilde{\Omega},\,w\in\widetilde{W}_{\mathrm{af}}\}
is a basis for
H_{\mathrm{ea}}
and the set
\{T_{\varepsilon}T_{w}\,:\,\varepsilon\in\widetilde{\Omega},\,w\in\widetilde{W}_{\mathrm{af}}\}
is a basis for
\mathcal{H}
. The map is a
\mathbb{C}
-algebra homomorphism because of Propositions
6.22
,
6.25
,
6.26
,
6.31
, and Lemma
6.33
.
∎

Theorem
6.34
and the work of
[
IM65
]
imply that
\mathcal{H}
is isomorphic to the Iwahori-Hecke algebra of a linear group. Specifically, let

G^{\prime}:=G/Z_{2},

where
Z_{2}
is the subgroup of
2
-torsion in the center of
G
. (The significance of
Z_{2}
is that the fundamental group of
G^{\prime}
is isomorphic to
\widetilde{Y}/2Y
. Thus the dual root datum of
G^{\prime}
agrees with the dual root datum of
\widetilde{G}
.) Let
I^{\prime}
be an Iwahori subgroup of
G^{\prime}
and let
\mathcal{H}(G^{\prime},I^{\prime})
be the Iwahori-Hecke algebra of locally constant
I^{\prime}
-biinvariant functions on
G^{\prime}
.

Corollary 6.35
.

As
\mathbb{C}
-algebras, we have

\mathcal{H}(G^{\prime},I^{\prime})\cong\mathcal{H}\cong H_{\mathrm{ea}}.

Proof.

This follows directly from Theorem
6.34
and
[
IM65
, Section 3]
.
∎

Remark
.

We mention another consequence of Theorem
6.34
. From the Iwahori presentation it is not difficult to show that the Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}(\widetilde{G},\widetilde{K},\sigma)
is isomorphic to the center of
\mathcal{H}
, the structure of which is well known. This gives an analog of the Satake isomorphism for the wild cover
\widetilde{G}
.

We end this subsection with a few comments on the cases of
E_{8}
and
D_{2r}
. We expect the same type of Iwahori-Matsumoto presentation to hold in the case of
D_{2r}
and
E_{8}
as well. When
F
is unramified over
\mathbb{Q}_{2}
the approach of
[
Kar21
]
can be generalized by incorporating some ideas from this current paper. We will not go into this because we believe that these cases for any
F
will follow as we refine and expand the techniques used in this paper.

For example, for
E_{8}
the only obstruction is proving that the the simple affine reflection satisfies the desired quadratic relation. This should follow from an analysis along the lines of §
5.1
for other vertices in the building of
G
and an analogous finite Shimura correspondence at these vertices. For
D_{2r}
the difficulty arises because in this case
\widetilde{\Omega}
is not a cyclic group. The resolution of this issue is related to the question of extending
\sigma_{2}
from
\widetilde{I}_{2}
to its normalizer in
\widetilde{G}
. However, at the moment, certain basic properties of
\sigma_{2}
remain unknown, such as its dimension. As we further investigate the basic properties of
\sigma_{2}
, this will naturally put us in a better position to address the extension question.

7.
Iwahori types

In this section we show that
(\widetilde{I},\sigma)
is a type in the sense of
[
BK98
, (4.1) Definition]
. We emphasize that this result is valid for any Cartan type. As a corollary, we derive a local Shimura correspondence when
G
is of Cartan type
A_{r},D_{2r+1},E_{6}
and
E_{7}
.

The following arguments essentially follow
[
BK98
]
, which build on the Borel-Casselman theorem. However, there is one notable difference. Bushnell-Kutzko introduce the notion
G
-cover
[
BK98
, (8.1) Definition]
as a way to axiomatize the construction of types for a reductive group
G
from types of its Levi subgroups. Their main theorem for constructing types using
G
-covers is
[
BK98
, (8.3) Theorem]
. The definition of
G
-cover refers back to
[
BK98
, (6.1) Definition]
which contains two conditions (i) and (ii). Our proposed type
(\widetilde{I},\sigma)
satisfies condition (ii) by Proposition
6.29
, but it does not satisfy condition (i). Specifically,
\sigma
is neither trivial on
U_{0}
nor
U^{-}_{1}
, when
e>1
. Fortunately, we are able to modify the argument of Bushnell-Kutzko using special properties of
\sigma
. For example, Lemma
7.2
plays a particularly important role. So, we include the details to make clear the necessary changes, in the hope that they might point toward a valuable extension of the notion of
G
-cover.

We begin with a few lemmas.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
G
be a finite group with an Iwahori factorization
(V,T,U)
. Let
\pi\in\operatorname{Irr}(G)
be such that
r_{U,V}\pi\neq 0
. The element
e_{U}e_{V}e_{U}\in\operatorname{End}({\pi})
induces a linear isomorphism of the space
\pi^{U}
and thus is a generator of the one-dimensional space
\operatorname{Hom}_{T}(\pi^{U},\pi^{U})=\mathbb{C}e_{U}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
5.9
the element
e_{U}e_{V}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{T}(\pi^{V},\pi^{U})
is an isomorphism, and
r_{U,V}\pi\cong\pi^{U}\cong\pi^{V}
is an irreducible
T
-module. Similarly,
e_{V}e_{U}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{T}(\pi^{U},\pi^{V})
is an isomorphism. Hence
e_{U}e_{V}e_{U}=(e_{U}e_{V})(e_{V}e_{U})\in\operatorname{Hom}_{T}(\pi^{U},\pi^{U})=\mathbb{C}e_{U}
is nonzero and the result follows.
∎

In the next lemma we prove that the Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}
contains elements supported on certain positive elements of
Z(\widetilde{T})
with a natural normalization. We note that this lemma does not rely on any of the previous support computations in
\mathcal{H}
.

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
z\in Z(\widetilde{T})
be such that
zU_{0}z^{-1}\subseteq U_{2e}
. Then the element
e_{\mathsf{U}_{0}}e_{\mathsf{U}^{-}_{1}}\in\mathcal{H}(\widetilde{\mathsf{I}})
defines a nonzero element in
\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{I}\cap\,^{z}\widetilde{I}}(\sigma,\,^{z}\sigma)
.

Proof.

Note that since
zU_{0}z^{-1}\subseteq U_{2e}
, it also follows that
z^{-1}U^{-}_{1}z\subseteq U^{-}_{2e+1}
. This implies that
\widetilde{I}\cap\,^{z}\widetilde{I}
has an Iwahori decomposition

\widetilde{I}\cap\,^{z}\widetilde{I}\cong\,^{z}U_{0}\times\widetilde{T}_{0}\times U^{-}_{1}.

We have to show
e_{\mathsf{U}_{0}}e_{\mathsf{U}^{-}_{1}}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{I}\cap\,^{z}\widetilde{I}}(\sigma,\,^{z}\sigma)
. For this it suffices to show that it is
\widetilde{T}_{0}
-,
U_{0}
- and
U_{1}^{-}
-intertwining. But one can readily see that it is
\widetilde{T}_{0}
-intertwining because
z\in Z(\widetilde{T})
. Next, since
\,{}^{z}U_{0}\subseteq U_{2e}\subseteq\ker\sigma
, we see that
\,{}^{z}U_{0}
acts trivially on the domain. Now the image of
e_{\mathsf{U}_{0}}e_{\mathsf{U}^{-}_{1}}
is contained in
\sigma^{U_{0}}=(\,^{z}\sigma)^{\,{}^{z}U_{0}}
. Thus
\,{}^{z}U_{0}
also acts trivially on the codomain. Thus
e_{\mathsf{U}_{0}}e_{\mathsf{U}^{-}_{1}}
intertwines the
\,{}^{z}U_{0}
action. The case of
U^{-}_{1}
is similar.

The map
e_{\mathsf{U}_{0}}e_{\mathsf{U}^{-}_{1}}
is nonzero by Proposition
5.9
(4) because
\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{T}}(\sigma^{\mathsf{U}_{0}},\sigma^{\mathsf{U}_{1}^{-}})\neq 0
.
∎

Remark
.

Lemma
7.2
and Proposition
6.11
together imply that
\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{I}\cap\,^{z}\widetilde{I}}(\sigma,\,^{z}\sigma)=\mathbb{C}e_{\mathsf{U}_{0}}e_{\mathsf{U}^{-}_{1}}
.

The next two lemmas are based on
[
BK98
, (7.9) Theorem]
. We cannot invoke this result directly because in our case the pair
(\widetilde{I},\sigma)
does not satisfy hypothesis (a) of this theorem, because
(\widetilde{I},\sigma)
does not satisfy condition (i) in
[
BK98
, (6.1) Definition]
as mentioned at the beginning of this section.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
q:\pi\to\pi_{U}
be the canonical surjection. The map
\operatorname{Hom}(\sigma,\pi)\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(\sigma^{U_{0}},\pi_{U})
defined by
f\mapsto q\circ f|_{\sigma^{U_{0}}}
is a homomorphism of
(\widetilde{I}\cap\widetilde{B},\widetilde{B})
-bimodules. The map induced by restriction
\Psi:\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{I}}(\sigma,\pi)\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{T}_{0}}(\sigma^{U_{0}},\pi_{U})
is a surjective linear map.

Proof.

Let
f\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{T}_{0}}(\sigma^{U_{0}},\pi_{U})
. Note that by definition
f\circ e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}=f
. When convenient we use this to identify
f
with
f\circ e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{T}_{0}}(\sigma,\pi_{U})
.

Now since the category of
\widetilde{T}_{0}
-module is semisimple we can fix a
\widetilde{T}_{0}
-module splitting
s:\pi_{U}\rightarrow\pi
of the
\widetilde{T}_{0}
-module surjection
q:\pi\rightarrow\pi_{U}
. Define

F:=s\circ f\circ e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{T}_{0}}(\sigma,\pi),

so that
q\circ F=f\circ e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}
.

Since
\sigma
is finite dimensional there exists
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
such that
\Im(F)\subseteq\pi^{U^{-}_{j}}
. Let
z\in Z(\widetilde{T})
be such that
z^{-1}U^{-}_{1}z\subseteq U^{-}_{j}\cap U^{-}_{2e+1}
.

Define

F_{1}:=\int_{\widetilde{I}}\pi(x)\pi(z)F\circ\sigma(x^{-1})dx\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{I}}(\sigma,\pi).

Let us compare
F_{1}
to
f
. Let
w\in\sigma
. Then by the Iwahori factorization, we have

\displaystyle q\circ F_{1}(e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}w)=

\displaystyle q\circ\int_{\widetilde{I}}\pi(x)\pi(z)F\circ\sigma(x^{-1})e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}w\,dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q\circ\int_{U_{0}\widetilde{T}_{0}U^{-}_{1}}\pi(utv)\pi(z)F\circ\sigma((utv)^{-1})e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}w\,du\,dt\,dv

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q\circ\int_{U_{0}\widetilde{T}_{0}U^{-}_{1}}\pi(utvz)F\circ\sigma((utv)^{-1})e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}w\,du\,dt\,dv.

Using the idempotent
e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}
and the fact that
F
is a
\widetilde{T}_{0}
-module homomorphism we have

\displaystyle q\circ\int_{U_{0}\widetilde{T}_{0}U^{-}_{1}}\pi(utvz)F\circ\sigma((utv)^{-1})e_{U_{0}}w\,du\,dt\,dv\equiv

\displaystyle q\circ\int_{\widetilde{T}_{0}U^{-}_{1}}\pi(tvz)F\circ\sigma((tv)^{-1})e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}w\,dt\,dv

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle q\circ\int_{U^{-}_{1}}\pi(vz)F\circ\sigma(v^{-1})e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}w\,dv,

where by
\equiv
we mean equality up to a positive scalar. (The discrepancy comes from the choice of measures. We will use the same convention in what follow.)
Now because
\Im(F)\subseteq\pi^{U^{-}_{j}}
and
z^{-1}U^{-}_{1}z\subseteq U^{-}_{j}
, we have

\displaystyle q\circ\int_{U^{-}_{1}}\pi(vz)F\circ\sigma(v^{-1})e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}w\,dv\equiv

\displaystyle q\circ\int_{U^{-}_{1}}\pi(z)\pi(z^{-1}vz)F\circ\sigma(v^{-1})e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}w\,dv

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle q\circ\pi(z)F\circ e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{1}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}w

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\pi_{U}(z)f\circ e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{1}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}w,

where the last equality is by the definition of
F
. Finally we can apply Lemma
7.1
to get

\pi_{U}(z)f\circ e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{1}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}w\equiv\pi_{U}(z)f\circ e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}w.

Thus we have shown that

(7.4)

q\circ F_{1}(e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}w)\equiv\pi_{U}(z)f\circ e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}w.

Recall that for
T\in\mathcal{H}
and
A\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{I}}(\sigma,\pi)
, we define
A*T\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{I}}(\sigma,\pi)
by

(A*T)(w):=\int_{\widetilde{G}}\pi(h^{-1})A(T(h)w)\,dh.

Now suppose that
T\in\mathcal{H}_{z^{-1}}
, such that
T(z^{-1})=e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{1}}
. Such an element exists by Lemma
7.2
because
z^{-1}U^{-}_{1}z\subseteq U^{-}_{2e+1}
. Then by definition

\displaystyle q[A*T(w)]=

\displaystyle q[\int_{\widetilde{G}}\pi(h^{-1})A(T(h)w)\,dh]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q[\int_{\widetilde{I}z^{-1}\widetilde{I}}\pi(h^{-1})A(T(h)w)\,dh]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q[\sum_{\widetilde{I}\backslash\widetilde{I}z^{-1}\widetilde{I}}\pi(h^{-1})A(T(h)w)].

Now because
z^{-1}U^{-}_{1}z\subseteq U^{-}_{j}\cap U^{-}_{2e+1}
,

\displaystyle q[\sum_{\widetilde{I}\backslash\widetilde{I}z^{-1}\widetilde{I}}\pi(h^{-1})A(T(h)w)]=

\displaystyle q[\sum_{zU_{0}z^{-1}\backslash U_{0}}\pi(u^{-1}z)A(T(z^{-1})\sigma(u)w)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q[\sum_{zU_{0}z^{-1}\backslash U_{0}}\pi(z)A(e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{1}}\sigma(u)w)]

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\pi_{U}(z)q[A(e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{1}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}w)].

By Lemma
7.1
,

\pi_{U}(z)q[A(e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{1}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}w)]\equiv\pi(z)q[A(e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}w)].

Thus we have shown

(7.5)

q[A*T(w)]\equiv\pi_{U}(z)q[A(e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}w)].

Now we set
A=F_{1}*T^{-1}
and combine equations (
7.4
) and (
7.5
) to get

\pi_{U}(z)f\circ e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}w\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{SurjEq1}}}{{\equiv}}q\circ F_{1}(e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}w)\equiv q\circ F_{1}*T^{-1}*T(e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}w)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{SurjEq2}}}{{\equiv}}\pi_{U}(z)q[[F*T^{-1}](e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}w)].

Thus

q\circ[F*T^{-1}]\circ e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}\equiv f\circ e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}

and the result follows.
∎

Lemma 7.6
.

The map
\operatorname{Hom}(\sigma,\pi)\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(\sigma^{U_{0}},\pi_{U})
defined by
f\mapsto q\circ f|_{\sigma^{U_{0}}}
is a homomorphism of
(\widetilde{I}\cap\widetilde{B},\widetilde{B})
-bimodules. The map induced by restriction
\Psi:\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{I}}(\sigma,\pi)\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{T}_{0}}(\sigma^{U_{0}},\pi_{U})
is an injective linear map.

Proof.

Suppose that
F\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{I}}(\sigma,\pi)
is such that
q\circ F\circ e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}=0
. Since
F(\sigma^{U_{0}})
is finite dimensional there exists
j\in\mathbb{Z}
such that for all
w\in\sigma^{U_{0}}
we have
\int_{U_{j^{\prime}}}\pi(u)F(w)du=0
for any
j^{\prime}\leq j
.

Let
z\in Z(\widetilde{T})
be such that
zU_{j}z^{-1}\subseteq U_{0}\cap U_{j+2e}
. Let
T\in\mathcal{H}_{z^{-1}}
be such that
T(z^{-1})=e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{1}}
, which exists by Lemma
7.2
. Then since
zU_{j}z^{-1}\subseteq U_{0}
for
w\in\sigma^{U_{0}}
, we have

\displaystyle F*T(w)=

\displaystyle\int_{\widetilde{I}z^{-1}\widetilde{I}}\pi(h^{-1})F(T(h)w)\,dh

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{U_{0}}\pi(u^{-1}z)F(T(z^{-1})\sigma(u)w)\,du

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{U_{0}}\pi(u^{-1}z)F(e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{1}}\sigma(u)w)\,du

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi(z)\int_{U_{0}}\pi(z^{-1}u^{-1}z)F(e_{{\mathsf{U}}_{0}}e_{{\mathsf{U}}^{-}_{1}}\sigma(u)w)\,du

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

where the last equality follows because
zU_{j}z^{-1}\subseteq U_{0}
.

Thus
F*T
is zero on
\sigma^{U_{0}}
. Since
F*T
is an
\widetilde{I}
-module homomorphism and
\sigma^{U_{0}}
generates
\sigma
as an
\widetilde{I}
-module, it follows that
F*T=0
. Since
T
is invertible we see that
F=0
.
∎

Proposition 7.7
.

Let
\pi
be a smooth irreducible representation of
\widetilde{G}
. Then the
\sigma
-isotypic subspace
\pi^{\sigma}
is nonzero if and only if
\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{G}}(\pi,\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde{B}}^{\widetilde{G}}i(\chi))\neq 0
for some unramified character
\chi:T\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}
.

Proof.

We begin with a few preliminary remarks. The
\widetilde{T}
-representation
\pi_{U}
is finitely generated, because
\pi
is irreducible, and thus
\pi_{U}
has an irreducible quotient. By Frobenius reciprocity it is equivalent to proving the following. The
\sigma
-isotypic subspace
\pi^{\sigma}
is nonzero if and only if
\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{T}}(\pi_{U},i_{\eta,\tau}(\chi))\neq 0
for some unramified character
\chi:T\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. Now we start the proof.

Suppose that
\pi^{\sigma}\neq 0
. This implies that
\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{I}}(\sigma,\pi)\neq 0
. Thus by Lemma
7.6
it follows that
\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{T}_{0}}(\sigma^{U_{0}},\pi_{U})\neq 0
. Since
\sigma^{U_{0}}\cong\tau
as
\widetilde{T}_{0}
-representations we see that
\pi^{\tau}\neq 0
.

Since
\tau
is a
\widetilde{T}
-type and
\pi_{U}^{\tau}\neq 0
it follows that
\pi_{U}
has an irreducible quotient with a nonzero
\tau
-isotypic subspace. Thus by Proposition
4.27
,
\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{T}}(\pi_{U},i_{\eta,\tau}(\chi))\neq 0
for some unramified character
\chi:T\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}
.

Now we prove the converse. Suppose that
\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{T}}(\pi_{U},i_{\eta,\tau}(\chi))\neq 0
for some unramified character
\chi:T\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. Since
i_{\eta,\tau}(\chi)
is irreducible, there is a surjective
\widetilde{T}
-homomorphism
\pi_{U}\twoheadrightarrow i_{\eta,\tau}(\chi)
. Thus
\pi_{U}^{\tau}\neq 0
, because
i_{\eta,\tau}(\chi)^{\tau}\neq 0
. Finally we apply Lemma
7.3
to see that
\pi^{\sigma}\neq 0
.
∎

Now we can prove our main theorem.

Theorem 7.8
.

Let
\mathcal{M}(\widetilde{G},\sigma)
be the full subcategory of
\mathcal{M}(\widetilde{G})
consisting of objects generated by their
\sigma
-isotypic subspace.

(1)

The category
\mathcal{M}(\widetilde{G},\sigma)
is closed relative to subquotients in
\mathcal{M}(\widetilde{G})
.

(2)

For every irreducible object
(\pi,V)
in
\mathcal{M}(\widetilde{G},\sigma)
, there is an unramified character
\chi:T\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}
such that
(\pi,V)
is isomorphic to an irreducible subquotient of
\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde{B}}^{\widetilde{G}}(i(\chi))
.

In particular,
(\widetilde{I},\sigma)
is a type.

Proof.

(
1
) can be proved as in
[
Kar21
, Theorem 7.1]
, with
[
Kar21
, Proposition 7.4]
being replaced by Proposition
7.7
. This argument essentially follows
[
BK98
, (8.3) Theorem]
.

(
2
) is already part of Proposition
7.7
.
∎

As a corollary, we have the following local Shimura correspondence.

Corollary 7.9
.

Assume the Cartan type of
G
is
A_{r},D_{2r+1},E_{6}
or
E_{7}
. Then the category
\mathcal{M}(\widetilde{G},\sigma)
is equivalent to the Bernstein block of the Iwahori spherical representations of
G^{\prime}=G/\penalty Z_{2}
.

Proof.

This is immediate from the above theorem and Corollary
6.35
. Also see the proof of
[
Kar21
, Theorem 8.1]
.
∎

Appendix A
Hilbert symbol over
2
-adic fields

In this appendix, we establish various properties of the quadratic Hilbert symbol for our
2
-adic field
F
. Everything in this appendix is likely known, but we include proofs because we have not been able to locate the proofs in the literature.

Since
F
is
2
-adic, we first have

Lemma A.1
.

The quadratic Hilbert symbol is not trivial on
\mathcal{O}^{\times}\times\mathcal{O}^{\times}
.

Proof.

If
a\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
is such that
(a,b)=1
for all
a\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
then
F(\sqrt{a})
is an unramified extension of
F
by
[
Ser79
, Exercise 5, p.209]
. But certainly there is a unit
a\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
such that
F(\sqrt{a})
is ramified over
F
.
∎

We need to analyze the Hilbert symbol restricted to
\mathcal{O}^{\times}\times\mathcal{O}^{\times}
. For this purpose, define

R=\{a\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}\,:\,\text{$(a,-)$ is trivial on $\mathcal{O}^{\times}$}\},

which we call the
integral radical
of the Hilbert symbol. By the above lemma,
R\subsetneq\mathcal{O}^{\times}
. Clearly we have
\mathcal{O}^{\times 2}\subseteq R
.

Let us mention the following “one-dimensionality” of the integral radical.

Lemma A.2
.

Let us view
F^{\times}/\penalty F^{\times 2}
as an
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-vector space. Then

\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}(RF^{\times 2}/F^{\times 2})=1,

where we view
RF^{\times 2}/\penalty F^{\times 2}
as a subspace of
F^{\times}/\penalty F^{\times 2}
.

Proof.

The
\mathbb{F}_{2}
linear map
F^{\times}/F^{\times 2}\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}(F^{\times}/F^{\times 2},\mathbb{F}_{2})
defined by
t\mapsto(u\mapsto(t,u))
is an isomorphism, because the Hilbert symbol is nondegenerate. Moreover, by definition, this map identifies
RF^{\times 2}/F^{\times 2}
with the annihilator
\mathrm{ann}(\mathcal{O}^{\times}F^{\times 2}/F^{\times 2})
. Since
\mathcal{O}^{\times}F^{\times 2}/F^{\times 2}\subseteq F^{\times}/F^{\times 2}
is a hyperplane, the lemma follows.
∎

For each
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, we set

U_{k}=1+\varpi^{k}\mathcal{O}.

We often use the well-known

Lemma A.3
.

Every element in
\mathcal{O}/\penalty\varpi\mathcal{O}
has a square root, and hence
\mathcal{O}^{\times}=\mathcal{O}^{\times 2}U_{1}
.

Proof.

The Frobenius map (the squaring map) on
\mathcal{O}/\penalty\varpi\mathcal{O}
is an automorphism, and hence every element in
\mathcal{O}/\penalty\varpi\mathcal{O}
has a square root. Then every element in
\mathcal{O}^{\times}
is written as
a^{2}+b\varpi
for some nonzero
a\in\mathcal{O}/\penalty\varpi\mathcal{O}
and some
b\in\mathcal{O}/\penalty\varpi\mathcal{O}
. We can then write
a^{2}+b\varpi=a^{2}(1+a^{-2}b\varpi)
.
∎

We then have

Lemma A.4
.

(a)

U_{2e+1}\subseteq\mathcal{O}^{\times 2}
.

(b)

For each
u\in\mathcal{O}
, we have
1+4u\in\mathcal{O}^{\times 2}
if and only if there exists
v\in\mathcal{O}
such that
v+v^{2}=u
in
\mathcal{O}/\penalty\varpi\mathcal{O}
.

(c)

U_{2e}\nsubseteq\mathcal{O}^{\times 2}
.

(d)

U_{2e}/\penalty(U_{2e}\cap\mathcal{O}^{\times 2})\cong\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

(a)

This follows from
[
Neu99
, (5.5) Proposition, page 137]
because the exponential map
\varpi^{e+1}\mathcal{O}\to U_{e+1}
is an isomorphism.

(b)

Let
u\in\mathcal{O}
. Assume
1+4u\in\mathcal{O}^{\times 2}
, so
1+4u=w^{2}
for some
w\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
. One can then write
w=1+\varpi^{\ell}v
for some
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
v\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
. So, we have
1+4u=(1+\varpi^{\ell}v)^{2}
. A short calculation shows that
\ell=e
, so
\varpi^{\ell}v\in 2\mathcal{O}^{\times}
. Hence by re-choosing
v
(if necessary), we have
1+4u=(1+2v)^{2}
, which implies
v+v^{2}=u
. Conversely, assume there exists
v\in\mathcal{O}
such that
v+v^{2}=u
in
\mathcal{O}/\penalty\varpi\mathcal{O}
, namely
u=v+v^{2}+\varpi v^{\prime}
for some
v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{O}
. We then have
1+4u=(1+2v)^{2}+4\varpi v^{\prime}
. But

(1+2v)^{2}+4\varpi v^{\prime}=(1+2v)^{2}(1+\frac{4\varpi v^{\prime}}{(1+2v)^{2}}){\quad\text{and}\quad}1+\frac{4\varpi v^{\prime}}{(1+2v)^{2}}\in U_{2e+1}.

By part (a), we know
U_{2e+1}\subseteq\mathcal{O}^{\times 2}
, which implies
1+4u\in\mathcal{O}^{\times 2}
.

(c)

Consider the group homomorphism
\sigma:\mathbb{F}_{q}\to\mathbb{F}_{q}
given by
x\mapsto x+x^{2}
. The kernel is exactly
\mathbb{F}_{2}
. Hence there exists
u\in\mathcal{O}
such that
u=x+x^{2}
has no solution. For such
u
, we know
1+4u\notin\mathcal{O}^{\times 2}
by part (b).

(d)

Recall that there is a group isomorphism
\mathcal{O}/\varpi\mathcal{O}\rightarrow U_{2e}/U_{2e+1}
defined by
u+\varpi\mathcal{O}\mapsto(1+4u)U_{2e+1}
. Under this map we see that the squares in
U_{2e}
correspond to the elements of
\mathcal{O}/\varpi\mathcal{O}\cong\mathbb{F}_{q}
in the image of the map
\sigma
of part (c). Since the image of
\sigma
has index
2
in
\mathbb{F}_{q}
the result follows.

∎

In what follows, we will show
R=U_{2e}\mathcal{O}^{\times 2}
. Let us start with one inclusion.

Lemma A.5
.

We have
U_{2e}\subseteq R
.

Proof.

Let
1-4u\in U_{2e}
for
u\in\mathcal{O}
. If
u\notin\mathcal{O}^{\times}
, then
1-4u\in U_{2e+1}
and we already know
U_{2e+1}\subseteq\mathcal{O}^{\times 2}\subseteq R
. Hence we assume
u\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
. We have to show
(1-4u,a)=1
for all
a\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
. Let
v\in\mathcal{O}
be such that
1-4v\in U_{2e}\cap\mathcal{O}^{\times 2}
. Then for all
a\in F^{\times}
we have

\displaystyle(1-4u,a)

\displaystyle=((1-4u)(1-4v),a)=(1-4(u+v)+16uv,a)

\displaystyle=((1-4(u+v))(1+\frac{16uv}{1-4(u+v)}),a)=(1-4(u+v),a),

where we used
1+\frac{16uv}{1-4(u+v)}\in U_{2e+1}\subseteq\mathcal{O}^{\times 2}
by Lemma
A.4
(a). Also
(1-4(u+v),u+v)=(1-4(u+v),4(u+v))=1
, where we used
(1-x,x)=1
for all
x\in F^{\times}
. Hence by setting
a=u+v
, we obtain

(A.6)

(1-4u,u+v)=(1-4(u+v),u+v)=1.

Now, for
a\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
, one can write
au^{-1}=r^{2}(1+\varpi s)
for some
r\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
and
s\in\mathcal{O}
by Lemma
A.3
. Then

(1-4u,a)=(1-4u,ur^{2}(1+\varpi s))=(1-4u,u+\varpi(us)).

But
1-4\varpi(us)\in U_{2e+1}\subseteq\mathcal{O}^{\times 2}
, and hence by taking
v=\varpi(us)
in (
A.6
), we obtain
(1-4u,a)=1
.
∎

We can now prove

Proposition A.7
.

We have
U_{2e}\mathcal{O}^{\times 2}=R
, and hence
R/\penalty\mathcal{O}^{\times 2}=\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

We already know
\mathcal{O}^{\times 2}\subseteq U_{2e}\mathcal{O}^{\times 2}\subseteq R
and
[U_{2e}\mathcal{O}^{\times 2}:\mathcal{O}^{\times 2}]=2
. By Lemma
A.2
we know that
[R:\mathcal{O}^{\times 2}]=2
. Thus
R=U_{2e}\mathcal{O}^{\times 2}
.
∎

The above discussion can be summarized as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}^{\times 2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R=U_{2e}\mathcal{O}^{\times 2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{2e+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{2e}\cap\mathcal{O}^{\times 2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{2e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{2e-1}\hbox to0.0pt{\, ,\hss}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}

\subseteq

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}

\subseteq

where

U_{2e}/\penalty(U_{2e}\cap\mathcal{O}^{\times 2})=\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}.

Let us also mention

Corollary A.8
.

[\mathcal{O}^{\times}:R]=2^{ef}.

Proof.

It is well-known that

[\mathcal{O}^{\times}:\mathcal{O}^{\times 2}]=2^{ef+1}.

(See, for example,
[
Neu99
, (5.8) Proposition, p.142]
.) Since
R/\penalty\mathcal{O}^{\times 2}=\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}
, the corollary follows.
∎
